She turned away, and I bounded back to the study. The advent of the mistress of the house had materially altered my plans for the afternoon. What I proposed to do now was to inform Ukridge of her arrival, advise him to eject the curate with all possible speed, give him my blessing, and then slide quietly and unostentatiously away, without any further formalities of farewell. I am not unduly sensitive, but there had been that in Miss Ukridge’s manner at our recent meeting which told me that I was not her ideal guest.

I entered the study. The curate was gone, and Ukridge, breathing heavily, was fast asleep in an armchair.

The disappearance of the curate puzzled me for a moment. He was rather an insignificant little man, but not so insignificant that I would not have noticed him if he had passed me while I was standing at the front door. And then I saw that the french windows were open.

It seemed to me that there was nothing to keep me. The strong distaste for this house which I had never lost since my first entry into it had been growing, and now the great open spaces called to me with an imperious voice. I turned softly, and found my hostess standing in the doorway.

“Oh, ah!” I said; and once more was afflicted by that curious sensation of having swelled in a very loathsome manner about the hands and feet. I have observed my hands from time to time during my life and have never been struck by anything particularly hideous about them: but whenever I encounter Miss Julia Ukridge they invariably take on the appearance and proportions of uncooked hams.

“Did you tell me, Mr. Corcoran,” said the woman in that quiet, purring voice which must lose her so many friends, not only in Wimbledon but in the larger world outside, “that you saw Mr. Sims and he said that he wished to speak to me?”

“That’s right.”

“Curious,” said Miss Ukridge. “I find that Mr. Sims is confined to his bed with a chill and has not been here today.”

I could sympathize with Mr. Sims’s chills. I felt as if I had caught one myself. I would﻿—possibly﻿—have made some reply, but at this moment an enormous snore proceeded from the armchair behind me, and such was my overwrought condition that I leaped like a young ram.

“Stanley!” cried Miss Ukridge, sighting the chair.

===Chinese===

她转身走了，我一蹦回到书房。女主人的降临，让我下午的计划大大改了道。眼下我打算的是：报告厄克里奇她回来了，劝他火速把副牧师送走，祝他好运，然后自己悄没声、不惊动地溜走，免掉一切告别的繁文缛节。我并非过分敏感的人，可方才同厄克里奇小姐照面时她那副神气告诉我，我不是她理想的客人。

我走进书房。副牧师没了；厄克里奇呼吸粗重，正瘫在扶手椅里酣睡。

副牧师的失踪叫我纳闷了一阵。他是个不起眼的小个子，可再不起眼，他若从我守前门时身边经过，我也不至于看不见。随即我发现法式长窗开着。

我看没有什么可留恋的了。对这座宅子的强烈厌恶，自我头回进门起从未消减，此刻愈发高涨；屋外广阔天地正以专横的声音召唤我。我轻轻转身，却见女主人站在门口。

“哦，啊！”我说；手脚又开始了那股可憎的肿胀感。生平我不止一次观察过自己的手，从不觉得它们有什么特别丑恶；可每回撞见朱莉娅·厄克里奇小姐，它们无不一变而成两只生火腿的模样和尺寸。

“科科伦先生，”这位夫人用她那轻柔的、喉音沙沙的嗓音说——这嗓音必定替她在温布尔登和温布尔登之外的广大世界失去过许多朋友——“你刚才是不是告诉我，你见到了西姆斯先生，他说有话要对我讲？”

“正是。”

“奇怪，”厄克里奇小姐说。“西姆斯先生害了寒，卧床不起，今天根本没来。”

西姆斯先生的寒症我完全体谅。我自己就像也染上一场。我本可能——兴许——回敬几句，可就在这时，身后扶手椅里发出一声惊天动地的鼾，我神经本已绷到极限，闻声像头小公羊般蹦了起来。

“斯坦利！”厄克里奇小姐叫道，一眼看见了椅子。

===Original===

Another snore rumbled through the air, competing with the music of the merry-go-round. Miss Ukridge advanced and shook her nephew’s arm.

“I think,” I said, being in the frame of mind when one does say silly things of that sort, “I think he’s asleep.”

“Asleep!” said Miss Ukridge briefly. Her eye fell on the half empty glass on the table, and she shuddered austerely.

The interpretation which she obviously placed on the matter seemed incredible to me. On the stage and in motion-pictures one frequently sees victims of drink keel over in a state of complete unconsciousness after a single glass, but Ukridge was surely of sterner stuff.

“I can’t understand it,” I said.

“Indeed!” said Miss Ukridge.

“Why, I have only been out of the room half a minute, and when I left him he was talking to a curate.”

“A curate?”

“Absolutely a curate. It’s hardly likely, is it, that when he was talking to a curate he would﻿—”

My speech for the defence was cut short by a sudden, sharp noise which, proceeding from immediately behind me, caused me once more to quiver convulsively.

“Well, sir?” said Miss Ukridge.

She was looking past me; and, turning, I perceived that a stranger had joined us. He was standing in the french windows, and the noise which had startled me had apparently been caused by him rapping on the glass with the knob of a stick.

“Miss Ukridge?” said the newcomer.

He was one of those hard-faced, keen-eyed men. There clung about him, as he advanced into the room, a subtle air of authority. That he was a man of character and resolution was proved by the fact that he met Miss Ukridge’s eye without a tremor.

“I am Miss Ukridge. Might I inquire﻿—?”

The visitor looked harder-faced and more keen-eyed than ever.

“My name is Dawson. From the Yard.”

“What yard?” asked the lady of the house, who, it seemed, did not read detective stories.

“Scotland Yard.”

“Oh!”

“I have come to warn you, Miss Ukridge,” said Mr. Dawson, looking at me as if I were a bloodstain, “to be on your guard. One of the greatest rascals in the profession is hanging about your grounds.”

“Then why don’t you arrest him?” demanded Miss Ukridge. The visitor smiled faintly.

“Because I want to get him good,” he said.

“Get him good? Do you mean reform him?”

===Chinese===

又一声鼾隆隆滚过空中，同旋转木马的音乐一较高下。厄克里奇小姐上前摇她侄子的胳膊。

“我想，”我说——人到了会说这种傻话的心境里——“我想他是睡着了。”

“睡着了！”厄克里奇小姐言简意赅地说。她的目光落在桌上那只半空的酒杯上，凛然一颤。

她显然加在这件事上的解释，在我看来匪夷所思。舞台和电影里，常见受酒之害的人一杯下肚便人事不省；可厄克里奇总不至于这么不经打。

“我弄不懂，”我说。

“哦！”厄克里奇小姐说。

“我离开屋子才半分钟，走的时候他还在同一位副牧师谈话呢。”

“副牧师？”

“千真万确，一位副牧师。他同副牧师谈着话，总不见得就——”

我的辩护词被身后一声突兀的脆响截断，惊得我又是一哆嗦。

“嗯，先生？”厄克里奇小姐说。

她望着我的身后；我一回头，发现屋里多了个生人。他站在法式长窗前，吓我一跳的声响，显然是他用手杖头敲玻璃发出来的。

“厄克里奇小姐？”来客说。

他是那种面孔冷硬、目光锐利的人。他走进屋来时，周身笼着一股无形的威严之气。他能同厄克里奇小姐对视而眼皮不眨一下，足证是个有胆有识之人。

“我就是厄克里奇小姐。敢问——？”

来客的面孔愈发冷硬、目光愈发锐利。

“我姓道森。苏格兰场来的。”

“哪个场？”女主人问——看来她不读侦探小说。

“苏格兰场。”

“哦！”

“我是来提醒您，厄克里奇小姐，”道森先生说，看我的眼神仿佛我是滩血迹，“要多加提防。本行里数一数二的大流氓，正在您园子里转悠。”

“那你们怎么不抓他？”厄克里奇小姐质问。来客淡淡一笑。

“因为我要抓他个扎实的。”他说。

“抓个扎实的？你是说要感化他？”

===Original===

“I do not mean reform him,” said Mr. Dawson, grimly. “I mean that I want to catch him trying on something worth pulling him in for. There’s no sense in taking a man like Stuttering Sam for being a suspected person.”

“Stuttering Sam!” I cried, and Mr. Dawson eyed me keenly once more, this time almost as intently as if I had been the blunt instrument with which the murder was committed.

“Eh?” he said.

“Oh, nothing. Only it’s curious﻿—”

“What’s curious?”

“Oh, no, it couldn’t be. This fellow was a curate. A most respectable man.”

“Have you seen a curate who stuttered?” exclaimed Mr. Dawson.

“Why, yes. He﻿—”

“Hullo!” said Mr. Dawson. “Who’s this?”

“That,” replied Miss Ukridge, eyeing the armchair with loathing, “is my nephew Stanley.”

“Sound sleeper.”

“I prefer not to talk about him.”

“Tell me about this curate,” said Mr. Dawson, brusquely.

“Well, he came in﻿—”

“Came in? In here?”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Well﻿—”

“He must have had some story. What was it?”

I thought it judicious, in the interests of my sleeping friend, to depart somewhat from the precise truth.

“He﻿—er﻿—I think he said something about being interested in Miss Ukridge’s collection of snuffboxes.”

“Have you a collection of snuffboxes, Miss Ukridge?”

“Yes.”

“Where do you keep them?”

“In the drawing-room.”

“Take me there, if you please.”

“But I don’t understand.”

Mr. Dawson clicked his tongue in an annoyed manner. He seemed to be an irritable sleuthhound.

“I should have thought the thing was clear enough by this time. This man worms his way into your house with a plausible story, gets rid of this gentleman here﻿—How did he get rid of you?”

“Oh, I just went, you know. I thought I would like a stroll.”

“Oh? Well, having contrived to be alone with your nephew, Miss Ukridge, he slips knockout drops in his drink﻿—”

“Knockout drops?”

“A drug of some kind,” explained Mr. Dawson, chafing at her slowness of intelligence.

“But the man was a curate!”

Mr. Dawson barked shortly.

“Posing as a curate is the thing Stuttering Sam does best. He works the races in that character. Is this the drawing-room?”

It was. And it did not need the sharp, agonized cry which proceeded from its owner’s lips to tell us that the worst had happened. The floor was covered with splintered wood and broken glass.

“They’re gone!” cried Miss Ukridge.

===Chinese===

“我不是要感化他，”道森先生冷酷地说。“我的意思是，我要当场逮住他干一票值得抓的。单凭身份可疑就带走结巴萨姆这种人，没有意思。”

“结巴萨姆！”我叫出声来，道森先生锐利地又把我审视一遍，这回的专注劲儿，几乎赶上前一回把我当成了作案的钝器。

“嗯？”他说。

“哦，没什么。只是巧了——”

“什么巧了？”

“哦，不，不可能。那人是位副牧师。最体面的一位。”

“你们见过一个结巴的副牧师？”道森先生失声问道。

“可不是。他——”

“咦！”道森先生说。“这位又是谁？”

“那位，”厄克里奇小姐憎恶地望着扶手椅，“是我侄子斯坦利。”

“睡得真沉。”

“我不愿谈他。”

“给我讲讲那位副牧师，”道森先生直截了当地说。

“呃，他进来——”

“进来？进这儿？”

“是。”

“干什么？”

“呃——”

“他必有套说辞。什么说辞？”

为着我那位酣睡的朋友，我以为稍稍偏离事实真相才是明智的。

“他——呃——我记得他说了句什么，对厄克里奇小姐的鼻烟壶收藏很感兴趣。”

“您收藏鼻烟壶吗，厄克里奇小姐？”

“是。”

“存放在哪儿？”

“客厅里。”

“请带我去。”

“可我不明白。”

道森先生烦躁地弹了下舌头。他似乎是条脾气急的猎犬。

“我以为事情到此已经够清楚了。这人用一套圆滑说辞钻进您的宅子，支开这位先生——他怎么支开您的？”

“哦，我自己走的。想散散步。”

“哦？好，等他设法同您侄子单独相处之后，厄克里奇小姐，他便往他的酒里下了蒙汗药——”

“蒙汗药？”

“某种麻醉药，”道森先生解释，为她的迟钝大为光火。

“可那人是位副牧师！”

道森先生短促地吠了一声。

“扮副牧师是结巴萨姆的看家本领。他老用这副行头在赛马场上做活儿。这是客厅吗？”

正是客厅。不必等女主人那声尖锐、痛苦的呼喊来宣布：最坏的事已经发生了。满地都是碎木片和玻璃碴。

“都没了！”厄克里奇小姐哭喊道。

===Original===

It is curious how differently the same phenomenon can strike different people. Miss Ukridge was a frozen statue of grief. Mr. Dawson, on the other hand, seemed pleased. He stroked his short moustache with an air of indulgent complacency, and spoke of neat jobs. He described Stuttering Sam as a Tough Baby, and gave it as his opinion that the absent one might justly be considered one of the lads and not the worst of them.

“What shall I do?” wailed Miss Ukridge. I was sorry for the woman. I did not like her, but she was suffering.

“The first thing to do,” said Mr. Dawson, briskly, “is to find out how much the fellow has got away with. Have you any other valuables in the house?”

“My jewels are in my bedroom.”

“Where?”

“I keep them in a box in the dress-cupboard.”

“Well, it’s hardly likely that he would find them there, but I’d better go and see. You be taking a look round in here and make a complete list of what has been stolen.”

“All my snuffboxes are gone.”

“Well, see if there is anything else missing. Where is your bedroom?”

“On the first floor, facing the front.”

“Right.”

Mr. Dawson, all briskness and efficiency, left us. I was sorry to see him go. I had an idea that it would not be pleasant being left alone with this bereaved woman. Nor was it.

“Why on earth,” said Miss Ukridge, rounding on me as if I had been a relation, “did you not suspect this man when he came in?”

“Why, I﻿—he﻿—”

“A child ought to have been able to tell that he was not a real curate.”

“He seemed﻿—”

“Seemed!” She wandered restlessly about the room, and suddenly a sharp cry proceeded from her. “My jade Buddha!”

“I beg your pardon?”

“That scoundrel has stolen my jade Buddha. Go and tell the detective.”

“Certainly.”

“Go on! What are you waiting for?”

I fumbled at the handle.

“I don’t seem able to get the door open,” I explained, meekly.

“Tchah!” said Miss Ukridge, swooping down. One of the rooted convictions of each member of the human race is that he or she is able without difficulty to open a door which has baffled their fellows. She took the handle and gave it a vigorous tug. The door creaked but remained unresponsive.

“What’s the matter with the thing?” exclaimed Miss Ukridge, petulantly.

“It’s stuck.”

“I know it has stuck. Please do something at once. Good gracious, Mr. Corcoran, surely you are at least able to open a drawing-room door?”

===Chinese===

同一现象落在不同人眼里，引发的反应何其悬殊，煞是有趣。厄克里奇小姐成了一尊悲恸的冰雕。道森先生呢，反倒像是满意了。他捻着修剪整齐的小胡子，一派得意洋洋的宽容，满口“干净利落的手艺”。他称结巴萨姆为“厉害角色”，并断言那位缺席者尽可以算得上好汉里的一名，且不是最末等的一名。

“我可怎么办啊？”厄克里奇小姐哀号。我为这女人难过。我不喜欢她，可她在受罪。

“头一件事，”道森先生干净利落地说，“是查清那家伙卷走了多少。府上还有别的贵重物品吗？”

“我的珠宝在卧室里。”

“哪儿？”

“收在衣柜的一只盒子里。”

“嗯，他在那儿找到的可能性不大，不过我最好去看看。您在这屋里四处查查，把被偷的列张全单。”

“我的鼻烟壶全没了。”

“好，看看还短了什么别的。您卧室在哪儿？”

“二楼，朝前。”

“好。”

道森先生风风火火、干练利落，走了。我舍不得他走。我有预感，同这位遭劫的女人单独关在一起，不会是桩美事。果然不是。

“你倒说说，”厄克里奇小姐猛地转向我，仿佛我是她亲戚，“那人进来的时候，你怎么就一点不起疑？”

“这个，我——他——”

“小孩子都该看得出他不是真副牧师。”

“他看上去——”

“看上去！”她烦躁地在屋里踱来踱去，忽然发出一声尖叫。“我的玉佛！”

“你说什么？”

“那恶棍偷了我的玉佛。去告诉那侦探。”

“这就去。”

“去呀！还等什么？”

我摸索门把手。

“这门我好像拉不开，”我低声下气地解释。

“嘖！”厄克里奇小姐一扑而上。人类每个成员都有一条根深蒂固的信念：别人打不开的门，自己一开就开。她握住把手，使劲一拽。门吱呀作响，岿然不动。

“这玩意儿怎么了？”厄克里奇小姐怒道。

“卡住了。”

“我知道卡住了。请你赶紧想辙。天哪，科科伦先生，开一扇客厅的门，这点本事你总该有吧？”

===Original===

It seemed, put in that tone of voice, a feat sufficiently modest for a man of good physique and fair general education; but I was reluctantly compelled to confess, after a few more experiments, that it was beyond my powers. This appeared to confirm my hostess in the opinion, long held by her, that I was about the most miserable worm that an inscrutable Providence had ever permitted to enter the world.

She did not actually say as much, but she sniffed, and I interpreted her meaning exactly.

“Ring the bell!”

I rang the bell.

“Ring it again!”

I rang it again.

“Shout!”

I shouted.

“Go on shouting!”

I went on shouting. I was in good voice that day. I shouted “Hi!”; I shouted “Here!”; I shouted “Help!” I also shouted in a broad, general way. It was a performance which should have received more than a word of grateful thanks. But all Miss Ukridge said, when I paused for breath, was:

“Don’t whisper!”

I nursed my aching vocal cords in a wounded silence.

“Help!” cried Miss Ukridge.

Considered as a shout, it was not in the same class as mine. It lacked body, vim, and even timbre. But, by that curious irony which governs human affairs, it produced results. Outside the door a thick voice spoke in answer.

“What’s up?”

“Open this door!”

The handle rattled.

“It’s stuck,” said a voice, which I now recognized as that of my old friend, Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge.

“I know it has stuck. Is that you, Stanley? See what is causing it to stick.”

A moment of silence followed. Investigations were apparently in progress without.

“There’s a wedge jammed under it.”

“Well, take it out at once.”

“I’ll have to get a knife or something.”

Another interval for rest and meditation succeeded. Miss Ukridge paced the floor with knit brows; while I sidled into a corner and stood there feeling a little like an inexperienced young animal-trainer who has managed to get himself locked into the lions’ den and is trying to remember what Lesson Three of his correspondence course said he ought to do in such circumstances.

Footsteps sounded outside, and then a wrenching and scratching. The door opened and we beheld on the mat Ukridge, with a carving-knife in his hand, looking headachy and dishevelled, and the butler, his professional poise rudely disturbed and his face stained with coal-dust.

===Chinese===

用她那个腔调说出来，这倒像是对一个体格健壮、受过正规教育的男人的恰如其分的要求；可又试了几回之后，我只好勉强承认，这超出了我的能力。这似乎坐实了女主人由来已久的看法：我是冥冥天意容许放进这世上来的、最可怜的虫豸。

她没有明说，只哼了一声，可那意思我全懂了。

“按铃！”

我按了铃。

“再按！”

我又按。

“喊！”

我喊了。

“接着喊！”

我接着喊。那天我嗓门正足。我喊“喂！”；喊“这儿！”；喊“来人啊！”我还放开嗓子、无所不喊。这场表演，本该换来不止一句感激的谢辞。可等我停下来换气，厄克里奇小姐只说了一句：

“别嘀咕！”

我怀着一颗受伤的心，默默揉着我酸痛的声带。

“来人啊！”厄克里奇小姐喊道。

作为一声喊，它同我的不在一个档次。缺分量、缺劲头，连音色都缺。可是，主宰人间事务的那股奇妙的反讽，竟叫它见了效。门外一个粗嗓门应声答话。

“怎么啦？”

“开这门！”

门把手哗啦啦响。

“卡住了，”一个声音说——我听出来是我老朋友斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇。

“我知道卡住了。是斯坦利吗？查查是什么卡住了它。”

一阵沉默。门外显然正在进行勘查。

“底下楔了个楔子。”

“那就赶紧起出来。”

“我得找把刀什么的。”

又一段休息与思考的间隙。厄克里奇小姐锁眉踱步；我侧身溜到一个角落站着，自觉像一名没经验的年轻驯兽师，把自己锁进了狮子笼，正拼命回忆函授教材第三课讲没讲过眼下这情形该怎么办。

外面响起脚步声，接着是撬动声、刮擦声。门开了。门垫上站着厄克里奇，手里一把切肉刀，一脸宿醉头疼、衣冠不整；旁边是管家，职业风度被粗暴搅乱，脸上抹着煤灰。

===Original===

It was characteristic of Miss Ukridge that it was to the erring domestic rather than the rescuing nephew that she turned first.

“Barter,” she hissed, as far as a woman, even of her intellectual gifts, is capable of hissing the word “Barter,” “why didn’t you come when I rang?”

“I did not hear the bell, madam. I was﻿—”

“You must have heard the bell.”

“No, madam.”

“Why not?”

“Because I was in the coal-cellar, madam.”

“What on earth were you doing in the coal-cellar?”

“I was induced to go there, madam, by a man. He intimidated me with a pistol. He then locked me in.”

“What! What man?”

“A person with a short moustache and penetrating eyes. He﻿—”

A raconteur with a story as interesting as his to tell might reasonably have expected to be allowed to finish it, but butler Barter at this point ceased to grip his audience. With a gasping moan his employer leaped past him, and we heard her running up the stairs.

Ukridge turned to me plaintively.

“What is all this, laddie? Gosh, I’ve got a headache. What has been happening?”

“The curate put knockout drops in your drink, and then﻿—”

I have seldom seen anyone display such poignant emotion as Ukridge did at that moment.

“The curate! It’s a little hard. Upon my Sam, it’s a trifle thick. Corky, old horse, I have travelled all over the world in tramp-steamers and whatnot. I have drunk in waterfront saloons from Montevideo to Cardiff. And the only time anyone has ever succeeded in doctoring the stuff on me it was done in Wimbledon﻿—and by a curate. Tell me, laddie, are all curates like that? Because, if so﻿—”

“He has also pinched your aunt’s collection of snuffboxes.”

“The curate?”

“Yes.”

“Golly!” said Ukridge in a low, reverent voice, and I could see a new respect for the Cloth dawning within him.

“And then this other fellow came along﻿—his accomplice, pretending to be a detective﻿—and locked us in and shut the butler in the coal-cellar. And I rather fancy he has got away with your aunt’s jewels.”

A piercing scream from above rent the air.

“He has,” I said, briefly. “Well, old man, I think I’ll be going.”

“Corky,” said Ukridge, “stand by me!”

I shook my head.

“In any reasonable circumstances, yes. But I will not meet your aunt again just now. In a year or so, perhaps, but not now.”

Hurrying footsteps sounded on the staircase.

===Chinese===

厄克里奇小姐的做派便是如此：她先找的不是救命的侄子，而是失职的仆人。

“巴特，”她嘶声道——一个女人，纵然有她这般才智，嘶出“巴特”二字也就到这个份上了——“我按铃你为什么不来？”

“我没有听见铃，夫人。我——”

“你一定听见了。”

“没有，夫人。”

“为什么？”

“因为我在煤窖里，夫人。”

“你在煤窖里做什么？”

“有个男人把我弄进去的，夫人。他用手枪吓唬我。然后把我锁在了里面。”

“什么！什么男人？”

“一个留着短髭、目光锐利的人。他——”

有这么精彩的故事要讲，讲述者本有理由指望人家让他讲完；可管家巴特说到这儿，听众却被夺走了。他的女主人一声闷哼，从他身边一跃而过，我们听见她奔上了楼梯。

厄克里奇哀怨地转向我。

“这都是怎么了，小老弟？天哪，我头疼。出了什么事？”

“那副牧师往你酒里下了蒙汗药，然后——”

我很少见谁显出厄克里奇此刻这般锥心的激动。

“副牧师！太说不过去了。我的老天，实在欺人太甚。科基，老伙计，我坐着流浪货轮周游过全世界，从蒙得维的亚到加的夫的码头酒馆我都喝过。平生唯一一回有人在我酒里做手脚，竟是在温布尔登——还是个副牧师干的。告诉我，小老弟，副牧师个个都这样吗？要是这样——”

“他还顺走了你姑妈的鼻烟壶收藏。”

“副牧师？”

“是。”

“乖乖！”厄克里奇低声说，语气敬畏，我看得出，他心里正涌起一股对教职的新敬意。

“跟着又来了另一位——他的同伙，装成侦探——把我们锁在这儿，又把管家关进了煤窖。而且我料他多半把你姑妈的珠宝也拐走了。”

楼上一声穿云裂帛的尖叫。

“果然拐走了，”我简短地说。“好了，老伙计，我看我该走了。”

“科基，”厄克里奇说，“拉我一把！”

我摇头。

“讲道理的话，该的。可眼下我不愿再见你姑妈。过个一年半载，也许；现在不行。”

楼梯上响起急促的脚步声。

===Original===

“Goodbye,” I said, pushing past and heading for the open. “I must be off. Thanks for a very pleasant afternoon.”

Money was tight in those days, but it seemed to me next morning that an outlay of twopence on a telephone call to Heath House could not be considered an unjustifiable extravagance. I was conscious of a certain curiosity to learn at long range what had happened after I had removed myself on the previous afternoon.

“Are you there?” said a grave voice in answer to my ring.

“Is that Barter?”

“Yes, sir.”

“This is Mr. Corcoran. I want to speak to Mr. Ukridge.”

“Mr. Ukridge is no longer here, sir. He left perhaps an hour ago.”

“Oh? Do you mean left﻿—er﻿—forever?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Oh! Thanks.”

I rang off and, pondering deeply, returned to my rooms. I was not surprised to be informed by Bowles, my landlord, that Ukridge was in my sitting-room. It was this storm-tossed man’s practice in times of stress to seek refuge with me.

“Hullo, laddie,” said Ukridge in a graveyard voice.

“So here you are.”

“Here I am.”

“She kicked you out?”

Ukridge winced slightly, as at some painful recollection.

“Words passed, old horse, and in the end we decided that we were better apart.”

“I don’t see why she should blame you for what happened.”

“A woman like my aunt, Corky, is capable of blaming anybody for anything. And so I start life again, laddie, a penniless man, with no weapons against the great world but my vision and my brain.”

I endeavoured to attract his attention to the silver lining.

“You’re all right,” I said. “You’re just where you wanted to be. You have the money which your buttercup girl collected.”

A strong spasm shook my poor friend, causing, as always happened with him in moments of mental agony, his collar to shoot off its stud and his glasses to fall from his nose.

“The money that girl collected,” he replied, “is not available. It has passed away. I saw her this morning and she told me.”

“Told you what?”

===Chinese===

“再会，”我说，从他身边挤过去，直奔开阔地。“我得走了。多谢这个非常愉快的下午。”

那些日子我手头拮据，可第二天早晨我想，花两便士往希思宅打个电话，算不得罪过的奢侈。我多少有点好奇，想远远地了解一下，昨天下午我撤走之后那边怎么样了。

“喂？”铃响过后，一个端肃的声音答道。

“是巴特吗？”

“是，先生。”

“我是科科伦。我找厄克里奇先生。”

“厄克里奇先生已经不在了，先生。大约一小时前走的。”

“哦？你是说——呃——永远走了？”

“是，先生。”

“哦！谢谢。”

我挂上电话，满腹心事地回寓所。房东鲍尔斯告诉我厄克里奇在我的起居室里，我毫不意外。这位风雨飘摇的人，每逢逆境，照例到我这儿避难。

“你好啊，小老弟，”厄克里奇用坟场的嗓音说。

“果然在这儿。”

“在这儿。”

“她把你轰出来了？”

厄克里奇微微一缩，像碰到一段痛楚的回忆。

“话赶话地说了几句，老伙计，最后我们一致认为，还是分开的好。”

“我不明白，出了这样的事，她凭什么怪你。”

“我姑妈那样的女人，科基，什么事都能怪到任何人头上。就这样，我又重新开始人生了，小老弟，一个身无分文的人，对抗这个大千世界的武器，只剩下我的远见和我的头脑。”

我努力引导他看事物光明的一面。

“你没事，”我说。“你正好落到你想要的位置上了。你那位毛茛姑娘募的钱还在你手里呢。”

一阵剧烈的抽搐撼动了我可怜的朋友——每逢精神剧痛，他必定领扣绷开、眼镜滑落，这一回照例也没例外。

“那姑娘募的钱，”他答道，“拿不到了。没了。今天早上我见了她，她告诉了我。”

“告诉你什么？”

===Original===

“That a curate came up to her in the garden while she was selling those buttercups and﻿—in spite of a strong stammer﻿—put it to her so eloquently that she was obtaining money under false pretences that she gave him the entire takings for his Church Expenses Fund and went home, resolved to lead a better life. Women are an unstable, emotional sex, laddie. Have as little to do with them as possible. And, for the moment, give me a drink, old horse, and mix it fairly strong. These are the times that try men’s souls.”

===Chinese===

“说是有位副牧师在她卖毛茛的时候凑上去，顶着严重的结巴，把道理讲得那样雄辩——说她这是诈取钱财——结果她把全部进款都捐给了他的教会开支基金，然后回了家，决心重新做人。女人啊，小老弟，是个水性杨花、感情用事的性别。能少沾就少沾。眼下么，老伙计，给我倒杯酒，调得浓些。这样的时世，熬炼人的灵魂啊。”

===Original===

Dedicated with esteem and gratitude to

**Old Bill Townend**

my friend from boyhood’s days who first introduced me to Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge

===Chinese===

谨以敬意与感激，献给

**老比尔·汤恩德**

我自少年时代的朋友，是他最早把我引荐给斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇

## First Aid for Dora / 对多拉的急救
===Original===

Never in the course of a long and intimate acquaintance having been shown any evidence to the contrary, I had always looked on Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge, my boyhood chum, as a man ruggedly indifferent to the appeal of the opposite sex. I had assumed that, like so many financial giants, he had no time for dalliance with women﻿—other and deeper matters, I supposed, keeping that great brain permanently occupied. It was a surprise, therefore, when, passing down Shaftesbury Avenue one Wednesday afternoon in June at the hour when matinée audiences were leaving the theatres, I came upon him assisting a girl in a white dress to mount an omnibus.

As far as this simple ceremony could be rendered impressive, Ukridge made it so. His manner was a blend of courtliness and devotion; and if his mackintosh had been a shade less yellow and his hat a trifle less disreputable, he would have looked just like Sir Walter Ralegh.

The bus moved on, Ukridge waved, and I proceeded to make enquiries. I felt that I was an interested party. There had been a distinctly “object-matrimony” look about the back of his neck, it seemed to me; and the prospect of having to support a Mrs. Ukridge and keep a flock of little Ukridges in socks and shirts perturbed me.

“Who was that?” I asked.

“Oh, hallo, laddie!” said Ukridge, turning. “Where did you spring from? If you had come a moment earlier, I’d have introduced you to Dora.” The bus was lumbering out of sight into Piccadilly Circus, and the white figure on top turned and gave a final wave. “That was Dora Mason,” said Ukridge, having flapped a large hand in reply. “She’s my aunt’s secretary-companion. I used to see a bit of her from time to time when I was living at Wimbledon. Old Tuppy gave me a couple of seats for that show at the Apollo, so I thought it would be a kindly act to ask her along. I’m sorry for that girl. Sorry for her, old horse.”

“What’s the matter with her?”

“Hers is a grey life. She has few pleasures. It’s an act of charity to give her a little treat now and then. Think of it! Nothing to do all day but brush the Pekingese and type out my aunt’s rotten novels.”

“Does your aunt write novels?”

===Chinese===

在我与他漫长而亲密的交往中，从未见到任何相反的证据，所以我一直把斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇——我少年时代的朋友——看作一个对异性的魅力无动于衷的硬汉。我猜想，同许多金融巨头一样，他没有闲情同女人调笑——想必是别的更深的营生，把那颗伟大的脑袋永久占满了。因此，六月里一个星期三的下午，日场散戏、观众正涌出各家剧院的时分，我在沙夫茨伯里大街撞见他扶一位白裙姑娘上公共汽车，实在吃了一惊。
这么一桩简单的仪式，能办出多隆重，厄克里奇就办出了多隆重。他的做派兼有骑士的恭敬与信徒的虔诚；倘若他那件胶布雨衣的颜色能浅上几分、那顶帽子能少寒碜几分，他活脱就是沃尔特·雷利爵士。
汽车开动，厄克里奇挥手；我则着手盘问。我自觉利益攸关。他的后颈上分明写着“意在婚姻”四个字——至少我这样看；想到往后要养活一位厄克里奇太太、供一群小厄克里奇穿袜子穿衬衫，我心里就发毛。
“那是谁？”我问。
“哦，哈罗，小老弟！”厄克里奇转过身来。“你打哪儿冒出来的？早来一步，我就把你介绍给多拉了。”公共汽车正轰隆隆驶进皮卡迪利广场没了影，车顶上那团白影回头最后挥了挥手。“那是多拉·梅森，”厄克里奇朝那边扇了扇大手掌答礼，说道，“我姨妈的秘书兼女伴。我住温布尔登那阵，时不时见她一两回。老塔皮给了我两张阿波罗剧院的票，我想着带她去开开心，也算积德。那姑娘，我可怜她。可怜她哪，老伙计。”
“她怎么了？”
“她的日子灰暗。没多少乐子。时不时给她一点小享受，是慈善行为。你想想！成天没事干，就刷刷哈巴狗、打打我姨妈那些烂小说的字。”
“你姨妈写小说？”

===Original===

“The world’s worst, laddie, the world’s worst. She’s been steeped to the gills in literature ever since I can remember. They’ve just made her president of the Pen and Ink Club. As a matter of fact, it was her novels that did me in when I lived with her. She used to send me to bed with the beastly things and ask me questions about them at breakfast. Absolutely without exaggeration, laddie, at breakfast. It was a dog’s life, and I’m glad it’s over. Flesh and blood couldn’t stand the strain. Well, knowing my aunt, I don’t mind telling you that my heart bleeds for poor little Dora. I know what a foul time she has, and I feel a better, finer man for having given her this passing gleam of sunshine. I wish I could have done more for her.”

“Well, you might have stood her tea after the theatre.”

“Not within the sphere of practical politics, laddie. Unless you can sneak out without paying, which is dashed difficult to do with these cashiers watching the door like weasels, tea even at an A.B.C. shop punches the pocketbook pretty hard, and at the moment I’m down to the scrapings. But I’ll tell you what, I don’t mind joining you in a cup, if you were thinking of it.”

“I wasn’t.”

“Come, come! A little more of the good old spirit of hospitality, old horse.”

“Why do you wear that beastly mackintosh in midsummer?”

“Don’t evade the point, laddie. I can see at a glance that you need tea. You’re looking pale and fagged.”

“Doctors say that tea is bad for the nerves.”

“Yes, possibly there’s something in that. Then I’ll tell you what,” said Ukridge, never too proud to yield a point, “we’ll make it a whisky-and-soda instead. Come along over to the Criterion.”

===Chinese===

“天底下最烂的，小老弟，天底下最烂的。打我记事起，她就泡在文学里，泡到嗓子眼。他们刚推她当了笔墨俱乐部的会长。说真的，当年我住她家，就是叫她那些小说给毁的。她总打发我带着那些糟烂玩意儿上床，第二天早饭桌上还考我。一点不掺假，小老弟，早饭桌上考。那是狗过的日子，如今总算熬出头了，谢天谢地。血肉之躯架不住那份熬煎。所以，深知我家姨妈，我不妨对你交底：我这颗心为可怜的小多拉流血。我知道她过的什么鬼日子，而给了她这一线过眼的阳光，我觉得自己成了一个更好、更高尚的人。只恨没能为她多做点。”
“那你本可以请她看完戏吃顿茶点嘛。”
“不在切实可行的范围之内，小老弟。除非能不付钱溜走——可这些收银员守着门口像黄鼠狼，想溜难于登天——就连ABC茶馆的一顿茶，对钱袋也是不小的打击，何况我眼下囊中只剩点底子了。不过这么着吧：你要是有心喝一杯，我不介意奉陪。”
“我没这心思。”
“来来来！拿出点老派的待客精神，老伙计。”
“大仲夏的，你穿这件破雨衣干什么？”
“别岔开正题，小老弟。我一眼就看出你需要茶。你脸色发白，一脸倦容。”
“医生说茶伤神经。”
“是，这话兴许有点道理。那这么办，”厄克里奇说——他从不屑于死守一个阵地，“咱们改喝威士忌苏打。走，上克里特里昂去。”

===Original===

It was a few days after this that the Derby was run, and a horse of the name of Gunga Din finished third. This did not interest the great bulk of the intelligentsia to any marked extent, the animal having started at a hundred to three, but it meant much to me, for I had drawn his name in the sweepstake at my club. After a monotonous series of blanks stretching back to the first year of my membership, this seemed to me the outstanding event of the century, and I celebrated my triumph by an informal dinner to a few friends. It was some small consolation to me later to remember that I had wanted to include Ukridge in the party, but failed to get hold of him. Dark hours were to follow, but at least Ukridge did not go through them bursting with my meat.

There is no form of spiritual exaltation so poignant as that which comes from winning even a third prize in a sweepstake. So tremendous was the moral uplift that, when eleven o’clock arrived, it seemed silly to sit talking in a club and still sillier to go to bed. I suggested spaciously that we should all go off and dress and resume the revels at my expense half an hour later at Mario’s, where, it being an extension night, there would be music and dancing till three. We scattered in cabs to our various homes.

How seldom in this life do we receive any premonition of impending disaster. I hummed a gay air as I entered the house in Ebury Street where I lodged, and not even the usually quelling sight of Bowles, my landlord, in the hall as I came in could quench my bonhomie. Generally a meeting with Bowles had the effect on me which the interior of a cathedral has on the devout, but tonight I was superior to this weakness.

“Ah, Bowles,” I cried, chummily, only just stopping myself from adding “Honest fellow!” “Hallo, Bowles! I say, Bowles, I drew Gunga Din in the club sweep.”

“Indeed, sir?”

“Yes. He came in third, you know.”

“So I see by the evening paper, sir. I congratulate you.”

“Thank you, Bowles, thank you.”

“Mr. Ukridge called earlier in the evening, sir,” said Bowles.

“Did he? Sorry I was out. I was trying to get hold of him. Did he want anything in particular?”

“Your dress-clothes, sir.”

“My dress-clothes, eh?” I laughed genially. “Extraordinary fellow! You never know﻿—” A ghastly thought smote me like a blow. A cold wind seemed to blow through the hall. “He didn’t *get* them, did he?” I quavered.

===Chinese===

过了几天，德比马赛开跑，一匹名叫冈加·丁的马跑了个第三。这对广大知识界没多大触动——此马开赔率一百比三——可于我意义重大：我在俱乐部的赌马抽签里，抽中的正是它的名字。在一路空白、直追溯到入会头一年的沉闷纪录之后，这在我眼里简直是本世纪头等大事，我便请了几位朋友小宴，庆贺这场凯旋。日后想来也算一点安慰：我原想拉厄克里奇入席，没能找到他。黑暗的时辰固然在后头，至少厄克里奇不是撑饱了我的肉扛过去的。
世上没有哪种精神的飞扬，赛过赌马抽签里哪怕中个三等奖。那道德升华之猛烈，到十一点钟，坐在俱乐部里聊天显得愚蠢，上床更显得愚蠢。我大手一挥，提议各自回家更衣，半小时后在马里奥餐馆重开欢宴，费用全归我——那晚是特许延时之夜，有音乐有跳舞，直闹到三点。众人分乘马车四散回家。
人这一生，多么难得收到灾祸将临的预感啊。我哼着一支欢快小调，踏进伊伯里街我寄宿的宅子；连鲍尔斯——我的房东——照例那一眼足以叫人肃然的目光在门厅里迎着我，也没能压下我的好兴致。平日撞见鲍尔斯，对我的效果如同信徒走进大教堂；今晚我超越了这份软弱。
“啊，鲍尔斯，”我亲亲热热地喊，差点加上一句“老实人！”。“哈罗，鲍尔斯！我说鲍尔斯，我在俱乐部赌马抽签里抽中了冈加·丁。”
“是吗，先生？”
“是。它跑了第三，你知道。”
“晚报上见了，先生。恭喜您。”
“谢谢，鲍尔斯，谢谢。”
“厄克里奇先生傍晚来过，先生。”鲍尔斯说。
“是吗？可惜我不在。我正找他呢。他有什么特别的事？”
“您的晚礼服，先生。”
“我的晚礼服，嗯？”我呵呵地笑。“这家伙真绝！谁猜得着——”一个可怕的念头像一记闷棍打下来。一阵冷风仿佛穿堂而过。“他不会已经拿走了吧？”我颤声问。

===Original===

“Why, yes, sir.”

“Got my dress-clothes?” I muttered thickly, clutching for support at the hatstand.

“He said it would be all right, sir,” said Bowles, with that sickening tolerance which he always exhibited for all that Ukridge said or did. One of the leading mysteries of my life was my landlord’s amazing attitude towards this hellhound. He fawned on the man. A splendid fellow like myself had to go about in a state of hushed reverence towards Bowles, while a human blot like Ukridge could bellow at him over the banisters without the slightest rebuke. It was one of those things which make one laugh cynically when people talk about the equality of man.

“He got my dress-clothes?” I mumbled.

“Mr. Ukridge said that he knew you would be glad to let him have them, as you would not be requiring them tonight.”

“But I do require them, damn it!” I shouted, lost to all proper feeling. Never before had I let fall an oath in Bowles’s presence. “I’m giving half a dozen men supper at Mario’s in a quarter of an hour.”

Bowles clicked his tongue sympathetically.

“What am I going to do?”

“Perhaps if you would allow me to lend you mine, sir?”

“Yours?”

“I have a very nice suit. It was given to me by his lordship the late Earl of Oxted, in whose employment I was for many years. I fancy it would do very well on you, sir. His lordship was about your height, though perhaps a little slenderer. Shall I fetch it, sir? I have it in a trunk downstairs.”

The obligations of hospitality are sacred. In fifteen minutes’ time six jovial men would be assembling at Mario’s, and what would they do, lacking a host? I nodded feebly.

“It’s very kind of you,” I managed to say.

“Not at all, sir. It is a pleasure.”

If he was speaking the truth, I was glad of it. It is nice to think that the affair brought pleasure to someone.

===Chinese===

“呃——拿了，先生。”
“拿了我的晚礼服？”我含混地念叨，一把抓住帽架支撑住。
“他说没问题的，先生。”鲍尔斯说，带着他对厄克里奇的言行一贯显出的那副叫人作呕的宽容。我平生一大悬案，便是房东对这条恶犬的惊人态度。他巴结这个人。我这样一位体面人物，见了鲍尔斯只敢屏声敛气、毕恭毕敬；而厄克里奇那样一个人渣，却可以隔着楼梯栏杆冲他吼，半句训斥也落不下。人类平等之说，听了只叫人冷笑——这便是一例。
“他拿了我的晚礼服？”我又喃喃了一遍。
“厄克里奇先生说，他知道您一定乐意借他，因为您今晚用不着。”
“可我偏偏用得着，见鬼！”我忘了体统，嚷了起来。我在鲍尔斯面前还从来没有爆过粗口。“一刻钟后，六位快活的先生要在马里奥赴我的宵夜！”
鲍尔斯同情地咂了咂舌。
“那我怎么办？”
“先生若肯赏光，可否将就穿我的？”
“你的？”
“我有很好的一套。是已故奥克斯泰德伯爵大人赏的，我在他府上当了多年差。我想您穿正合身，先生。大人同您个头相仿，只是略瘦些。要我去取吗，先生？在楼下衣箱里收着。”
待客的义务是神圣的。十五分钟后，六位兴高采烈的先生就要在马里奥聚齐；缺了主人，他们怎么办？我有气无力地点了点头。
“太谢谢你了。”我好容易说出这一句。
“不客气，先生。这是我的荣幸。”
他若说的是真话，我倒替他高兴。这桩事总算也叫某人得了乐趣，想想总是好的。

===Original===

That the late Earl of Oxted had indeed been a somewhat slenderer man than myself became manifest to me from the first pulling on of the trousers. Hitherto I had always admired the slim, small-boned type of aristocrat, but it was not long before I was wishing that Bowles had been in the employment of someone who had gone in a little more heartily for starchy foods. And I regretted, moreover, that the fashion of wearing a velvet collar on an evening coat, if it had to come in at all, had not lasted a few years longer. Dim as the light in my bedroom was, it was strong enough to make me wince as I looked in the mirror.

And I was aware of a curious odour.

“Isn’t this room a trifle stuffy, Bowles?”

“No, sir. I think not.”

“Don’t you notice an odd smell?”

“No, sir. But I have a somewhat heavy cold. If you are ready, sir, I will call a cab.”

Mothballs! That was the scent I had detected. It swept upon me like a wave in the cab. It accompanied me like a fog all the way to Mario’s, and burst out in its full fragrance when I entered the place and removed my overcoat. The cloakroom waiter sniffed in a startled way as he gave me my check, one or two people standing near hastened to remove themselves from my immediate neighbourhood, and my friends, when I joined them, expressed themselves with friend-like candour. With a solid unanimity they told me frankly that it was only the fact that I was paying for the supper that enabled them to tolerate my presence.

The leper-like feeling induced by this uncharitable attitude caused me after the conclusion of the meal to withdraw to the balcony to smoke in solitude. My guests were dancing merrily, but such pleasures were not for me. Besides, my velvet collar had already excited ribald comment, and I am a sensitive man. Crouched in a lonely corner of the balcony, surrounded by the outcasts who were not allowed on the lower floor because they were not dressed, I chewed a cigar and watched the revels with a jaundiced eye. The space reserved for dancing was crowded and couples either revolved warily or ruthlessly bumped a passage for themselves, using their partners as battering-rams. Prominent among the ruthless bumpers was a big man who was giving a realistic imitation of a steam-plough. He danced strongly and energetically, and when he struck the line, something had to give.

===Chinese===

已故奥克斯泰德伯爵果然比我瘦，这一点，裤子头一回套上腿就给了我明证。从前我一向欣赏清瘦小骨的贵族类型，可没过多久，我便开始巴望鲍尔斯当年伺候的主儿是一位在淀粉类食物上更肯下本钱的。而且我还惋惜：晚礼服领子镶天鹅绒的时兴，倘若注定要兴，何不多兴上几年。我卧室里灯光再暗，也暗得足以叫我对镜一望便龇牙咧嘴。
我还闻到一股古怪的气味。
“这屋子是不是有点闷，鲍尔斯？”
“不闷，先生。我想不闷。”
“你没闻到怪味么？”
“没有，先生。我伤风，鼻子堵得厉害。您若好了，先生，我去叫车。”
樟脑丸！我嗅到的正是这个。在马车上，它像一阵浪头将我掀翻。去马里奥的一路，它像一团雾跟着我；一进门脱下大衣，它便香气四溢、全副登场。衣帽间的侍者接过号牌时惊得吸了吸鼻子；站得近的几位忙不迭撤离我的周边；朋友们等我一落座，便以朋友特有的坦率发表了意见。他们异口同声、直截了当地告诉我：若不是看在我付账的份上，他们谁也忍不了挨我这么近。
这份不厚道的做派，害得我滋生了麻风病人般的心情；饭毕便退到阳台上，独自抽烟。宾客们跳得正欢，那样的乐趣却与我无缘。何况我的天鹅绒领子早已招来过轻薄的品评，而我是个脸皮薄的人。蜷在阳台冷清的一角，身边围着那些因衣冠不整不得下楼的被逐者们，我嚼着一支雪茄，用一双黄疽的眼睛望着楼下的狂欢。舞池挤得满满当当，双双对对或小心翼翼地旋转，或拿舞伴当攻城槌，横冲直撞杀出一条路来。横冲直撞者里头打眼的一位，是个大块头，正逼真地模仿着一台蒸汽犁。他舞得雄壮有力，一撞上人墙，总有什么得让一让。

===Original===

From the very first something about this man had seemed familiar; but owing to his peculiar crouching manner of dancing, which he seemed to have modelled on the ring-style of Mr. James J. Jeffries, it was not immediately that I was able to see his face. But presently, as the music stopped and he straightened himself to clap his hands for an encore, his foul features were revealed to me.

It was Ukridge. Ukridge, confound him, with my dress-clothes fitting him so perfectly and with such unwrinkled smoothness that he might have stepped straight out of one of Ouida’s novels. Until that moment I had never fully realized the meaning of the expression “faultless evening dress.” With a passionate cry I leaped from my seat, and, accompanied by a rich smell of camphor, bounded for the stairs. Like Hamlet on a less impressive occasion, I wanted to slay this man when he was full of bread, with all his crimes, broad-blown, as flush as May, at drinking, swearing, or about some act that had no relish of salvation in it.

“But, laddie,” said Ukridge, backed into a corner of the lobby apart from the throng, “be reasonable.”

I cleansed my bosom of a good deal of that perilous stuff that weighs upon the heart.

“How could I guess that you would want the things? Look at it from my position, old horse. I knew you, laddie, a good true friend who would be delighted to lend a pal his dress-clothes any time when he didn’t need them himself, and as you weren’t there when I called, I couldn’t ask you, so I naturally simply borrowed them. It was all just one of those little misunderstandings which can’t be helped. And, as it luckily turns out, you had a spare suit, so everything was all right, after all.”

“You don’t think this poisonous fancy dress is mine, do you?”

“Isn’t it?” said Ukridge, astonished.

“It belongs to Bowles. He lent it to me.”

“And most extraordinarily well you look in it, laddie,” said Ukridge. “Upon my Sam, you look like a duke or something.”

“And smell like a secondhand clothes-store.”

===Chinese===

打一开头，此人身上便有点眼熟的东西；无奈他跳舞的蹲姿奇特——瞧那架势，是照着詹姆斯·J·杰弗里斯的拳台路数学的——一时竟看不到他的脸。可不多时，乐声一停，他直起身来鼓掌再来一个，那张可憎的面目便对我全盘亮相了。
是厄克里奇。是厄克里奇，这该死的，穿着我的晚礼服，合身之妥帖、平展之无痕，仿佛刚从奥维达哪部小说里走出来。直到那一刻，我才算彻悟“无懈可击的晚装”一语的真义。我一声怒吼，从座位上跃起，裹着一团浓郁的樟脑味，直扑楼梯。如同哈姆雷特在一场排场稍逊的场合里那样，我要趁这人酒足饭饱、罪愆怒放、酗酒咒骂、干着毫无得救之望的勾当之际，结果了他。
“可是，小老弟，”厄克里奇退到门厅一角、避开人群，说，“讲点道理。”
我把胸中那许多压得人心口发沉的危险物事，倾倒了个痛快。
“我怎么料得到你要用这身行头？设身处地替我想想，老伙计。我知道你，小老弟，一位货真价实的好朋友——自己不用晚礼服的时候，朋友要借，你求之不得。我上门你不在，问不着，我自然就干脆借走了。这不过是那种免不了的小小误会中的一个。而事有凑巧，你正好另有一套，所以到头来什么都没耽误。”
“你不会当真以为，这身毒气熏天的化装服是我自己的吧？”
“不是么？”厄克里奇吃了一惊。
“是鲍尔斯的。他借给我的。”
“你穿上精神极了，小老弟，”厄克里奇说，“以萨姆起誓，你像个公爵什么的。”
“气味像家旧衣铺。”

===Original===

“Nonsense, my dear old son, nonsense. A mere faint suggestion of some rather pleasant antiseptic. Nothing more. I like it. It’s invigorating. Honestly, old man, it’s really remarkable what an air that suit gives you. Distinguished. That’s the word I was searching for. You look distinguished. All the girls are saying so. When you came in just now to speak to me, I heard one of them whisper ‘Who is it?’ That shows you.”

“More likely ‘What is it?’ ”

“Ha, ha!” bellowed Ukridge, seeking to cajole me with sycophantic mirth. “Dashed good! Deuced good! Not ‘Who is it?’ but ‘What is it?’ It beats me how you think of these things. Golly, if I had a brain like yours﻿—But now, old son, if you don’t mind, I really must be getting back to poor little Dora. She’ll be wondering what has become of me.”

The significance of these words had the effect of making me forget my just wrath for a moment.

“Are you here with that girl you took to the theatre the other afternoon?”

“Yes. I happened to win a trifle on the Derby, so I thought it would be the decent thing to ask her out for an evening’s pleasure. Hers is a grey life.”

“It must be, seeing you so much.”

“A little personal, old horse,” said Ukridge reprovingly. “A trifle bitter. But I know you don’t mean it. Yours is a heart of gold really. If I’ve said that once, I’ve said it a hundred times. Always saying it. Rugged exterior but heart of gold. My very words. Well, goodbye for the present, laddie. I’ll look in tomorrow and return these things. I’m sorry there was any misunderstanding about them, but it makes up for everything, doesn’t it, to feel that you’ve helped brighten life for a poor little downtrodden thing who has few pleasures.”

“Just one last word,” I said. “One final remark.”

“Yes?”

“I’m sitting in that corner of the balcony over there,” I said. “I mention the fact so that you can look out for yourself. If you come dancing underneath there, I shall drop a plate on you. And if it kills you, so much the better. I’m a poor downtrodden little thing, and I have few pleasures.”

===Chinese===

“胡说，我的老兄，胡说。不过是隐隐约约一丝挺好闻的防腐剂，仅此而已。我喜欢。提神。老实说，老哥，这套衣服给你添的那份气派，真叫人惊叹。气宇轩昂。我要找的就是这个词。你看上去气宇轩昂。姑娘们个个这么说。方才你进来同我说话，我听见其中一个低声问‘那是谁？’可见一斑。”
“多半是‘那是什么？’”
“哈哈！”厄克里奇吼道，想用谄媚的欢谑哄住我。“妙极！妙极！不是‘那是谁’，是‘那是什么’！亏你想得出。老天，我要有你那个脑子——好了老兄，恕我失陪，我真得回到可怜的小多拉身边去了。她该纳闷我上哪儿去了。”
这几句话的意思，使我一时间忘了满腔义愤。
“同你来的，就是那天下午你带看戏的那姑娘？”
“是。德比上我碰巧赢了几个钱，就想着体面体面，请她出来乐一晚。她的日子灰暗啊。”
“老见你，可不就灰暗了。”
“这话有点扎人，老伙计，”厄克里奇责备道，“有点酸。不过我知道你不是那个意思。你其实是颗金子做的心。这话我说过一回，就是说过一百回。逢人就说。糙皮囊，金子心。我的原话。好，回见，小老弟。明天我过来把这身行头还你。这回的误会我很抱歉；不过一想到你帮一个没多少乐子、受尽作践的小可怜儿点亮了生活，别的不都抵了么？”
“最后说一句，”我说，“最后一句话。”
“嗯？”
“我坐在那边阳台角上，”我说，“特此声明，你自己当心。你要是晃到底下来跳舞，我就拿盘子砸你。砸死了最好。我也是个可怜的、受作践的小东西，我也没多少乐子。”

===Original===

Owing to a mawkish respect for the conventions, for which I reproach myself, I did not actually perform this service to humanity. With the exception of throwing a roll at him﻿—which missed him but most fortunately hit the member of my supper-party who had sniffed with the most noticeable offensiveness at my camphorated costume﻿—I took no punitive measures against Ukridge that night. But his demeanour, when he called at my rooms next day, could not have been more crushed if I had dropped a pound of lead on him. He strode into my sitting room with the sombre tread of the man who in a conflict with Fate has received the loser’s end. I had been passing in my mind a number of good snappy things to say to him, but his appearance touched me to such an extent that I held them in. To abuse this man would have been like dancing on a tomb.

“For Heaven’s sake what’s the matter?” I asked. “You look like a toad under the harrow.”

He sat down creakingly, and lit one of my cigars.

“Poor little Dora!”

“What about her?”

“She’s got the push!”

“The push? From your aunt’s, do you mean?”

“Yes.”

“What for?”

Ukridge sighed heavily.

“Most unfortunate business, old horse, and largely my fault. I thought the whole thing was perfectly safe. You see, my aunt goes to bed at half-past ten every night, so it seemed to me that if Dora slipped out at eleven and left a window open behind her she could sneak back all right when we got home from Mario’s. But what happened? Some dashed officious ass,” said Ukridge, with honest wrath, “went and locked the damned window. I don’t know who it was. I suspect the butler. He has a nasty habit of going round the place late at night and shutting things. Upon my Sam, it’s a little hard! If only people would leave things alone and not go snooping about﻿—”

“What happened?”

===Chinese===

由于我对世俗礼法存着一种婆婆妈妈的敬意——这一点我至今自责——我终究没有真为人类效这份劳。除了朝他扔一个小面包——没砸着他，倒极其幸运地砸中了我宵夜席上那位对我的樟脑装束嗅得最响、最不客气的朋友——当夜我没再对厄克里奇采取什么惩罚措施。可第二天他到我房里来时那副垂头丧气，倒好像我真朝他砸了一磅铅似的。他迈着阴沉的步子走进我的起居室，俨然一个同命运交手、领了输家那一头的人。我本来在心里备好了不少干脆利落的狠话，可他那副样子叫我心软，都咽了回去。骂这个人，无异于在坟头上跳舞。
“看老天分上，出什么事了？”我问。“你像耙子底下的癞蛤蟆。”
他嘎吱一声坐下，点了我一支雪茄。
“可怜的小多拉！”
“她怎么了？”
“她被辞了！”
“辞了？你是说，从你姨妈家？”
“是。”
“为什么？”
厄克里奇重重叹了口气。
“倒了大霉的一桩事，老伙计，多半得怪我。我原以为这事万无一失。你瞧，我姨妈每晚十点半上床，所以我想：多拉十一点溜出去，身后留扇窗，等马里奥散了回来，照样能溜回去，神不知鬼不觉。可结果呢？哪个多管闲事的蠢货，”厄克里奇义愤填膺地说，“跑去把那扇窗锁死了。不知道是谁。我疑心是管家。他有这么个坏毛病：半夜里满宅子转，见什么关什么。以萨姆起誓，这也太难了！人们要是肯少管闲事、别到处窥探——”
“后来呢？”

===Original===

“Why, it was the scullery window which we’d left open, and when we got back at four o’clock this morning the infernal thing was shut as tight as an egg. Things looked pretty rocky, but Dora remembered that her bedroom window was always open, so we bucked up again for a bit. Her room’s on the second floor, but I knew where there was a ladder, so I went and got it, and she was just hopping up as merry as dammit when somebody flashed a great beastly lantern on us, and there was a policeman, wanting to know what the game was. The whole trouble with the police force of London, laddie, the thing that makes them a hissing and a byword, is that they’re snoopers to a man. Zeal, I suppose they call it. Why they can’t attend to their own affairs is more than I can understand. Dozens of murders going on all the time, probably, all over Wimbledon, and all this bloke would do was stand and wiggle his infernal lantern and ask what the game was. Wouldn’t be satisfied with a plain statement that it was all right. Insisted on rousing the house to have us identified.”

Ukridge paused, a reminiscent look of pain on his expressive face.

“And then?” I said.

“We were,” said Ukridge, briefly.

“What?”

“Identified. By my aunt. In a dressing-gown and a revolver. And the long and the short of it is, old man, that poor little Dora has got the sack.”

I could not find it in my heart to blame his aunt for what he evidently considered a high-handed and tyrannical outrage. If I were a maiden lady of regular views, I should relieve myself of the services of any secretary-companion who returned to roost only a few short hours in advance of the milk. But, as Ukridge plainly desired sympathy rather than an austere pronouncement on the relations of employer and employed, I threw him a couple of tuts, which seemed to soothe him a little. He turned to the practical side of the matter.

“What’s to be done?”

“I don’t see what you can do.”

“But I must do something. I’ve lost the poor little thing her job, and I must try to get it back. It’s a rotten sort of job, but it’s her bread and butter. Do you think George Tupper would biff round and have a chat with my aunt, if I asked him?”

“I suppose he would. He’s the best-hearted man in the world. But I doubt if he’ll be able to do much.”

===Chinese===

“嗐，我们留的是厨房那扇窗；凌晨四点我们回去，那扇可恶的窗关得严丝合缝，像只鸡蛋。局面眼看要砸，可多拉想起她自己卧室的窗从来开着，我们精神又为之一振。她的房间在三楼，可我知道哪儿有架梯子，就去搬了来；她正高高兴兴往上蹦，冷不丁有人打出一盏贼亮贼亮的手电，照定我们，旁边站着个警察，问这是演的哪一出。伦敦警察队伍的病根，小老弟，害得他们千夫所指的东西，就是他们一个个都爱窥探。他们管这叫热忱，大概。他们怎么不肯各人自扫门前雪，我实在想不通。全温布尔登这时候多半正发生着几十桩凶案，这家伙却只会站着，摇他那只该死的手电，问这是演的哪一出。一句‘没事儿’的明白话打发不掉他。非要把全家吵起来认人。”
厄克里奇顿住，那张富于表情的脸上掠过一阵追忆的痛楚。
“然后呢？”我说。
“认了，”厄克里奇言简意赅。
“什么？”
“我姨妈认的。披着晨衣，端着左轮枪。长话短说，老哥：可怜的小多拉，饭碗砸了。”
他显然认为这是横暴专制的奇冤大辱，可我实在无法从心里怪他姨妈。我若是位持守正统观念的老小姐，家里那位秘书兼女伴回巢归窝只比送奶的早那么几个钟头，我也会请她卷铺盖。可厄克里奇分明要的是同情，不是关于劳资关系的一纸严正判词，我便丢给他两声“啧啧”，他似乎得了些许安抚。随即他转向了问题的实际一面。
“怎么办呢？”
“我看你无能为力。”
“可我总得干点什么。是我害那可怜的小东西丢了差事，我得设法给她讨回来。差事是烂，可那是她的饭碗。你看，我要是开口，乔治·塔珀肯不肯跑一趟，找我姨妈聊聊？”
“我想他肯。他是天底下心肠最好的人。不过我怀疑他办不成什么。”

===Original===

“Nonsense, laddie,” said Ukridge, his unconquerable optimism rising bravely from the depths. “I have the utmost confidence in old Tuppy. A man in a million. And he’s such a dashed respectable sort of bloke that he might have her jumping through hoops and shamming dead before she knew what was happening to her. You never know. Yes, I’ll try old Tuppy. I’ll go and see him now.”

“I should.”

“Just lend me a trifle for a cab, old son, and I shall be able to get to the Foreign Office before one o’clock. I mean to say, even if nothing comes of it, I shall be able to get a lunch out of him. And I need refreshment, laddie, need it sorely. The whole business has shaken me very much.”

It was three days after this that, stirred by a pleasant scent of bacon and coffee, I hurried my dressing and, proceeding to my sitting room, found that Ukridge had dropped in to take breakfast with me, as was often his companionable practice. He seemed thoroughly cheerful again, and was plying knife and fork briskly like the good trencherman he was.

“Morning, old horse,” he said agreeably.

“Good morning.”

“Devilish good bacon, this. As good as I’ve ever bitten. Bowles is cooking you some more.”

“That’s nice. I’ll have a cup of coffee, if you don’t mind me making myself at home while I’m waiting.” I started to open the letters by my plate, and became aware that my guest was eyeing me with a stare of intense penetration through his pince-nez, which were all crooked as usual. “What’s the matter?”

“Matter?”

“Why,” I said, “are you looking at me like a fish with lung-trouble?”

“Was I?” He took a sip of coffee with an overdone carelessness. “Matter of fact, old son, I was rather interested. I see you’ve had a letter from my aunt.”

“What?”

I had picked up the last envelope. It was addressed in a strong female hand, strange to me. I now tore it open. It was even as Ukridge had said. Dated the previous day and headed “Heath House, Wimbledon Common,” the letter ran as follows:﻿—

“**Dear Sir**﻿—I shall be happy to see you if you will call at this address the day after tomorrow (Friday) at four-thirty.﻿—Yours faithfully, Julia Ukridge.”

===Chinese===

“胡说，小老弟，”厄克里奇说，他那不服输的乐观从谷底昂然抬头。“我对老塔皮信心十足。万里挑一的人。而且他这个人规矩得吓人，说不定三下两下，就叫我姨妈服服帖帖钻圈、装死了——她自己还不知道是怎么回事。没准的事。好，我去找老塔皮。这就去。”
“该去。”
“借我几个子儿打车，老兄，我就能赶在一点前到外交部。我是说，就算一无所成，好歹能蹭他一顿午饭。我急需滋补，小老弟，急需。这一趟把我折腾得不轻。”
三天之后，一阵诱人的煎咸肉和咖啡的香味催我加紧穿衣；我走进起居室，发现厄克里奇又来同我共进早餐了——他好交游，这是常事。他像是彻底恢复了欢快，正以好食客的本色，刀叉使得飞快。
“早啊，老伙计。”他和气地说。
“早。”
“这咸肉好得很。生平吃过数得着的。鲍尔斯再给你煎一份。”
“那敢情好。等着的时候我就不客气，先来杯咖啡。”我开始拆手边盘子旁的信件，却发觉客人正隔着夹鼻眼镜——照例歪在鼻梁上——用极犀利的目光盯着我。“怎么了？”
“什么怎么了？”
“我说，”我问道，“你干嘛像条肺痨鱼似的看着我？”
“是么？”他过分漫不经心地呷了口咖啡。“实不相瞒，老兄，我正有点上心。我见你收到我姨妈的信了。”
“什么？”
我拿起最末一个信封。信封上是遒劲的女性字迹写的地址，我不认得。拆开一看，果然如厄克里奇所料。信是前一天写的，抬头“温布尔登公地，石楠府”，正文如下：
“**先生**：如蒙于后天（星期五）四时三十分莅临舍下，不胜欣幸。——您忠实的，朱莉娅·厄克里奇”

===Original===

I could make nothing of this. My morning mail, whether pleasant or the reverse, whether bringing a bill from a tradesman or a cheque from an editor, had had till now the uniform quality of being plain, straightforward, and easy to understand; but this communication baffled me. How Ukridge’s aunt had become aware of my existence, and why a call from me should ameliorate her lot, were problems beyond my unravelling, and I brooded over it as an Egyptologist might over some newly-discovered hieroglyphic.

“What does she say?” enquired Ukridge.

“She wants me to call at half-past four tomorrow afternoon.”

“Splendid!” cried Ukridge. “I knew she would bite.”

“What on earth are you talking about?”

Ukridge reached across the table and patted me affectionately on the shoulder. The movement involved the upsetting of a full cup of coffee, but I suppose he meant well. He sank back again in his chair and adjusted his pince-nez in order to get a better view of me. I seemed to fill him with honest joy, and he suddenly burst into a spirited eulogy, rather like some minstrel of old delivering an extempore boost of his chieftain and employer.

“Laddie,” said Ukridge, “if there’s one thing about you that I’ve always admired it’s your readiness to help a pal. One of the most admirable qualities a bloke can possess, and nobody has it to a greater extent than you. You’re practically unique in that way. I’ve had men come up to me and ask me about you. ‘What sort of a chap is he?’ they say. ‘One of the very best,’ I reply. ‘A fellow you can rely on. A man who would die rather than let you down. A bloke who would go through fire and water to do a pal a good turn. A bird with a heart of gold and a nature as true as steel.’ ”

“Yes, I’m a splendid fellow,” I agreed, slightly perplexed by this panegyric. “Get on.”

“I am getting on, old horse,” said Ukridge with faint reproach. “What I’m trying to say is that I knew you would-be delighted to tackle this little job for me. It wasn’t necessary to ask you. I *knew*.”

A grim foreboding of an awful doom crept over me, as it had done so often before in my association with Ukridge.

“Will you kindly tell me what damned thing you’ve let me in for now?”

Ukridge deprecated my warmth with a wave of his fork. He spoke soothingly and with a winning persuasiveness. He practically cooed.

===Chinese===

我简直摸不着头脑。我的晨间邮件，无论愉快与否，无论送来的是铺子的账单还是编辑的支票，至今都有个一律的长处：明白、直截、好懂。这一封却把我难住了。厄克里奇的姨妈怎么知道世上有我这么个人，我的拜访又如何能为她排忧解难——这些谜，我参不透，只好对着它发愣，像埃及学家对着一块新出土的圣书体碑文。
“她说什么？”厄克里奇打听。
“她请我明天下午四点半上门。”
“好极了！”厄克里奇叫道，“我就说她会上钩。”
“你到底在说什么？”
厄克里奇隔着桌子伸过手来，慈爱地拍拍我的肩。这个动作掀翻了一满杯咖啡，不过他的用意总是好的。他重新往椅背上一靠，扶正夹鼻眼镜，好把我看个仔细。我似乎叫他满心欢喜；他忽然高声赞美起我来，倒像古时哪位行吟诗人，即兴给自己的首领兼雇主吹擂一段。
“小老弟，”厄克里奇说，“你的为人，若说有一样我最佩服，就是你随时肯帮朋友一把。这是男人身上顶难得的品质之一，而谁也比不过你。这一点上你简直是独一份。常有汉子走过来向我打听你。‘那是个什么样的人？’他们问。‘头一等的好人，’我答，‘靠得住的朋友。宁死也不拆你台的人。为朋友两肋插刀也肯的人。金子心、钢铁性子的人。’”
“是，我是条好汉，”我表示同意，这顿捧叫我有几分发懵。“往下说。”
“我正往下说，老伙计，”厄克里奇略带责备地说，“我要说的是：我知道你准乐意替我把这桩小差事接下来。都不用求你。我早知道。”
一阵大祸临头的阴冷预感爬上心头——同厄克里奇打交道，这滋味我已尝过许多回。
“能不能劳驾告诉我，这回你又把我坑到哪桩该死的事里了？”
厄克里奇挥了挥叉子，不以为然地按住我的火气。他说得温存，带着动人的说服力。他简直是在咕咕叫。

===Original===

“It’s nothing, laddie. Practically nothing. Just a simple little act of kindness which you will thank me for putting in your way. It’s like this. As I ought to have foreseen from the first, that ass Tuppy proved a broken reed. In that matter of Dora, you know. Got no result whatever. He went to see my aunt the day before yesterday, and asked her to take Dora on again, and she gave him the miss-in-balk. I’m not surprised. I never had any confidence in Tuppy. It was a mistake ever sending him. It’s no good trying frontal attack in a delicate business like this. What you need is strategy. You want to think what is the enemy’s weak side and then attack from that angle. Now, what is my aunt’s weak side, laddie? Her weak side, what is it? Now think. Reflect, old horse.”

“From the sound of her voice, the only time I ever got near her, I should say she hadn’t one.”

===Chinese===

“没什么，小老弟。实实在在没什么。不过一桩小小的善举，你还得谢我给你牵线。是这么回事：果然不出我当初所料，塔皮那头蠢驴成了根靠不住的芦苇。多拉那件事，你懂。毫无结果。他前天去见我姨妈，请她把多拉收回去，碰了个软钉子。我一点不奇怪。我对塔皮从来没信心。派他去就是个错。这种需要细功夫的营生，正面强攻不行。要的是谋略。得琢磨敌人的软肋在哪儿，从那一路进攻。那么，小老弟，我姨妈的软肋在哪儿？她的软肋，是什么？想想。掂量掂量，老伙计。”
“就我唯一一次领教她嗓音的架势判断，她压根没有软肋。”

===Original===

“That’s where you make your error, old son. Butter her up about her beastly novels, and a child could eat out of her hand. When Tuppy let me down I just lit a pipe and had a good think. And then suddenly I got it. I went to a pal of mine, a thorough sportsman﻿—you don’t know him. I must introduce you some day﻿—and he wrote my aunt a letter from you, asking if you could come and interview her for *Woman’s Sphere*. It’s a weekly paper, which I happen to know she takes in regularly. Now, listen, laddie. Don’t interrupt for a moment. I want you to get the devilish shrewdness of this. You go and interview her, and she’s all over you. Tickled to death. Of course, you’ll have to do a good deal of Young Disciple stuff, but you won’t mind that. After you’ve soft-soaped her till she’s purring like a dynamo, you get up to go. ‘Well,’ you say, ‘this has been the proudest occasion of my life, meeting one whose work I have so long admired.’ And she says, ‘The pleasure is mine, old horse.’ And you slop over each other a bit more. Then you say sort of casually, as if it had just occurred to you, ‘Oh, by the way, I believe my cousin﻿—or sister﻿—No, better make it cousin﻿—I believe my cousin, Miss Dora Mason, is your secretary, isn’t she?’ ‘She isn’t any such dam’ thing,’ replies my aunt. ‘I sacked her three days ago.’ That’s your cue, laddie. Your face falls, you register concern, you’re frightfully cut up. You start in to ask her to let Dora come back. And you’re such pals by this time that she can refuse you nothing. And there you are! My dear old son, you can take it from me that if you only keep your head and do the Young Disciple stuff properly the thing can’t fail. It’s an ironclad scheme. There isn’t a flaw in it.”

“There is one.”

“I think you’re wrong. I’ve gone over the thing very carefully. What is it?”

“The flaw is that I’m not going anywhere near your infernal aunt. So you can trot back to your forger chum and tell him he’s wasted a good sheet of letter paper.”

A pair of pince-nez tinkled into a plate. Two pained eyes blinked at me across the table. Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge was wounded to the quick.

“You don’t mean to say you’re backing out?” he said, in a low, quivering voice.

“I never was in.”

===Chinese===

“这你可就错了，老兄。夸她那些烂小说，夸得她一个孩子都能哄。塔皮误了我的事，我就点上一斗烟，好生想了一回。猛可里我想到了。我去找了位朋友，一位十足的好汉——你不认识，改天引荐——他以你的名义给我姨妈写了封信，问能不能上门为《女性界》采访她。是份周刊，我恰好知道她期期都订。听着，小老弟，先别插嘴。我要你领会这里的毒辣精明。你上门采访她，她把你当个宝。欢喜煞了。当然，好一段‘虔诚门徒’的戏你得做足，这你不介意。等蜜糖抹得她像台发电机似的咕噜咕噜，你起身告辞。‘今天，’你说，‘得见平素如此敬仰其作品的作家，是我生平最荣幸的一天。’她说：‘荣幸的是我，老伙计。’彼此再甜一阵。然后你像是刚刚想起，随口说：‘哦对了，我想我表妹——还是表姐——不，说表妹稳妥——我想我表妹多拉·梅森小姐，是您的秘书吧？’‘才不是呢，’我姨妈答，‘三天前我就把她辞了。’这就是你的关节，小老弟。你脸色一沉，满面关切，痛心疾首。你开口求她让多拉回来。到这步你们交情已深，她什么都拒绝不了你。这不就结了！亲爱的老兄，你听我的：只要沉得住气、‘虔诚门徒’那套演到家，此事万无一失。铁一般的方案，一处破绽也没有。”
“有一处。”
“你错了。这方案我翻来覆去推敲过。破绽在哪儿？”
“破绽就是：你那位地狱姨妈的门，我一寸也不靠近。你可以跑回你那位造假的朋友那儿，告诉他，他浪费了一张挺好的信纸。”
一副夹鼻眼镜叮当落进盘子。一双痛心的眼睛隔着桌子朝我眨巴。斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇伤透了心。
“你不会是说，你要缩？”他低声发颤地说。
“我从来就没上过船。”

===Original===

“Laddie,” said Ukridge, weightily, resting an elbow on his last slice of bacon, “I want to ask you one question. Just one simple question. Have you ever let me down? Has there been one occasion in our long friendship when I have relied upon you and been deceived? Not one!”

“Everything’s got to have a beginning. I’m starting now.”

“But think of her. Dora! Poor little Dora. Think of poor little Dora.”

“If this business teaches her to keep away from you, it will be a blessing in the end.”

“But, laddie﻿—”

I suppose there is some fatal weakness in my character, or else the brand of bacon which Bowles cooked possessed a peculiarly mellowing quality. All I know is that, after being adamant for a good ten minutes, I finished breakfast committed to a task from which my soul revolted. After all, as Ukridge said, it was rough on the girl. Chivalry is chivalry. We must strive to lend a helping hand as we go through this world of ours, and all that sort of thing. Four o’clock on the following afternoon found me entering a cab and giving the driver the address of Heath House, Wimbledon Common.

===Chinese===

“小老弟，”厄克里奇把胳膊肘支在他最后一片咸肉上，郑重地说，“我只问你一个问题。就一个简单的问题。你可曾拆过我的台？咱们这么长的交情里，我指望过你，可曾被你误过一回？一回也没有！”
“凡事总有个开头。我现在开头。”
“可你想想她。多拉！可怜的小多拉。想想可怜的小多拉。”
“这件事若能教她躲你远点，到头来倒是她的福气。”
“可是，小老弟——”
想必我的性格里藏着某种致命的弱点；再不，就是鲍尔斯煎的这咸肉格外有化人之功。总之，硬了足足十分钟之后，一顿早餐吃完，我已把自己搭进了一桩灵魂深恶痛绝的差事。说到底，正如厄克里奇所言，这事于那姑娘确是不公。骑士精神就是骑士精神。人活一世，总得尽力搭把手，如此等等。第二天下午四点，我已坐进一辆马车，向车夫报出地址：温布尔登公地，石楠府。

===Original===

My emotions on entering Heath House were such as I would have felt had I been keeping a tryst with a dentist who by some strange freak happened also to be a duke. From the moment when a butler of super-Bowles dignity opened the door and, after regarding me with ill-concealed dislike, started to conduct me down a long hall, I was in the grip of both fear and humility. Heath House is one of the stately homes of Wimbledon; how beautiful they stand, as the poet says: and after the humble drabness of Ebury Street it frankly overawed me. Its keynote was an extreme neatness which seemed to sneer at my squashy collar and reproach my baggy trouser-leg. The farther I penetrated over the polished floor, the more vividly was it brought home to me that I was one of the submerged tenth and could have done with a haircut. I had not been aware when I left home that my hair was unusually long, but now I seemed to be festooned by a matted and offensive growth. A patch on my left shoe which had had a rather comfortable look in Ebury Street stood out like a blot on the landscape. No, I was not at my ease; and when I reflected that in a few moments I was to meet Ukridge’s aunt, that legendary figure, face to face, a sort of wistful admiration filled me for the beauty of the nature of one who would go through all this to help a girl he had never even met. There was no doubt about it﻿—the facts spoke for themselves﻿—I was one of the finest fellows I had ever known. Nevertheless, there was no getting away from it, my trousers did bag at the knee.

“Mr. Corcoran,” announced the butler, opening the drawing-room door. He spoke with just that intonation of voice that seemed to disclaim all responsibility. If I had an appointment, he intimated, it was his duty, however repulsive, to show me in; but, that done, he dissociated himself entirely from the whole affair.

There were two women and six Pekingese dogs in the room. The Pekes I had met before, during their brief undergraduate days at Ukridge’s dog college, but they did not appear to recognise me. The occasion when they had lunched at my expense seemed to have passed from their minds. One by one they came up, sniffed, and then moved away as if my bouquet had disappointed them. They gave the impression that they saw eye to eye with the butler in his estimate of the young visitor. I was left to face the two women.

===Chinese===

踏进石楠府的心情，好比去赴一位牙医的约——而这位牙医鬼使神差又兼着公爵。打那位气派压过鲍尔斯一头的管家开了门、并用毫不掩饰的嫌恶打量我、领我走下长长的门厅那一刻起，恐惧与自卑便双双攥住了我。石楠府是温布尔登的豪宅之一；诗人说得好，它们亭亭玉立，何等气象；见惯了伊伯里街的低矮寒酸，它把我彻底镇住了。它的基调是极端的整洁，仿佛时时冷笑我发软的领子、数落我打晃的裤腿。在打过蜡的地板上走得越深，我越痛切地感到：我属于沉沦的十分之一，而且早该理个发了。离家时并不觉得头发有多长，此刻却仿佛满头披挂着纠结的、冒犯观瞻的荒草。左脚那只鞋上的一块补丁，在伊伯里街看着还挺熨帖，此刻却像美景上的一摊污渍。是的，我浑身不自在；一想到片刻之后就要同厄克里奇的姨妈——那位传奇人物——当面相见，对那位的品性我不由涌起一阵钦羡：为了帮一个素未谋面的姑娘，肯把这一切都受下来。没跑——事实胜于雄辩——我所认识的人里，我是数得着的好汉。不过，话说回来，膝盖那儿的确打晃，赖不掉。
“科科伦先生。”管家推开客厅门，通报。他那语调，恰是一副概不负责的口吻。倘若我有约在先，他的意思是，那么领我进来便是他的职责，再恶心也是职责；办完这一件，他便与全局一刀两断。
客厅里是两位女士和六只哈巴狗。狗我在厄克里奇狗学堂的短暂“本科”年代领教过，可它们像不认得我。在我破费请它们用过午餐一事，似乎已从它们的记忆里抹去了。它们挨个凑上来，嗅一嗅，又踱开去，仿佛我的气味很叫它们失望。它们给人的印象是：对这位年轻来客的评价，同管家所见略同。我只好独自面对那两位女士。

===Original===

Of these﻿—reading from right to left﻿—one was a tall, angular, hawk-faced female with a stony eye. The other, to whom I gave but a passing glance at the moment, was small, and so it seemed to me, pleasant-looking. She had bright hair faintly powdered with grey, and mild eyes of a china blue. She reminded me of the better class of cat. I took her to be some casual caller who had looked in for a cup of tea. It was the hawk on whom I riveted my attention. She was looking at me with a piercing and unpleasant stare, and I thought how exactly she resembled the picture I had formed of her in my mind from Ukridge’s conversation.

“Miss Ukridge?” I said, sliding on a rug towards her and feeling like some novice whose manager, against his personal wishes, has fixed him up with a match with the heavyweight champion.

“I am Miss Ukridge,” said the other woman. “Miss Watterson, Mr. Corcoran.”

It was a shock, but, the moment of surprise over, I began to feel something approaching mental comfort for the first time since I had entered this house of slippery rugs and supercilious butlers. Somehow I had got the impression from Ukridge that his aunt was a sort of stage aunt, all stiff satin and raised eyebrows. This half-portion with the mild blue eyes I felt that I could tackle. It passed my comprehension why Ukridge should ever have found her intimidating.

“I hope you will not mind if we have our little talk before Miss Watterson,” she said with a charming smile. “She has come to arrange the details of the Pen and Ink Club dance which we are giving shortly. She will keep quite quiet and not interrupt. You don’t mind?”

“Not at all, not at all,” I said in my attractive way. It is not exaggerating to say that at this moment I felt debonair. “Not at all, not at all. Oh, not at all.”

“Won’t you sit down?”

“Thank you, thank you.”

The hawk moved over to the window, leaving us to ourselves.

“Now we are quite cosy,” said Ukridge’s aunt.

“Yes, yes,” I agreed. Dash it, I liked this woman.

“Tell me, Mr. Corcoran,” said Ukridge’s aunt, “are you on the staff of *Woman’s Sphere*? It is one of my favourite papers. I read it every week.”

“The outside staff.”

“What do you mean by the outside staff?”

“Well, I don’t actually work in the office, but the editor gives me occasional jobs.”

“I see. Who is the editor now?”

===Chinese===

这两位——自右向左念去——一位高挑、棱角分明、鹰隼脸、目光冷硬；另一位我当时只扫了一眼，个子娇小，模样——我觉得——可亲，早生的灰白淡淡地掺在亮发里，一双温吞的中国蓝眼睛。她叫我想起上等品种的猫。我当她是顺路进来吃杯茶的闲客。我凝神对付的是那只鹰。她正用尖利而不快的目光打量我；我心想，这同我凭厄克里奇的描述在脑中勾勒的画像，分毫不差。
“厄克里奇小姐？”我说着，在一块小地毯上打了个滑，自觉像个新人拳手，被经理违着本意、安排去同重量级冠军过招。
“我才是厄克里奇小姐，”另一位女士说。“沃特森小姐，这位是科科伦先生。”
这是个意外；可惊愕一过，我进了这满屋滑地毯、傲慢管家的宅子以来，头一回感到了近乎心安的东西。不晓得怎么，厄克里奇给我留下的印象是：他姨妈是戏台上那种姨妈，一身硬缎、双眉高挑。眼下这个蓝眼睛温柔的小不点，我觉得对付得了。厄克里奇何以怕她怕成那样，超出了我的理解。
“咱们的小小谈话，有沃特森小姐在场，您不会介意吧？”她和蔼地笑着说，“她来商量笔墨俱乐部不久后要办的舞会的细节。她会安安静静，不插嘴。您不介意吧？”
“不介意，一点也不。”我以我动人的风度说。不夸大地说，此刻我觉得自己风流倜傥。“一点也不，一点也不。哦，一点也不。”
“请坐。”
“谢谢，谢谢。”
那只鹰踱到窗边去，把这一角留给我们。
“现在咱们方便了。”厄克里奇的姨妈说。
“是，是。”我应和。说真的，我喜欢上了这位太太。
“告诉我，科科伦先生，”厄克里奇的姨妈说，“您在《女性界》供职吗？那是我心爱的刊物。我期期都读。”
“外面的编辑队伍。”
“外面的编辑队伍，怎么讲？”
“呃，我并不常驻编辑部，不过主编时不时派我点活。”
“明白了。如今的主编是谁？”

===Original===

I began to feel slightly less debonair. She was just making conversation, of course, to put me at my ease, but I wished she would stop asking me these questions. I searched desperately in my mind for a name﻿—any name﻿—but as usual on these occasions every name in the English language had passed from me.

“Of course. I remember now,” said Ukridge’s aunt, to my profound relief. “It’s Mr. Jevons, isn’t it? I met him one night at dinner.”

“Jevons,” I burbled. “That’s right. Jevons.”

“A tall man with a light moustache.”

“Well, fairly tall,” I said, judicially.

“And he sent you here to interview me?”

“Yes.”

“Well, which of my novels do you wish me to talk about?”

I relaxed with a delightful sense of relief. I felt on solid ground at last. And then it suddenly came to me that Ukridge in his woollen-headed way had omitted to mention the name of a single one of this woman’s books.

“Er﻿—oh, all of them,” I said hurriedly.

“I see. My general literary work.”

“Exactly,” I said. My feeling towards her now was one of positive affection.

She leaned back in her chair with her fingertips together, a pretty look of meditation on her face.

“Do you think it would interest the readers of *Woman’s Sphere* to know which novel of mine is my own favourite?”

“I am sure it would.”

“Of course,” said Ukridge’s aunt, “it is not easy for an author to answer a question like that. You see, one has moods in which first one book and then another appeals to one.”

“Quite,” I replied. “Quite.”

“Which of my books do *you* like best, Mr. Corcoran?”

There swept over me the trapped feeling one gets in nightmares. From six baskets the six Pekingese stared at me unwinkingly.

“Er﻿—oh, all of them,” I heard a croaking voice reply. My voice, presumably, though I did not recognise it.

“How delightful!” said Ukridge’s aunt. “Now, I really do call that delightful. One or two of the critics have said that my work was uneven. It is so nice to meet someone who doesn’t agree with them. Personally, I think my favourite is *The Heart of Adelaide*.”

I nodded my approval of this sound choice. The muscles which had humped themselves stiffly on my back began to crawl back into place again. I found it possible to breathe.

“Yes,” I said, frowning thoughtfully, “I suppose *The Heart of Adelaide* is the best thing you have written. It has such human appeal,” I added, playing it safe.

===Chinese===

我的风流倜傥开始打折扣。她自然只是没话找话、好叫我放松；可我但愿她别再问了。我在脑子里发疯似的搜名字——随便什么名字——偏偏每逢这种场合，英语里所有的名字照例跑个精光。
“对了，我想起来了，”厄克里奇的姨妈说，叫我如释重负。“是杰文斯先生，对吧？有天晚宴上我遇过他。”
“杰文斯，”我连声附和，“正是。杰文斯。”
“高个子，留两撇浅色小胡子。”
“呃，算高吧。”我以裁判的口吻裁定。
“是他派您来采访我的？”
“是。”
“那么，您要我谈哪一部小说呢？”
我松了口气，通体舒泰。总算踩上实地了。随即猛然想起：厄克里奇那颗毛线脑子，偏偏漏了没提这女人哪怕一部书名。
“呃——哦，全部。”我慌忙答。
“明白了。我的整体文学创作。”
“正是。”我说。此刻我对她的感情，已是正面的疼爱了。
她朝椅背上一靠，指尖抵着指尖，脸上一副动人的沉思。
“您认为，把我自己最心爱的一部作品告诉《女性界》的读者，他们会有兴趣么？”
“一定有。”
“当然，”厄克里奇的姨妈说，“这样的问题，作家不好答。您知道，人的兴致有起有落，今天爱这本，明天爱那本。”
“正是，”我答，“正是。”
“您最喜欢我的哪一本呢，科科伦先生？”
一阵噩梦般的困兽之感兜头罩下。六只哈巴狗从六只筐里一眨不眨地瞪着我。
“呃——哦，全部。”我听见一个嘶哑的声音答。多半是我的声音，虽然我自己没听出来。
“真可爱！”厄克里奇的姨妈说。“这我可真要叫一声可爱了。有一两位批评家说过，我的作品水平不匀。能遇上不赞成他们的人，多好。就我个人说，我最心爱的是《阿德莱德之心》。”
我点头赞同这一稳妥的选择。背上僵成一团的肌肉开始各归其位。我觉得又能喘气了。
“是，”我若有所思地皱着眉说，“我看《阿德莱德之心》是您写得最好的一部。它有那样的人情味。”我添了一句，走个稳字。

===Original===

“Have you read it, Mr. Corcoran?”

“Oh yes.”

“And you really enjoyed it?”

“Tremendously.”

“You don’t think it is a fair criticism to say that it is a little broad in parts?”

“Most unfair.” I began to see my way. I do not know why, but I had been assuming that her novels must be the sort you find in seaside libraries. Evidently they belonged to the other class of female novels, the sort which libraries ban. “Of course,” I said, “it is written honestly, fearlessly, and shows life as it is. But broad? No, no!”

“That scene in the conservatory?”

“Best thing in the book,” I said stoutly.

A pleased smile played about her mouth. Ukridge had been right. Praise her work, and a child could eat out of her hand. I found myself wishing that I had really read the thing, so that I could have gone into more detail and made her still happier.

“I’m so glad you like it,” she said. “Really, it is most encouraging.”

“Oh, no,” I murmured modestly.

“Oh, but it is. Because I have only just started to write it, you see. I finished chapter one this morning.”

She was still smiling so engagingly that for a moment the full horror of these words did not penetrate my consciousness.

“*The Heart of Adelaide* is my next novel. The scene in the conservatory, which you like so much, comes towards the middle of it. I was not expecting to reach it till about the end of next month. How odd that you should know all about it!”

I had got it now all right, and it was like sitting down on the empty space where there should have been a chair. Somehow the fact that she was so pleasant about it all served to deepen my discomfiture. In the course of an active life I have frequently felt a fool, but never such a fool as I felt then. The fearful woman had been playing with me, leading me on, watching me entangle myself like a fly on flypaper. And suddenly I perceived that I had erred in thinking of her eyes as mild. A hard gleam had come into them. They were like a couple of blue gimlets. She looked like a cat that had caught a mouse, and it was revealed to me in one sickening age-long instant why Ukridge went in fear of her. There was that about her which would have intimidated the Sheik.

===Chinese===

“您读过么，科科伦先生？”
“哦，读过。”
“当真喜欢？”
“喜欢极了。”
“有人说它个别地方写得略嫌大胆，您不认为这批评有失公道么？”
“大大有失公道。”我开始看出路子了。不知怎么，我一直想当然，以为她的小说必是海滨流通图书馆里那一类。看来她们那一路的，属于女性小说的另一支——被图书馆禁的那支。“当然，”我说，“它写得诚实，写得无所畏惧，写出了生活的本来面目。大胆？不，不是这个词！”
“温室里那一场？”
“全书最好的一场。”我理直气壮地说。
她唇边漾起满足的笑。厄克里奇说得不错。夸她的作品，夸得她一个孩子都能哄。我竟巴不得真读过那书，好往下细谈，叫她更欢喜几分。
“您喜欢，我真高兴。”她说。“实在叫人鼓舞。”
“哪里，哪里。”我谦虚地喃喃。
“不，实在叫人鼓舞。因为您瞧，我才刚开始写它。第一章是今天早晨写完的。”
她仍旧笑得那样可亲，以致这几个字的全部恐怖，一时没能渗进我的意识。
“《阿德莱德之心》是我的下一部小说。您那么喜欢的温室那一场，在全书的中间靠后。我自己都没想到月底前能写到那儿。您竟已了如指掌，多巧啊！”
这回我全明白了——那滋味，像一屁股坐进本该有椅子却是空着的所在。不知怎么，她偏偏这样和颜悦色，愈发衬得我狼狈透顶。活跃的一生里，我不止一回觉得自己是个傻子；可从来没有一回，傻成这样。这个可怕的女人一直在逗着我玩，引我上钩，看我像苍蝇粘上粘蝇纸那样越缠越紧。而且我顿时醒悟，先前把她的眼睛认作温吞，是看走了眼。眼里已透出一道硬光。那是一对蓝色的手钻。她像一只逮住老鼠的猫；在那漫长得一瞬、叫人作呕的一瞬里，我彻悟了厄克里奇何以怕她。这份气性，怕是连“沙漠酋长”也要叫她镇住。

===Original===

“It seems so odd, too,” she tinkled on, “that you should have come to interview me for *Woman’s Sphere*. Because they published an interview with me only the week before last. I thought it so strange that I rang up my friend Miss Watterson, who is the editress, and asked her if there had not been some mistake. And she said she had never heard of you. *Have* you ever heard of Mr. Corcoran, Muriel?”

“Never,” said the hawk, fixing me with a revolted eye.

“How strange!” said Ukridge’s aunt. “But then the whole thing is so strange. Oh, must you go, Mr. Corcoran?”

My mind was in a slightly chaotic condition, but on that one point it was crystal-clear. Yes, I must go. Through the door if I could find it﻿—failing that, through the window. And anybody who tried to stop me would do well to have a care.

“You will remember me to Mr. Jevons when you see him, won’t you?” said Ukridge’s aunt.

I was fumbling at the handle.

“And, Mr. Corcoran.” She was still smiling amiably, but there had come into her voice a note like that which it had had on a certain memorable occasion when summoning Ukridge to his doom from the unseen interior of his Sheep’s Cray Cottage. “Will you please tell my nephew Stanley that I should be glad if he would send no more of his friends to see me. Good afternoon.”

I suppose that at some point in the proceedings my hostess must have rung a bell, for out in the passage I found my old chum, the butler. With the uncanny telepathy of his species he appeared aware that I was leaving under what might be called a cloud, for his manner had taken on a warder-like grimness. His hand looked as if it was itching to grasp me by the shoulder, and when we reached the front door he eyed the pavement wistfully, as if thinking what splendid spot it would be for me to hit with a thud.

“Nice day,” I said, with the feverish instinct to babble which comes to strong men in their agony.

He scorned to reply, and as I tottered down the sunlit street I was conscious of his gaze following me.

“A very vicious specimen,” I could fancy him saying. “And mainly due to my prudence and foresight that he hasn’t got away with the spoons.”

===Chinese===

“更巧的是，”她叮叮咚咚地接着说，“您竟是为《女性界》来采访我的。因为前两个星期，他们才登过一篇对我的采访。我觉得太蹊跷，就打电话给我的朋友沃特森小姐——她就是主编——问她是不是弄错了。她说从没听说过您。缪丽尔，您听说过科科伦先生么？”
“从没。”那只鹰说着，用嫌恶的目光钉住我。
“多奇怪！”厄克里奇的姨妈说。“可整件事都这么奇怪。哦，您要走么，科科伦先生？”
我的心智已呈几分混沌，唯独这一桩水晶般明白：是，要走。有门走门——没有门，走窗。谁想拦我，最好先掂掂分量。
“见着杰文斯先生，替我问候一声，好么？”厄克里奇的姨妈说。
我正在门把手上瞎摸。
“还有，科科伦先生。”她依旧笑得和蔼，可嗓音里起了一个调子——同那个难忘的夜晚、从“羊溪小屋”看不见的内室里召唤厄克里奇去领他厄运时，一模一样的调子。“烦请转告我外甥斯坦利：请他别再派朋友来看我了。下午好。”
想必在哪个环节女主人拉过铃，因为过道里我又遇见了我的老朋友，那位管家。以他这一行的神秘感应，他像是察觉我是顶着一片乌云走的，神色里添了狱卒般的严峻。他那只手痒痒的，像要一把揪住我的肩膀；走到大门，他望着人行道出神，仿佛在盘算那一小块地用来接我“咚”的一声，该多合适。
“天气不错。”我说。硬汉在剧痛中总要絮絮叨叨，我正是那股冲动。
他不屑作答。我踉跄着走进洒满阳光的街道，背上觉出他的目光一路相送。
“好凶的一头货色，”我仿佛听见他在说，“多亏我谨慎、有远见，勺子才没叫他顺走。”

===Original===

It was a warm afternoon, but to such an extent had the recent happenings churned up my emotions that I walked the whole way back to Ebury Street with a rapidity which caused more languid pedestrians to regard me with a pitying contempt. Reaching my sitting room in an advanced state of solubility and fatigue, I found Ukridge stretched upon the sofa.

“Hallo, laddie!” said Ukridge, reaching out a hand for the cooling drink that lay on the floor beside him. “I was wondering when you would show up. I wanted to tell you that it won’t be necessary for you to go and see my aunt after all. It appears that Dora has a hundred quid tucked away in a bank, and she’s been offered a partnership by a woman she knows who runs one of these typewriting places. I advised her to close with it. So she’s all right.”

He quaffed deeply of the bowl and breathed a contented sigh. There was a silence.

“When did you hear of this?” I asked at length.

“Yesterday afternoon,” said Ukridge. “I meant to pop round and tell you, but somehow it slipped my mind.”

===Chinese===

那是个暖和的下午；可方才的种种把我的情绪搅得，我竟一路疾走回伊伯里街，快得叫懒散的行人报以怜悯的轻蔑。我拖着半溶解的身子、一脸疲惫进了起居室，只见厄克里奇四仰八叉躺在沙发上。
“哈罗，小老弟！”厄克里奇说着，伸手去够擑在他身边地板上的那杯凉饮。“我正纳闷你什么时候露面。正要告诉你：去看我姨妈那趟，你不用跑了。多拉原来在银行存着一百镑，认识的一个女人开了家打字行，请她合伙。我劝她一口应下。所以她没事了。”
他对着碗痛饮一番，心满意足地吁了口气。屋里静了一会儿。
“你什么时候听说的？”我总算问出一句。
“昨天下午，”厄克里奇说，“本想过来告诉你，不知怎么，忘了。”

## No Wedding Bells for Him / 他与婚礼钟声无缘
===Original===

To Ukridge, as might be expected from one of his sunny optimism, the whole affair has long since come to present itself in the light of yet another proof of the way in which all things in this world of ours work together for good. In it, from start to finish, he sees the finger of Providence; and, when marshalling evidence to support his theory that a means of escape from the most formidable perils will always be vouchsafed to the righteous and deserving, this is the episode which he advances as Exhibit A.

The thing may be said to have had its beginning in the Haymarket one afternoon towards the middle of the summer. We had been lunching at my expense at the Pall Mall Restaurant, and as we came out a large and shiny car drew up beside the kerb, and the chauffeur, alighting, opened the bonnet and began to fiddle about in its interior with a pair of pliers. Had I been alone, a casual glance in passing would have contented me, but for Ukridge the spectacle of somebody else working always had an irresistible fascination, and, gripping my arm, he steered me up to assist him in giving the toiler moral support. About two minutes after he had started to breathe earnestly on the man’s neck, the latter, seeming to become aware that what was tickling his back hair was not some wandering June zephyr, looked up with a certain petulance.

“ ’Ere!” he said, protestingly. Then his annoyance gave place to something which﻿—for a chauffeur﻿—approached cordiality. “ ’Ullo!” he observed.

“Why, hallo, Frederick,” said Ukridge. “Didn’t recognise you. Is this the new car?”

“Ah,” nodded the chauffeur.

“Pal of mine,” explained Ukridge to me in a brief aside. “Met him in a pub.” London was congested with pals whom Ukridge had met in pubs. “What’s the trouble?”

“Missing,” said Frederick the chauffeur. “Soon ’ave her right.”

His confidence in his skill was not misplaced. After a short interval he straightened himself, closed the bonnet, and wiped his hands.

“Nice day,” he said.

“Terrific,” agreed Ukridge. “Where are you off to?”

“Got to go to Addington. Pick up the guv’nor, playin’ golf there.” He seemed to hesitate for a moment, then the mellowing influence of the summer sunshine asserted itself. “Like a ride as far as East Croydon? Get a train back from there.”

===Chinese===

对于厄克里奇这样一个满怀阳光乐观的人来说，整个这件事，不用说，早已变成又一个例证，证明我们这个世界上万事都相互效力、叫人得益。从头到尾，他都在其中看见天意的手指；每当他列举证据，支持他那套“义人和好人总能得到逃脱最可怕险境的门路”的理论，这一段插曲总是被他当作甲号证物呈上。

这件事，可以说，起于仲夏时节的一个下午，地点在干草市场。那顿午饭是我做东，在帕尔马尔餐馆吃的；我们出来时，一辆锃亮的大汽车停在路边，司机下了车，掀开引擎盖，拿着一把钳子在里面摆弄。若是我一个人，路过扫一眼也就满足了；可对厄克里奇来说，看别人干活始终有一股无法抗拒的魅力，他一把攥住我的胳膊，把我拽上前去，帮着给那位劳动者道义上的声援。他对着那人的后颈诚恳地喷了约莫两分钟气流，后者似乎终于察觉到撩他后脑勺头发的并不是哪阵游荡的六月微风，抬头时带着几分愠怒。

“‘喂！”他抗议道。随即，他的恼怒让位给了某种——就一个司机而言——近乎热络的东西。“‘哟！”他说。

“哎呀，你好啊，弗雷德里克，”厄克里奇说。“没认出来。这是新车？”

“嗯，”司机点头。

“我一个哥们儿，”厄克里奇侧身向我简短介绍。“酒馆里认识的。”伦敦到处都是厄克里奇在酒馆里认识的哥们儿。“哪儿出毛病了？”

“不点火了，”弗雷德里克司机说。“一会儿就弄好。”

他对自己手艺的信心没有落空。不一会儿他直起身，合上引擎盖，擦了擦手。

“天儿不错。”他说。

“好得很，”厄克里奇附和。“这是上哪儿去？”

“得去阿丁顿。接老板，他在那儿打高尔夫。”他似乎迟疑了一下，随后夏日暖阳的融融影响占了上风。“捎你们到东克罗伊登怎么样？从那儿坐火车回来。”

===Original===

It was a handsome offer, and one which neither Ukridge nor myself felt disposed to decline. We climbed in, Frederick trod on the self-starter, and off we bowled, two gentlemen of fashion taking their afternoon airing. Speaking for myself, I felt tranquil and debonair, and I have no reason to suppose that Ukridge was otherwise. The deplorable incident which now occurred was thus rendered doubly distressing. We had stopped at the foot of the street to allow the northbound traffic to pass, when our pleasant after-luncheon torpidity was shattered by a sudden and violent shout.

“Hi!”

That the shouter was addressing us there was no room for doubt. He was standing on the pavement not four feet away, glaring unmistakably into our costly tonneau﻿—a stout, bearded man of middle age, unsuitably clad, considering the weather and the sartorial prejudices of Society, in a frock-coat and a bowler hat. “Hi! You!” he bellowed, to the scandal of all good passersby.

Frederick the chauffeur, after one swift glance of godlike disdain out of the corner of his left eye, had ceased to interest himself in this undignified exhibition on the part of one of the lower orders, but I was surprised to observe that Ukridge was betraying all the discomposure of some wild thing taken in a trap. His face had turned crimson and assumed a bulbous expression, and he was staring straight ahead of him with a piteous effort to ignore what manifestly would not be ignored.

“I’d like a word with you,” boomed the bearded one.

And then matters proceeded with a good deal of rapidity. The traffic had begun to move on now, and as we moved with it, travelling with increasing speed, the man appeared to realise that if ’twere done ’twere well ’twere done quickly. He executed a cumbersome leap and landed on our running-board; and Ukridge, coming suddenly to life, put out a large flat hand and pushed. The intruder dropped off, and the last I saw of him he was standing in the middle of the road, shaking his fist, in imminent danger of being run over by a number three omnibus.

“Gosh!” sighed Ukridge, with some feverishness.

“What was it all about?” I enquired.

“Bloke I owe a bit of money to,” explained Ukridge, tersely.

===Chinese===

这是份体面的美意，厄克里奇同我谁也不想推辞。我们钻进车，弗雷德里克踩下自动启动钮，车便滚滚而去——两位时髦绅士做午后兜风。就我而论，只觉心平气和、潇洒自在；我也没有理由认为厄克里奇另是一样。正因为如此，紧接着发生的那桩可叹事故，才加倍令人痛心。我们在街口停下，让北向的车流先过，饭后那阵愉快的慵懒，猛然被一声粗暴的大喊击得粉碎。

“喂！”
