我一时的恼火消散了，因为我忽然想到，换个角度看，这实在是一桩极大的恭维——这帮人死活不肯相信我可能是他们的同类。无疑，我想，他们此刻的立场，同某家夜总会的老板如出一辙：那位老板拒绝一位小姐入内，理由是她不宜与会员为伍，被告上诽谤法庭后，向法官解释说，他那是当恭维讲的。

"不是，谢天谢地！"

"那么——"

"新闻票，"我说明。

"新闻票？哪家的报？"

"《社交界》。"

===Original===

There was nothing of the Julia Ukridge spirit in this young man, no ingrained pride which kept him aloof and outwardly indifferent. He beamed like the rising sun. He grasped my arm and kneaded it. He gambolled about me like a young lamb in the springtime.

“My dear fellow!” he exclaimed, exuberantly, and clutched my arm more firmly, lest even now I might elude him. “My dear fellow, I really must apologise. I would not have questioned you, but there are some persons present who were not invited. I met a man only a moment ago who said that he had bought a ticket. Some absurd mistake. There were no tickets for sale. I was about to question him further, but he disappeared into the crowd and I have not seen him since. This is a quite private dance, open only to members of the club. Come with me, my dear fellow, and I will give you a few particulars which you may find of use for your article.”

He led me resolutely into a small room off the floor, closed the door to prevent escape, and, on the principle on which you rub a cat’s paws with butter to induce it to settle down in a new home, began to fuss about with whisky and cigarettes.

“Do, do sit down.”

I sat down.

“First, about this club. The Pen and Ink Club is the only really exclusive organisation of its kind in London. We pride ourselves on the fact. We are to the literary world what Brooks’s and the Carlton are to the social. Members are elected solely by invitation. Election, in short, you understand, is in the nature of an accolade. We have exactly one hundred members, and we include only those writers who in our opinion possess vision.”

“And the big, broad, flexible outlook?”

“I beg your pardon?”

“Nothing.”

“The names of most of those here tonight must be very familiar to you.”

“I know Miss Ukridge, the president,” I said.

A faint, almost imperceptible shadow passed over the stout young man’s face. He removed his pince-nez and polished them with a touch of disfavour. There was a rather flat note in his voice.

“Ah, yes,” he said, “Julia Ukridge. A dear soul, but between ourselves, strictly between ourselves, not a great deal of help in an executive capacity.”

“No!”

“No. In confidence, I do all the work. I am the club’s secretary. My name, by the way, is Charlton Prout. You may know it?”

===Chinese===

这位青年身上没有一丝朱莉娅·厄克里奇的气性，没有那种叫他矜持孤傲、外表冷淡的根深蒂固的自尊。他笑得像初升的太阳。他抓住我的胳膊揉捏。他围着我蹦跳，像春天里的小羊羔。

"亲爱的伙计！"他热烈地喊，把我的胳膊攥得更紧，仿佛怕我此刻还要溜走。"亲爱的伙计，我实在失敬。我本不欲盘问您，只是今晚在场有些未受邀之人。方才我就撞见一位，说票是他买的。荒唐的误会。本会舞会并无售票一说。我正要细问，他钻进人堆就不见了。这是一场纯私人的舞会，仅限本会会员。跟我来，亲爱的伙计，我给您提供一点素材，您那篇报道或许用得上。"

他毅然把我领进舞池旁的一间小屋，关上门以防逃脱，又本着给猫爪抹黄油好让猫在新家安顿的原理，忙不迭地张罗威士忌和香烟。

"坐，快坐。"

我坐下了。

"先说本会。笔墨俱乐部是伦敦惟一真正排他的同类团体。我们引以为豪。在文学界，我们的地位相当于社交界的布鲁克斯与卡尔顿。会员纯凭邀请当选。选举，简而言之，您懂的，具有册封的性质。我们整整一百名会员，只收那些依我们看具备远见的作家。"

"还有那宏大、宽广、灵活的眼光？"

"您说什么？"

"没什么。"

"今晚在场诸君的大名，您想必大多如雷贯耳。"

"我认识会长厄克里奇小姐，"我说。

一层几乎不可察的阴影掠过这胖青年的脸。他摘下夹鼻眼镜，带着一点不以为然擦拭着。他的嗓音扁了几分。

"啊，是，"他说，"朱莉娅·厄克里奇。可亲的人，可是咱们私下说，严格私下说，抓起行政来帮不了什么忙。"

"不会吧！"

"会的。说句体己话，活儿全是我干的。我是本会秘书。鄙姓查尔顿·普劳特（Charlton Prout）。您也许听说过？"

===Original===

He eyed me wistfully, and I felt that something ought to be done about him. He was much too sleek, and he had no right to do his hair like that.

“Of course,” I said. “I have read all your books.”

“Really?”

“*A Shriek in the Night*. *Who Killed Jasper Bossom?*﻿—all of them.”

He stiffened austerely.

“You must be confusing me with some other﻿—ah﻿—writer,” he said. “My work is on somewhat different lines. The reviewers usually describe the sort of thing I do as Pastels in Prose. My best-liked book, I believe, is *Grey Myrtles*. Dunstable’s brought it out last year. It was exceedingly well received. And I do a good deal of critical work for the better class of review.” He paused. “If you think it would interest your readers,” he said, with a deprecating wave of the hand, “I will send you a photograph. Possibly your editor would like to use it.”

“I bet he would.”

“A photograph somehow seems to﻿—as it were﻿—set off an article of this kind.”

“That,” I replied, cordially, “is what it doesn’t do nothing else but.”

“And you won’t forget *Grey Myrtles*. Well, if you have finished your cigarette, we might be returning to the ballroom. These people rather rely on me to keep things going, you know.”

A burst of music greeted us as he opened the door, and even in that first moment I had an odd feeling that it sounded different. That tinny sound had gone from it. And as we debouched from behind a potted palm and came in sight of the floor, I realised why.

The floor was full. It was crammed, jammed, and overflowing. Where couples had moved as single spies, they were now in battalions. The place was alive with noise and laughter. These people might, as my companion had said, be relying on him to keep things going, but they seemed to have been getting along uncommonly well in his absence. I paused and surveyed the mob in astonishment. I could not make the man’s figures balance.

“I thought you said the Pen and Ink Club had only a hundred members.”

The secretary was fumbling for his glasses. He had an almost Ukridge-like knack of dropping his pince-nez in moments of emotion.

“It﻿—it has,” he stammered.

“Well, reading from left to right, I make it nearer seven hundred.”

“I cannot understand it.”

“Perhaps they have been having a new election and letting in some writers without vision,” I suggested.

I was aware of Miss Ukridge bearing down upon us, bristling.

===Chinese===

他以期盼的眼光望我，我觉得该为这一位做点什么。他太油光水滑了，头发也不该梳成那个样子。

"当然，"我说。"您的大作我全拜读过。"

"真的？"

"《夜半惊呼》（*A Shriek in the Night*）。《谁杀了贾斯珀·博瑟姆？》（*Who Killed Jasper Bossom?*）——全部。"

他严正地一僵。

"您一定是把我跟另一位——啊——作家搞混了，"他说。"我的作品路数不同。评论界通常把我写的那类东西称作'散文粉彩'。最受读者爱戴的一本，我想是《灰色的桃金娘》（*Grey Myrtles*）。邓斯特布尔公司去年出的。反响极好。我还为上流评论刊物写相当分量的批评文字。"他顿了顿。"您要是觉得贵报读者会有兴趣，"他虚晃一手，"我可以寄您一张照片。也许贵编辑愿意刊用。"

"他一定愿意。"

"配这类报道，有张照片总好像——嗯——相得益彰。"

"岂止，"我热诚地答，"简直是别无所长。"

"还有，别漏了《灰色的桃金娘》。好，您这支烟要是抽完了，咱们回舞厅去吧。这些人离了我，靠我撑场面呢，您知道的。"

他一开门，一阵音乐迎面涌来，第一个瞬间我就有种异样感：声音变了。那种发薄的金属声没有了。等我们从一盆棕榈后转出来、望见舞池，我明白了为什么。

舞池满了。挤满，塞满，往外漫。先前还是单镖探路的舞侣，如今已列营成阵。满场是喧声与笑语。这些人也许真如我同伴所说，离了他就没法热闹，可他不在的当口，他们分明自己闹腾得有声有色。我驻足，惊愕地检阅这乌泱泱一片。这位先生的账我算不平。

"我记得你说过，笔墨俱乐部只有一百名会员。"

秘书正摸索他的眼镜。他有个几乎不输厄克里奇的本事：一激动夹鼻眼镜就往下掉。

"是……是有一百，"他结结巴巴。

"呃，从左往右数，我数出的是将近七百。"

"我闹不明白。"

"也许是新搞了一次选举，放进了一些没有远见的作家，"我提了个说法。

我察觉厄克里奇小姐杀气腾腾地朝我们逼近。

===Original===

“Mr. Prout!”

The talented young author of *Grey Myrtles* leaped convulsively.

“Yes, Miss Ukridge?”

“Who are all these people?”

“I﻿—I don’t know,” said the talented young man.

“You don’t know! It’s your business to know. You are the secretary of the club. I suggest that you find out as quickly as possible who they are and what they imagine they are doing here.”

The goaded secretary had something of the air of a man leading a forlorn hope, and his ears had turned bright pink, but he went at it bravely. A serene-looking man with a light moustache and a made-up tie was passing, and he sprang upon him like a stoutish leopard.

“Excuse me, sir.”

“Eh?”

“Will you kindly﻿—would you mind﻿—pardon me if I ask﻿—”

“What are you doing here?” demanded Miss Ukridge, curtly, cutting in on his flounderings with a masterful impatience. “How do you come to be at this dance?”

The man seemed surprised.

“Who, me?” he said. “I came with the rest of ’em.”

“What do you mean, the rest of them?”

“The members of the Warner’s Stores Social and Outing Club.”

“But this is the dance of the Pen and Ink Club,” bleated Mr. Prout.

“Some mistake,” said the other, confidently. “It’s a bloomer of some kind. Here,” he added, beckoning to a portly gentleman of middle age who was bustling by, “you’d better have a talk with our hon. sec. He’ll know. Mr. Biggs, this gentleman seems to think there’s been some mistake about this dance.”

Mr. Biggs stopped, looked, and listened. Seen at close range, he had a forceful, determined air. I liked his looks.

“May I introduce Mr. Charlton Prout?” I said. “Author of *Grey Myrtles*. Mr. Prout,” I went on, as this seemed to make little or no sensation, “is the secretary of the Pen and Ink Club.”

“I’m the secretary of the Warner’s Stores Social and Outing Club,” said Mr. Biggs.

The two secretaries eyed each other warily, like two dogs.

“But what are you doing here?” moaned Mr. Prout, in a voice like the wind in the treetops. “This is a private dance.”

“Nothing of the kind,” said Mr. Biggs, resolutely. “I personally bought tickets for all my members.”

“But there were no tickets for sale. The dance was for the exclusive﻿—”

===Chinese===

"普劳特先生！"

《灰色的桃金娘》的天才青年作者痉挛般一跳。

"什么事，厄克里奇小姐？"

"这许多人都是谁？"

"我——我不知道，"天才青年说。

"不知道！搞明白是你的职责。你是本会秘书。我建议你以最快的速度查明他们的身份，以及他们自以为在这里做什么。"

被逼到墙角的秘书颇有几分孤军奋战的气概，耳朵涨成了粉红，但他勇敢地上了。一位神色安详、蓄浅髭、系活结领的男子正走过，他扑将上去，像一头微胖的豹。

"劳驾，先生。"

"嗯？"

"可否请您——能否劳驾——恕我冒昧一问——"

"你在这里做什么？"厄克里奇小姐厉声喝问，以干练的不耐切断了他的磕绊。"你怎么进的这个舞会？"

那人似乎很意外。

"谁，我？"他说。"我跟大伙儿一起来的呗。"

"什么大伙儿？"

"沃纳百货社交郊游会的会员们。"

"可这是笔墨俱乐部的舞会，"普劳特先生哀鸣。

"弄错了，"对方笃定地说。"是哪儿的岔子。这样，"他招手唤过一位正匆匆走过的中年胖绅士，"你最好同我们的荣誉秘书谈谈。他清楚。比格斯先生，这位先生好像以为这场舞会弄错了什么。"

比格斯先生站定，看了看，听了听。近看，他有一股斩钉截铁的气概。我中意他的相貌。

"容我介绍查尔顿·普劳特先生，"我说。"《灰色的桃金娘》的作者。普劳特先生，"我看这一介绍没激起半点涟漪，便接着说，"是笔墨俱乐部的秘书。"

"我是沃纳百货社交郊游会的秘书，"比格斯先生说。

两位秘书警觉地对峙，像两条狗。

"可你们来这儿干什么？"普劳特先生哀叹，声如林间之风。"这是私人舞会。"

"私人什么，"比格斯先生斩钉截铁。"票是我亲自给全体会员买的。"

"可根本没有售票。这舞会是专——"

===Original===

“It’s perfectly evident that you have come to the wrong hall or chosen the wrong evening,” snapped Miss Ukridge, abruptly superseding Mr. Prout in the supreme command. I did not blame her for feeling a little impatient. The secretary was handling the campaign very feebly.

The man behind the Warner’s Stores Social and Outing Club cocked a polite but belligerent eye at this new enemy. I liked his looks more than ever. This was a man who would fight it out on these lines if it took all the summer.

“I have not the honour of this lady’s acquaintance,” he said, smoothly, but with a gradually reddening eye. The Biggses, that eye seemed to say, were loath to war upon women, but if the women asked for it they could be men of iron, ruthless. “Might I ask who this lady is?”

“This is our president.”

“Happy to meet you, ma’am.”

“Miss Ukridge,” added Mr. Prout, completing the introduction.

The name appeared to strike a chord in Mr. Biggs. He bent forward and a gleam of triumph came into his eyes.

“Ukridge, did you say?”

“Miss Julia Ukridge.”

“Then it’s all right,” said Mr. Biggs, briskly. “There’s been no mistake. I bought our tickets from a gentleman named Ukridge. I got seven hundred at five bob apiece, reduction for taking a quantity and ten percent discount for cash. If Mr. Ukridge acted contrary to instructions, it’s too late to remedy the matter now. You should have made it clear to him what you wanted him to do before he went and did it.”

And with this extremely sound sentiment the honorary secretary of the Warner’s Stores Social and Outing Club turned on the heel of his shining dancing-pump and was gone. And I, too, sauntered away. There seemed nothing to keep me. As I went, I looked over my shoulder. The author of *Grey Myrtles* appeared to be entering upon the opening stages of what promised to be a painful tête-à-tête. My heart bled for him. If ever a man was blameless Mr. Prout was, but the president of the Pen and Ink Club was not the woman to allow a trifle like that to stand in her way.

===Chinese===

"显而易见，你们要么走错了厅，要么挑错了日子，"厄克里奇小姐厉声道，突然褫夺了普劳特先生的最高指挥权。我不怪她有点不耐。秘书这场仗打得实在窝囊。

沃纳百货社交郊游会背后那位汉子，朝这个新敌人挑衅又客气地斜起一只眼。我越发中意他的相貌了。这是个准备"就照这个打法耗下去，哪怕耗上一个夏天"的人。

"本人无缘结识这位夫人，"他从容地说，眼睛却渐渐红了。那眼神仿佛在说：比格斯家的人不愿对妇人开战，可妇人若逼上门来，他们也能做铁人，毫不留情。"可否请教这位夫人是？"

"这是我们的会长。"

"幸会，夫人。"

"厄克里奇小姐，"普劳特先生补完了介绍。

这名字似乎在比格斯先生心里拨响了一根弦。他探身向前，眼里燃起一线得意。

"厄克里奇，您说？"

"朱莉娅·厄克里奇小姐。"

"那就对了，"比格斯先生干脆地说。"没有弄错。我们的票，是跟一位叫厄克里奇的先生买的。七百张，五先令一张，批量另算优惠，现付再打九折。要是厄克里奇先生办事逾了矩，如今也没法补救了。你们本该在他动手之前，把要他办的事交代清楚。"

说完这句极其中肯的话，沃纳百货社交郊游会的荣誉秘书以他那只锃亮的舞鞋跟一转，去了。我也信步走开。没什么留我的了。走时我回头望了一眼。《灰色的桃金娘》的作者看来正进入一场前途堪忧的促膝谈心的开场阶段。我为他心口流血。要说无辜，没有谁比普劳特先生更无辜的了，可笔墨俱乐部的会长不是那种肯让这点小事挡道的女人。

===Original===

“Oh, it just came to me, laddie,” said Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge modestly, interviewed later by our representative. “You know me. One moment mind a blank, then﻿—*bing!*﻿—some dashed colossal idea. It was your showing me that ticket for the dance that set me thinking. And I happened to meet a bloke in a pub who worked in Warner’s Stores. Nice fellow, with a fair amount of pimples. Told me their Social and Outing Club was working up for its semiannual beano. One thing led to another, I got him to introduce me to the hon. sec., and we came to terms. I liked the man, laddie. Great treat to meet a bloke with a good, level business head. We settled the details in no time. Well, I don’t mind telling you, Corky my boy, that at last for the first time in many years I begin to see my way clear. I’ve got a bit of capital now. After sending poor little Dora her hundred, I shall have at least fifty quid left over. Fifty quid! My dear old son, you may take it from me that there’s no limit﻿—absolutely no limit﻿—to what I can accomplish with fifty o’goblins in my kick. From now on I see my way clear. My feet are on solid ground. The world, laddie, is my oyster. Nothing can stop me from making a colossal fortune. I’m not exaggerating, old horse﻿—a colossal fortune. Why, by a year from now I calculate, at a conservative estimate﻿—”

Our representative then withdrew.

===Chinese===

"哦，就是这么灵光一闪，小家伙，"斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇谦逊地对我们记者说。"你了解我。前一刻脑子里还一片空白，接着——*嘣*！——来了个顶天立地的大主意。正是你给我看的那张舞会票，点了我的思路。恰好我又在一家酒馆碰上个在沃纳百货当差的伙计。挺好的人，一脸粉刺。他说他们社交郊游会正筹备半年一度的联欢。顺藤摸瓜，我让他引见他们的荣誉秘书，谈成了。我喜欢那人，小家伙。碰上一个头脑清醒的生意人，是桩快事。细节三下五除二就敲定了。呃，不妨告诉你，科基老伙计，多年来我头一遭开始看清脚下的路了。我如今有本钱了。给可怜的小多拉寄去那一百之后，至少还能剩下五十镑。五十镑！亲爱的老伙计，你只管信我的：裤兜里揣上五十枚金镑，我干成的事没有止境——绝对没有止境。从今往后我看得清清楚楚。我脚踩实地了。世界，小家伙，是我的牡蛎。什么也拦不住我发一笔顶天的横财。我不夸张，老伙计——顶天的横财。嗐，不出一年，我保守估计——"

本报特派记者就此撤了。

## Ukridge’s Accident Syndicate / 厄克里奇的意外辛迪加
===Original===

“Half a minute, laddie,” said Ukridge. And, gripping my arm, he brought me to a halt on the outskirts of the little crowd which had collected about the church door.

It was a crowd such as may be seen any morning during the London mating-season outside any of the churches which nestle in the quiet squares between Hyde Park and the King’s Road, Chelsea.

It consisted of five women of cooklike aspect, four nursemaids, half a dozen men of the non-producing class who had torn themselves away for the moment from their normal task of propping up the wall of the Bunch of Grapes public house on the corner, a costermonger with a barrow of vegetables, divers small boys, eleven dogs, and two or three purposeful-looking young fellows with cameras slung over their shoulders. It was plain that a wedding was in progress﻿—and, arguing from the presence of the cameramen and the line of smart motorcars along the kerb, a fairly fashionable wedding. What was not plain﻿—to me﻿—was why Ukridge, sternest of bachelors, had desired to add himself to the spectators.

“What,” I enquired, “is the thought behind this? Why are we interrupting our walk to attend the obsequies of some perfect stranger?”

Ukridge did not reply for a moment. He seemed plunged in thought. Then he uttered a hollow, mirthless laugh﻿—a dreadful sound like the last gargle of a dying moose.

“Perfect stranger, my number eleven foot!” he responded, in his coarse way. “Do you know who it is who’s getting hitched up in there?”

“Who?”

“Teddy Weeks.”

“Teddy Weeks? Teddy Weeks? Good Lord!” I exclaimed. “Not really?”

And five years rolled away.

===Chinese===

"等半分钟，小家伙，"厄克里奇说。说着他攥住我的胳膊，让我在教堂门口聚起的那小堆人外围停下了脚。

那是伦敦婚配季节里，任何一个上午，在海德公园与切尔儿国王路之间那些幽静小广场里依偎着的任何一座教堂门外，都可能见到的一群人。

人群构成如下：五名厨娘模样的妇人，四个保姆，六七个不事生产阶级的男子——他们暂时从本职工作（在街角"葡萄串"酒馆的墙上撑着）里挣脱出来——一个推着菜车的菜贩，若干幼童，十一条狗，还有两三个肩挎照相机、来意分明的年轻人。显然一场婚礼正在进行——而且，从摄影师在场、以及路缘一溜时髦汽车来看，还是一场相当体面的婚礼。不明——对我不明的——是，厄克里奇，这单身汉里最铁面的一位，何以要把自己加进看客之列。

"这一出背后，"我问，"是什么章法？咱们干吗中断散步，来观一个八竿子打不着的人的典礼？"

厄克里奇半晌没答。他似乎陷入了沉思。随后他发出一声空洞的无笑之笑——一种可怕的声音，像垂死驼鹿的最后一记漱嗓。

"八竿子打不着，我的十一号脚！"他以他那粗鲁的方式回敬。"你知道在里头拜堂的是谁吗？"

"谁？"

"特迪·威克斯（Teddy Weeks）。"

"特迪·威克斯？特迪·威克斯？老天！"我叫道。"不会吧？"

于是五年岁月滚滚倒流。

===Original===

It was at Barolini’s Italian restaurant in Beak Street that Ukridge evolved his great scheme. Barolini’s was a favourite resort of our little group of earnest strugglers in the days when the philanthropic restaurateurs of Soho used to supply four courses and coffee for a shilling and sixpence; and there were present that night, besides Ukridge and myself, the following men-about-town: Teddy Weeks, the actor, fresh from a six-weeks’ tour with the Number Three “Only a Shop-Girl” Company; Victor Beamish, the artist, the man who drew that picture of the O-So-Eesi Piano-Player in the advertisement pages of the *Piccadilly Magazine*; Bertram Fox, author of *Ashes of Remorse*, and other unproduced motion-picture scenarios; and Robert Dunhill, who, being employed at a salary of eighty pounds per annum by the New Asiatic Bank, represented the sober, hardheaded commercial element. As usual, Teddy Weeks had collared the conversation, and was telling us once again how good he was and how hardly treated by a malignant fate.

There is no need to describe Teddy Weeks. Under another and a more euphonious name he has long since made his personal appearance dreadfully familiar to all who read the illustrated weekly papers. He was then, as now, a sickeningly handsome young man, possessing precisely the same melting eyes, mobile mouth, and corrugated hair so esteemed by the theatre-going public today. And yet, at this period of his career he was wasting himself on minor touring companies of the kind which open at Barrow-in-Furness and jump to Bootle for the second half of the week. He attributed this, as Ukridge was so apt to attribute his own difficulties, to lack of capital.

===Chinese===

厄克里奇的那项伟大方案，是在比克街的巴罗利尼意大利餐馆里孕育成形的。那是我们这伙认真的挣扎者最爱去的据点——在那些日子里，索霍区的慈善餐馆主们还肯供一先令六便士的四道菜加咖啡。当晚在座除厄克里奇与我之外，还有以下几位闲人：演员特迪·威克斯，刚随三号"不过是个女店员"剧团跑完六个星期的码头；画家维克托·比米什，就是《皮卡迪利杂志》广告页上那幅"欧索易"自动钢琴画像的作者；伯特伦·福克斯，《悔恨之灰》及其他尚未投拍的电影剧本的作者；还有罗伯特·邓希尔，他以年薪八十镑受雇于新亚银行，代表冷静精明的商业元素。照例是特迪·威克斯霸住了话头，再一次向我们讲述他如何优秀，命运又如何歹毒地亏待他。

特迪·威克斯无需描写。换了另一个更悦耳的名字，他的尊容早已叫所有读画报周刊的人看熟看腻。他当年同如今一样，是个俊得叫人牙酸的美青年，恰恰长着今天戏迷们推崇的那双多情眼、那张灵活嘴、那头波浪发。然而在他演艺生涯的这个阶段，他还在二三路巡回剧团里浪费自己——那种在巴罗因弗内斯开锣、下半周跳到布特尔的班子。他把这归咎于缺本钱，正如厄克里奇动辄把自己的困境归咎于缺本钱一样。

===Original===

“I have everything,” he said, querulously, emphasising his remarks with a coffee-spoon. “Looks, talent, personality, a beautiful speaking-voice﻿—everything. All I need is a chance. And I can’t get that because I have no clothes fit to wear. These managers are all the same, they never look below the surface, they never bother to find out if a man has genius. All they go by are his clothes. If I could afford to buy a couple of suits from a Cork Street tailor, if I could have my boots made to order by Moykoff instead of getting them ready-made and secondhand at Moses Brothers’, if I could once contrive to own a decent hat, a really good pair of spats, and a gold cigarette-case, all at the same time, I could walk into any manager’s office in London and sign up for a West-end production tomorrow.”

It was at this point that Freddie Lunt came in. Freddie, like Robert Dunhill, was a financial magnate in the making and an assiduous frequenter of Barolini’s; and it suddenly occurred to us that a considerable time had passed since we had last seen him in the place. We enquired the reason for this aloofness.

“I’ve been in bed,” said Freddie, “for over a fortnight.”

The statement incurred Ukridge’s stern disapproval. That great man made a practice of never rising before noon, and on one occasion, when a carelessly-thrown match had burned a hole in his only pair of trousers, had gone so far as to remain between the sheets for forty-eight hours; but sloth on so majestic a scale as this shocked him.

“Lazy young devil,” he commented severely. “Letting the golden hours of youth slip by like that when you ought to have been bustling about and making a name for yourself.”

Freddie protested himself wronged by the imputation.

“I had an accident,” he explained. “Fell off my bicycle and sprained an ankle.”

“Tough luck,” was our verdict.

“Oh, I don’t know,” said Freddie. “It wasn’t bad fun getting a rest. And of course there was the fiver.”

“What fiver?”

“I got a fiver from the *Weekly Cyclist* for getting my ankle sprained.”

“You﻿—*what?*” cried Ukridge, profoundly stirred﻿—as ever﻿—by a tale of easy money. “Do you mean to sit there and tell me that some dashed paper paid you five quid simply because you sprained your ankle? Pull yourself together, old horse. Things like that don’t happen.”

“It’s quite true.”

“Can you show me the fiver?”

===Chinese===

"我样样都有，"他委屈地说，拿咖啡匙敲打论点。"相貌，才华，个性，一副漂亮的说话嗓音——样样都有。缺的只是机会。可我捞不着机会，因为我没有一身像样的衣裳。这些经理全一个德性，从不看皮囊底下，从不劳神查究一个人有没有天才。他们只认衣裳。我要是买得起科克街裁缝的两三套西装，要是我的靴子能向莫伊科夫定做，而不是在摩西兄弟店里淘现成的二手货，要是我能一鼓作气置办起一顶像样的帽子、一副真正上等的鞋罩、一只金烟盒，我明天就能走进伦敦随便哪家经理办公室，签下西区制作的合同。"

正在此时，弗雷迪·伦特进来了。弗雷迪同罗伯特·邓希尔一样，是一位成长中的金融巨子，也是巴罗利尼的勤勉常客；我们忽然想起，已有好一段日子没在这地方见他了。我们询问这份疏远的缘故。

"我在床上躺着呢，"弗雷迪说，"躺了半个多月。"

这一陈述招致厄克里奇的严厉不满。那位伟人奉行正午之前绝不起床的主义，还曾在一根随手丢的火柴烧穿他仅有的一条裤子时，索性在被窝里足足待了四十八小时；但如此规模的怠惰仍然震骇了他。

"懒惰的小魔鬼，"他严词训斥。"青春的金色时辰就这么放走，你本该四处奔忙、挣个名头的。"

弗雷迪抗议这罪名冤枉了他。

"我出了意外，"他解释。"从自行车上摔下来，崴了脚踝。"

"真倒霉，"这是我们的裁决。

"哦，也难说，"弗雷迪说。"歇着倒也不坏玩。再说还有那五镑呢。"

"什么五镑？"

"《每周骑车人》为我这脚踝，付了五镑。"

"你——*什么？*"厄克里奇喊了起来，一闻快钱则喜，历来如此。"你是要坐在那儿告诉我，有份要命的报纸，就为你崴了脚踝，付了你五镑？振作点，老伙计。没这种事。"

"千真万确。"

"能把那五镑拿给我看看吗？"

===Original===

“No; because if I did you would try to borrow it.”

Ukridge ignored this slur in dignified silence.

“Would they pay a fiver to *anyone* who sprained his ankle?” he asked, sticking to the main point.

“Yes. If he was a subscriber.”

“I knew there was a catch in it,” said Ukridge, moodily.

“Lots of weekly papers are starting this wheeze,” proceeded Freddie. “You pay a year’s subscription and that entitles you to accident insurance.”

We were interested. This was in the days before every daily paper in London was competing madly against its rivals in the matter of insurance and offering princely bribes to the citizens to make a fortune by breaking their necks. Nowadays papers are paying as high as two thousand pounds for a genuine corpse and five pounds a week for a mere dislocated spine; but at that time the idea was new and it had an attractive appeal.

“How many of these rags are doing this?” asked Ukridge. You could tell from the gleam in his eyes that that great brain was whirring like a dynamo.

“As many as ten?”

“Yes, I should think so. Quite ten.”

“Then a fellow who subscribed to them all and then sprained his ankle would get fifty quid?” said Ukridge, reasoning acutely.

“More if the injury was more serious,” said Freddie, the expert. “They have a regular tariff. So much for a broken arm, so much for a broken leg, and so forth.”

Ukridge’s collar leaped off its stud and his pince-nez wobbled drunkenly as he turned to us.

“How much money can you blokes raise?” he demanded.

“What do you want it for?” asked Robert Dunhill, with a banker’s caution.

“My dear old horse, can’t you see? Why, my gosh, I’ve got the idea of the century. Upon my Sam, this is the giltest-edged scheme that was ever hatched. We’ll get together enough money and take out a year’s subscription for every one of these dashed papers.”

“What’s the good of that?” said Dunhill, coldly unenthusiastic.

They train bank clerks to stifle emotion, so that they will be able to refuse overdrafts when they become managers. “The odds are we should none of us have an accident of any kind, and then the money would be chucked away.”

===Chinese===

"不能；一给你看你准要借。"

厄克里奇对这污蔑报以庄严的沉默。

"随便谁崴了脚踝，他们照样付五镑？"他问，紧咬主旨不放。

"是。只要是订户。"

"我早知道有猫腻，"厄克里奇怏怏地说。

"好多周刊都在起这个新鲜招，"弗雷迪接着说。"你缴一年订费，就享意外保险。"

我们都来了兴趣。那是在伦敦各家日报还没疯狂互拼保险、还没悬出帝王级的赏格、引诱市民们靠摔断脖子发财的年代。如今，报纸买一具货真价实的尸体肯出到两千镑，单一条脱臼的脊椎每周五镑；可在当时，这主意还新鲜，颇有吸引力。

"搞这种把戏的破报，一共多少家？"厄克里奇问。看他眼里那道光就知道，那颗伟大的脑袋正像发电机似的嗡嗡飞转。

"总有个十家吧？"

"有，我看有。足有十家。"

"那么，谁要全订了，再崴个脚踝，就能进账五十镑？"厄克里奇敏锐地推理。

"伤得再重些，给的更多，"专家弗雷迪说。"人家有正经价目表。断一条胳膊多少钱，断一条腿多少钱，等等。"

厄克里奇的领子从领扣上蹦了起来，他转向我们，夹鼻眼镜醉汉似的乱晃。

"你们这帮家伙能凑多少钱？"他问。

"你要钱干什么？"罗伯特·邓希尔以银行家的审慎发问。

"我亲爱的老伙计，你还看不出来？哎呀我的天，我得到了本世纪的主意。我向山姆起誓，这是有史以来孵出的最金边的一项方案。咱们凑一笔钱，把这些要命的报纸挨家订它一年。"

"那有什么用？"邓希尔说，冷静得毫不热心。银行就是训练行员扼杀感情的，好让他们当了经理之后敢于拒绝透支。"多半咱们谁也遇不上一星半点意外，那钱就白扔了。"

===Original===

“Good heavens, ass,” snorted Ukridge, “you don’t suppose I’m suggesting that we should leave it to chance, do you? Listen! Here’s the scheme. We take out subscriptions for all these papers, then we draw lots, and the fellow who gets the fatal card or whatever it is goes out and breaks his leg and draws the loot, and we split it up between us and live on it in luxury. It ought to run into hundreds of pounds.”

A long silence followed. Then Dunhill spoke again. His was a solid rather than a nimble mind.

“Suppose he couldn’t break his leg?”

“My gosh!” cried Ukridge, exasperated. “Here we are in the twentieth century, with every resource of modern civilisation at our disposal, with opportunities for getting our legs broken opening about us on every side﻿—and you ask a silly question like that! Of course he could break his leg. Any ass can break a leg. It’s a little hard! We’re all infernally broke﻿—personally, unless Freddie can lend me a bit of that fiver till Saturday, I’m going to have a difficult job pulling through. We all need money like the dickens, and yet, when I point out this marvellous scheme for collecting a bit, instead of fawning on me for my ready intelligence you sit and make objections. It isn’t the right spirit. It isn’t the spirit that wins.”

“If you’re as hard up as that,” objected Dunhill, “how are you going to put in your share of the pool?”

A pained, almost a stunned, look came into Ukridge’s eyes. He gazed at Dunhill through his lopsided pince-nez as one who speculates as to whether his hearing has deceived him.

“Me?” he cried. “Me? I like that! Upon my Sam, that’s rich! Why, damme, if there’s any justice in the world, if there’s a spark of decency and good feeling in your bally bosoms, I should think you would let me in free for suggesting the idea. It’s a little hard! I supply the brains and you want me to cough up cash as well. My gosh, I didn’t expect this. This hurts me, by George! If anybody had told me that an old pal would﻿—”

“Oh, all right,” said Robert Dunhill. “All right, all right, all right. But I’ll tell you one thing. If you draw the lot it’ll be the happiest day of my life.”

“I shan’t,” said Ukridge. “Something tells me that I shan’t.”

===Chinese===

"老天爷，蠢驴，"厄克里奇嗤道，"你不会以为我主张听天由命吧？听着！方案是这样。咱们把这些报全订上，然后抽签，抽中死签的那位，管它什么签，就出门去断一条腿，把赏金领回来，大家均分，靠它过阔日子。算下来该有几百镑。"

长久的沉默。随后邓希尔又发言了。他的脑子是扎实的，不是灵巧的。

"万一他断不了腿呢？"

"我的天！"厄克里奇光火地喊。"咱们都活在二十世纪了，现代文明的一切资源任凭咱们调用，断腿的机会在四面八方朝咱们敞开——你倒问出这种蠢问题！他当然断得了腿。随便哪头蠢驴都断得了腿。这也叫难！咱们全都穷得冒烟——单说我，除非弗雷迪肯把那五镑借我一点撑到星期六，我这两天真不好过。咱们全都急疯了似的缺钱，可我一指出这条收钱的美妙方案，你们不巴巴地捧我的聪明，反倒坐着挑刺。这不是应有的精神。不是取胜的精神。"

"你既然这么紧，"邓希尔驳道，"你那份股金又从哪儿出？"

一种痛心的、简直震愣的神色进入厄克里奇的眼睛。他隔着那副歪斜的夹鼻眼镜凝视邓希尔，像一个人疑心自己的耳朵出了错。

"我？"他喊。"我？亏你说得出口！我向山姆起誓，真有你的！嗐，见鬼，这世上要还有半点公道，你们的胸膛里要还剩一星半点的体面与好心，光凭这主意是我想出来的，你们就该免收我的股金！这也叫难！我贡献的是脑子，你们还要我另掏现钱！我的天，我可没想到这一层。这真伤了我的心，千真万确！谁要是早告诉我，一个老哥们儿会——"

"行了行了，"罗伯特·邓希尔说。"行了，行了。不过有一句话我得说：要是抽签抽中的是你，那就是我这辈子最快活的一天。"

"抽不中我，"厄克里奇说。"我有预感，抽不中我。"

===Original===

Nor did he. When, in a solemn silence broken only by the sound of a distant waiter quarrelling with the cook down a speaking-tube, we had completed the drawing, the man of destiny was Teddy Weeks.

I suppose that even in the springtime of Youth, when broken limbs seems a lighter matter than they become later in life, it can never be an unmixedly agreeable thing to have to go out into the public highways and try to make an accident happen to one. In such circumstances the reflection that you are thereby benefiting your friends brings but slight balm. To Teddy Weeks it appeared to bring no balm at all. That he was experiencing a certain disinclination to sacrifice himself for the public good became more and more evident as the days went by and found him still intact. Ukridge, when he called upon me to discuss the matter, was visibly perturbed. He sank into a chair beside the table at which I was beginning my modest morning meal, and, having drunk half my coffee, sighed deeply.

“Upon my Sam,” he moaned, “it’s a little disheartening. I strain my brain to think up schemes for getting us all a bit of money just at the moment when we are all needing it most, and when I hit on what is probably the simplest and yet ripest notion of our time, this blighter Weeks goes and lets me down by shirking his plain duty. It’s just my luck that a fellow like that should have drawn the lot. And the worst of it is, laddie, that, now we’ve started with him, we’ve got to keep on. We can’t possibly raise enough money to pay yearly subscriptions for anybody else. It’s Weeks or nobody.”

“I suppose we must give him time.”

===Chinese===

果然没中。当我们在一片庄严的静默中——只有远处一个侍者对着传声管同厨师吵架的声音打破它——完成了抽签，天命之人原来是特迪·威克斯。

我想，纵然在青春的春天里，断胳膊断腿显得比日后轻松，被派到公路上去给自己制造一起意外，也终究不是件全然愉快的事。这种时候，"此举可以造福朋友"的念头带来的安慰甚微。对特迪·威克斯，它似乎压根没带来安慰。日子一天天过去而他完好无损，他为公众利益牺牲自我的兴致缺缺，这一点越来越明显。厄克里奇为这事跑来找我商量时，显然心绪不宁。他一屁股坐在我桌旁的椅子上——我正开始我那份清简的早餐——喝掉我半杯咖啡，深深叹了口气。

"我向山姆起誓，"他呻吟道，"这有点叫人寒心。正当咱们大伙最缺钱的时候，我绞尽脑汁想给大伙弄几个钱，刚想出这个没准儿是咱们这时代最简单又最成熟的点子，威克斯这个家伙就用逃避天职来涮我。偏偏这么个家伙抽中了签，这就是我的命。更糟的是，小家伙，既然开头用的是他，就只能一条道走到黑。咱们再也凑不出第二个全年订费了。要收获，只有威克斯。"

"我想，总得给他点时间。"

===Original===

“That’s what he says,” grunted Ukridge, morosely, helping himself to toast. “He says he doesn’t know how to start about it. To listen to him, you’d think that going and having a trifling accident was the sort of delicate and intricate job that required years of study and special preparation. Why, a child of six could do it on his head at five minutes’ notice. The man’s so infernally particular. You make helpful suggestions, and instead of accepting them in a broad, reasonable spirit of cooperation he comes back at you every time with some frivolous objection. He’s so dashed fastidious. When we were out last night, we came on a couple of navvies scrapping. Good hefty fellows, either of them capable of putting him in hospital for a month. I told him to jump in and start separating them, and he said no; it was a private dispute which was none of his business, and he didn’t feel justified in interfering. Finicky, I call it. I tell you, laddie, this blighter is a broken reed. He has got cold feet. We did wrong to let him into the drawing at all. We might have known that a fellow like that would never give results. No conscience. No sense of esprit de corps. No notion of putting himself out to the most trifling extent for the benefit of the community. Haven’t you any more marmalade, laddie?”

“I have not.”

“Then I’ll be going,” said Ukridge, moodily. “I suppose,” he added, pausing at the door, “you couldn’t lend me five bob?”

“How did you guess?”

“Then I’ll tell you what,” said Ukridge, ever fair and reasonable; “you can stand me dinner tonight.” He seemed cheered up for the moment by this happy compromise, but gloom descended on him again. His face clouded. “When I think,” he said, “of all the money that’s locked up in that poor fainthearted fish, just waiting to be released, I could sob. Sob, laddie, like a little child. I never liked that man﻿—he has a bad eye and waves his hair. Never trust a man who waves his hair, old horse.”

===Chinese===

"他就是这么说的，"厄克里奇悻悻地哼道，自己动手拿了吐司。"他说他不知从何下手。听他那口气，你准以为出趟门出个小小的意外，是那种得钻研多年、专门筹备的精细活儿。嗐，六岁娃娃接了通知五分钟都能倒立着办成。这人讲究得要死。你好心提建议，他不但不以宽广合作的精神纳谏，反倒每回都拿些不三不四的理由顶回来。他讲究得出奇。昨晚咱们俩出门，撞见两个挖土工斗殴。都是结实的壮汉，随便哪个都够送他进医院躺一个月。我叫他跳进去劝架，他说不；那是人家私人的争执，与他无关，他无权干预。我说这叫矫情。我告诉你，小家伙，这家伙是根靠不住的芦苇。他馁了。当初就不该让他进抽签。早该料到这种人不出活。没良心。没集体荣誉感。不肯为团体的利益出半点小力。你这儿没有别的果酱了吧，小家伙？"

"没有。"

"那我就走了，"厄克里奇郁郁地说。走到门口他又停住："我猜，"他补一句，"你总不至于借我五先令？"

"你怎么猜到的？"

"那么这样，"一贯公道讲理的厄克里奇说，"今晚你请我吃顿饭。"这一愉快的折衷似乎叫他振奋了一阵，随即阴云又落下来。他脸色一沉。"一想到，"他说，"那么多钱锁在那条怯懦的鱼肚子里，就等着一放出来，我简直要哭。哭，小家伙，像个小孩子似的。我从来看不上那人——他眼神不正，还老做头发。老伙计，做头发的男人，永远别信。"

===Original===

Ukridge’s pessimism was not confined to himself. By the end of a fortnight, nothing having happened to Teddy Weeks worse than a slight cold which he shook off in a couple of days, the general consensus of opinion among his apprehensive colleagues in the Syndicate was that the situation had become desperate. There were no signs whatever of any return on the vast capital which we had laid out, and meanwhile meals had to be bought, landladies paid, and a reasonable supply of tobacco acquired. It was a melancholy task in these circumstances to read one’s paper of a morning.

All over the inhabited globe, so the well-informed sheet gave one to understand, every kind of accident was happening every day to practically everybody in existence except Teddy Weeks. Farmers in Minnesota were getting mixed up with reaping-machines, peasants in India were being bisected by crocodiles; iron girders from skyscrapers were falling hourly on the heads of citizens in every town from Philadelphia to San Francisco; and the only people who were not down with ptomaine poisoning were those who had walked over cliffs, driven motors into walls, tripped over manholes, or assumed on too slight evidence that the gun was not loaded. In a crippled world, it seemed, Teddy Weeks walked alone, whole and glowing with health. It was one of those grim, ironical, hopeless, grey, despairful situations which the Russian novelists love to write about, and I could not find it in me to blame Ukridge for taking direct action in this crisis. My only regret was that bad luck caused so excellent a plan to miscarry.

My first intimation that he had been trying to hurry matters on came when he and I were walking along the King’s Road one evening, and he drew me into Markham Square, a dismal backwater where he had once had rooms.

“What’s the idea?” I asked, for I disliked the place.

“Teddy Weeks lives here,” said Ukridge. “In my old rooms.” I could not see that this lent any fascination to the place. Every day and in every way I was feeling sorrier and sorrier that I had been foolish enough to put money which I could ill spare into a venture which had all the earmarks of a washout, and my sentiments towards Teddy Weeks were cold and hostile.

“I want to enquire after him.”

“Enquire after him? Why?”

“Well, the fact is, laddie, I have an idea that he has been bitten by a dog.”

===Chinese===

悲观的并不止厄克里奇一人。到两个星期末尾，特迪·威克斯除了一场两天就好的轻感冒之外毫发无伤，辛迪加里忧心如焚的同仁们一致认为局势已臻绝望。我们投入的巨额资本毫无回收迹象，而饭总得吃，房东太太总得付，像样的烟草储备总得置办。在这样的境况下，每天早晨读报是一桩辛酸的苦役。

据那份消息灵通的报纸转达：在有人居住的全球各地，各式意外每天都在降临到几乎所有人的头上——除了特迪·威克斯。明尼苏达的农夫正卷进收割机，印度的庄稼汉正被鳄鱼拦腰咬断；从费城到旧金山，每个城镇里摩天楼的钢梁时时刻刻砸在市民头上；还没得尸碱中毒的，都是那些从悬崖上走下去了的、把汽车开进墙里的、让人行道检修口绊了的，以及仅凭想当然就认定枪里没弹药的。在这遍体鳞伤的世界上，看来只有特迪·威克斯独自一人囫囵而健康地走着。这是俄国小说家最爱写的那类严酷、讽刺、无望、灰色、绝望的处境，因此厄克里奇在这危机中采取直接行动，我实在无法责怪他。我惟一的遗憾是，坏运气叫这么完美的一项计划失了手。

他试图催办的头一个讯息，是我和他一天傍晚沿国王路散步时得到的：他把我拽进了马卡姆广场，一处他曾租过房的死水般的地方。

"什么意思？"我问，因为我厌恶那地方。

"特迪·威克斯住这儿，"厄克里奇说。"就住我从前的屋子。"我瞧不出这给此地添了什么魅力。一天天过去，我越来越懊悔自己一时糊涂，把本就拮据的钱投进了这个从头到尾透着赔本相的营生，我对特迪·威克斯的感情又冷又冲。

"我想去问候问候他。"

"问候他？为什么？"

"呃，实情是，小家伙，我有个感觉，他叫狗咬了。"

===Original===

“What makes you think that?”

“Oh, I don’t know,” said Ukridge, dreamily. “I’ve just got the idea. You know how one gets ideas.”

The mere contemplation of this beautiful event was so inspiring that for awhile it held me silent. In each of the ten journals in which we had invested dog-bites were specifically recommended as things which every subscriber ought to have. They came about halfway up the list of lucrative accidents, inferior to a broken rib or a fractured fibula, but better value than an ingrowing toenail. I was gloating happily over the picture conjured up by Ukridge’s words when an exclamation brought me back with a start to the realities of life. A revolting sight met my eyes. Down the street came ambling the familiar figure of Teddy Weeks, and one glance at his elegant person was enough to tell us that our hopes had been built on sand. Not even a toy Pomeranian had chewed this man.

“Hallo, you fellows!” said Teddy Weeks.

“Hallo!” we responded, dully.

“Can’t stop,” said Teddy Weeks. “I’ve got to fetch a doctor.”

“A doctor?”

“Yes. Poor Victor Beamish. He’s been bitten by a dog.”

Ukridge and I exchanged weary glances. It seemed as if Fate was going out of its way to have sport with us. What was the good of a dog biting Victor Beamish? What was the good of a hundred dogs biting Victor Beamish? A dog-bitten Victor Beamish had no market value whatever.

“You know that fierce brute that belongs to my landlady,” said Teddy Weeks. “The one that always dashes out into the area and barks at people who come to the front door.” I remembered. A large mongrel with wild eyes and flashing fangs, badly in need of a haircut. I had encountered it once in the street, when visiting Ukridge, and only the presence of the latter, who knew it well and to whom all dogs were as brothers, had saved me from the doom of Victor Beamish. “Somehow or other he got into my bedroom this evening. He was waiting there when I came home. I had brought Beamish back with me, and the animal pinned him by the leg the moment I opened the door.”

“Why didn’t he pin you?” asked Ukridge, aggrieved.

“What I can’t make out,” said Teddy Weeks, “is how on earth the brute came to be in my room. Somebody must have put him there. The whole thing is very mysterious.”

“Why didn’t he pin you?” demanded Ukridge again.

===Chinese===

"你凭什么这么觉得？"

"哦，说不清，"厄克里奇悠悠地说。"我就是有这个感觉。你知道，人有时候就是会有感觉。"

单是默想这桩美事就如此醉人，有一阵子它让我说不出话。咱们投了资的那十家刊物，家家都白纸黑字推荐狗咬，说这是每个订户必备之物。在生财意外名录上它排在中游，比不上断一根肋骨或腓骨骨折，却比嵌甲划算。我正对着厄克里奇的措辞唤起的图景陶陶然，一声惊呼把我吓得回到了人生的现实。一幅倒胃口的景象撞进我眼里。街那头，特迪·威克斯那熟悉的身影正信步而来，他那风雅的身段只消一瞥，便足以告诉我们，我们的希望筑在沙上。连一只玩具博美都没啃过这人。

"喂，你们俩！"特迪·威克斯说。

"喂！"我们有气无力地应答。

"不能多待，"特迪·威克斯说。"我得去请大夫。"

"大夫？"

"是。可怜的维克托·比米什。他叫狗咬了。"

厄克里奇和我交换了一个疲惫的眼神。命运似乎存心拿我们寻开心。狗咬维克托·比米什有什么用？一百条狗咬维克托·比米什又有什么用？被狗咬过的维克托·比米什没有半点市场价值。

"你知道我房东太太那只凶货，"特迪·威克斯说。"老往地下室口冲、朝走前门的人狂吠的那只。"我记得。一只大杂种狗，眼睛狂野，獠牙闪闪，身上急需理发。有一回我来看厄克里奇，在街上撞见过它，多亏后者在场——他跟它熟，所有的狗在他眼里都是兄弟——我才免遭维克托·比米什的命运。"不知怎么，它今晚钻进了我的卧室。我回家它正等在那儿。我带比米什回来一起，我一开门，那畜生就咬住他的腿了。"

"它怎么不咬你？"厄克里奇愤愤地问。

"我想不明白的，"特迪·威克斯说，"是那畜生究竟怎么进的屋。准是谁把它搁进去的。整件事神秘得很。"

"它怎么不咬你？"厄克里奇又追问。

===Original===

“Oh, I managed to climb on to the top of the wardrobe while he was biting Beamish,” said Teddy Weeks. “And then the landlady came and took him away. But I can’t stop here talking. I must go and get that doctor.”

We gazed after him in silence as he tripped down the street. We noted the careful manner in which he paused at the corner to eye the traffic before crossing the road, the wary way in which he drew back to allow a truck to rattle past.

“You heard that?” said Ukridge, tensely. “He climbed on to the top of the wardrobe!”

“Yes.”

“And you saw the way he dodged that excellent truck?”

“Yes.”

“Something’s got to be done,” said Ukridge, firmly.

“The man has got to be awakened to a sense of his responsibilities.”

Next day a deputation waited on Teddy Weeks.

Ukridge was our spokesman, and he came to the point with admirable directness.

“How about it?” asked Ukridge.

“How about what?” replied Teddy Weeks, nervously, avoiding his accusing eye.

“When do we get action?”

“Oh, you mean that accident business?”

“Yes.”

“I’ve been thinking about that,” said Teddy Weeks.

Ukridge drew the mackintosh which he wore indoors and out of doors and in all weathers more closely around him. There was in the action something suggestive of a member of the Roman Senate about to denounce an enemy of the State. In just such a manner must Cicero have swished his toga as he took a deep breath preparatory to assailing Clodius. He toyed for a moment with the ginger-beer wire which held his pince-nez in place, and endeavoured without success to button his collar at the back. In moments of emotion Ukridge’s collar always took on a sort of temperamental jumpiness which no stud could restrain.

“And about time you *were* thinking about it,” he boomed, sternly.

===Chinese===

"哦，它咬比米什的时候，我趁机爬上衣柜顶了，"特迪·威克斯说。"后来房东太太来把它牵走了。可我不能站着聊了。我得去请大夫。"

我们默默目送他碎步远去。我们注意到他在街角过马路前如何细细打量车流，如何警觉地退后一步让一辆卡车轰隆驶过。

"听见没有？"厄克里奇紧张地说。"他爬上了衣柜顶！"

"听见了。"

"他躲那辆好卡车的身手也看见了？"

"看见了。"

"得采取行动，"厄克里奇坚决地说。"必须唤醒这个人，让他认识自己的责任。"

第二天，一个代表团造访了特迪·威克斯。

厄克里奇是我们的发言人，他以可敬的开门见山直奔要害。

"怎么样？"厄克里奇问。

"什么怎么样？"特迪·威克斯答道，紧张地避开他那控诉的目光。

"什么时候行动？"

"哦，你说意外那档子事？"

"是。"

"我最近一直在琢磨，"特迪·威克斯说。

厄克里奇把那件他室内室外风雨无阻都穿的胶布雨衣往身上拢了拢。这动作里有点罗马元老即将痛斥国敌的意味。西塞罗深吸一口气准备痛击克洛狄乌斯之前，必也是这样一甩托加的。他摆弄了一阵拴夹鼻眼镜的姜汁汽水铁丝，又徒劳地试图把领子在后颈扣上。厄克里奇一激动，领子总要犯一种任何领扣都镇不住的急性抽搐。

"你也该琢磨琢磨了，"他严肃地轰隆道。

===Original===

We shifted appreciatively in our seats, all except Victor Beamish, who had declined a chair and was standing by the mantelpiece. “Upon my Sam, it’s about time you were thinking about it. Do you realise that we’ve invested an enormous sum of money in you on the distinct understanding that we could rely on you to do your duty and get immediate results? Are we to be forced to the conclusion that you are so yellow and few in the pod as to want to evade your honourable obligations? We thought better of you, Weeks. Upon my Sam, we thought better of you. We took you for a two-fisted, enterprising, big-souled, one hundred-percent he-man who would stand by his friends to the finish.”

“Yes, but﻿—”

“Any bloke with a sense of loyalty and an appreciation of what it meant to the rest of us would have rushed out and found some means of fulfilling his duty long ago. You don’t even grasp at the opportunities that come your way. Only yesterday I saw you draw back when a single step into the road would have had a truck bumping into you.”

“Well, it’s not so easy to let a truck bump into you.”

“Nonsense. It only requires a little ordinary resolution. Use your imagination, man. Try to think that a child has fallen down in the street﻿—a little golden-haired child,” said Ukridge, deeply affected. “And a dashed great cab or something comes rolling up. The kid’s mother is standing on the pavement, helpless, her hands clasped in agony. ‘Dammit,’ she cries, ‘will no one save my darling?’ ‘Yes, by George,’ you shout, ‘*I* will.’ And out you jump and the thing’s over in half a second. I don’t know what you’re making such a fuss about.”

“Yes, but﻿—” said Teddy Weeks.

“I’m told, what’s more, it isn’t a bit painful. A sort of dull shock, that’s all.”

“Who told you that?”

“I forget. Someone.”

“Well, you can tell him from me that he’s an ass,” said Teddy Weeks, with asperity.

“All right. If you object to being run over by a truck there are lots of other ways. But, upon my Sam, it’s pretty hopeless suggesting them. You seem to have no enterprise at all. Yesterday, after I went to all the trouble to put a dog in your room, a dog which would have done all the work for you﻿—all that you had to do was stand still and let him use his own judgment﻿—what happened? You climbed on to﻿—”

Victor Beamish interrupted, speaking in a voice husky with emotion.

===Chinese===

我们在座位上欣慰地挪了挪身子，只有维克托·比米什除外——他谢绝了椅子，正立在壁炉架旁。"我向山姆起誓，你是该琢磨琢磨了。你意识到没有，我们在你身上投下了一笔巨款，条件白纸黑字：指望你尽本分、立竿见影。莫非我们被迫得出结论，你这人黄得像豆荚里的瘪粒，想赖掉你光荣的义务？我们把你看高了，威克斯。我向山姆起誓，我们把你看高了。我们当你是个双拳过硬、敢作敢为、大魂灵、百分百的男子汉，肯为朋友撑到底的。"

"可是——"

"随便哪个有点义气、体谅大家处境的家伙，早就冲出门去想方设法尽义务了。你呢，送上门的机会都不抓。就在昨天，我还眼看你往后缩——其实只要往马路跨一步，就能让一辆卡车撞上。"

"唉，让卡车撞，也不是说撞就撞得上的。"

"胡说。只要一点寻常的决心。动用你的想象力。你就想，一个孩子摔倒在街上——一个金头发的小孩，"厄克里奇深情地说。"一辆要命的马车什么的骨碌碌驶来。孩子的母亲站在人行道上，束手无策，痛苦地绞着手。'天哪，'她喊，'没人救救我的宝贝吗？''有，见鬼，'你喊，'*我*来！'你一跃而出，半秒钟就完事。我不懂这有什么好小题大做的。"

"可是——"特迪·威克斯说。

"再说了，听说一点也不疼。就是一阵发闷的震荡，仅此而已。"

"谁告诉你的？"

"忘了。某个人。"

"那你可以转告他，他是头蠢驴，"特迪·威克斯没好气地说。

"好。你要是嫌叫卡车撞不好，法子有的是。可是，我向山姆起誓，跟你提也是白提。你好像一点闯劲也没有。昨天，我费那么大劲往你屋里放了一条狗——一条本可以替你包办一切的狗——你只需站住不动，由它自行判断——结果呢？你爬上了——"

维克托·比米什打断了，嗓音因激动而嘶哑。

===Original===

“Was it you who put that damned dog in the room?”

“Eh?” said Ukridge. “Why, yes. But we can have a good talk about all that later on,” he proceeded, hastily. “The point at the moment is how the dickens we’re going to persuade this poor worm to collect our insurance money for us. Why, damme, I should have thought you would have﻿—”

“All I can say﻿—” began Victor Beamish, heatedly.

“Yes, yes,” said Ukridge; “some other time. Must stick to business now, laddie. I was saying,” he resumed, “that I should have thought you would have been as keen as mustard to put the job through for your own sake. You’re always beefing that you haven’t any clothes to impress managers with. Think of all you can buy with your share of the swag once you have summoned up a little ordinary determination and seen the thing through. Think of the suits, the boots, the hats, the spats. You’re always talking about your dashed career, and how all you need to land you in a West-end production is good clothes. Well, here’s your chance to get them.”

His eloquence was not wasted. A wistful look came into Teddy Weeks’s eye, such a look as must have come into the eye of Moses on the summit of Pisgah. He breathed heavily. You could see that the man was mentally walking along Cork Street, weighing the merits of one famous tailor against another.

“I’ll tell you what I’ll do,” he said, suddenly. “It’s no use asking me to put this thing through in cold blood. I simply can’t do it. I haven’t the nerve. But if you fellows will give me a dinner tonight with lots of champagne I think it will key me up to it.”

A heavy silence fell upon the room. Champagne! The word was like a knell.

“How on earth are we going to afford champagne?” said Victor Beamish.

“Well, there it is,” said Teddy Weeks. “Take it or leave it.”

“Gentlemen,” said Ukridge, “it would seem that the company requires more capital. How about it, old horses? Let’s get together in a frank, businesslike cards-on-the-table spirit, and see what can be done. I can raise ten bob.”

“What!” cried the entire assembled company, amazed. “How?”

“I’ll pawn a banjo.”

“You haven’t got a banjo.”

“No, but George Tupper has, and I know where he keeps it.”

===Chinese===

"往屋里放那条该死的狗的，是你？"

"嗯？"厄克里奇说。"呃，是。不过这些咱们回头好好聊，"他赶紧接下去。"眼下的要害是，怎么劝这条可怜的虫子替咱们去领保险金。嗐，见鬼，我还当你准会——"

"我只想说——"维克托·比米什激愤地开腔。

"是是，"厄克里奇说，"改日再聊。现在得抓正事，小家伙。我是说，"他重新拉起话头，"我还当你准会为了你自己，起劲把这事办成。你老是抱怨没有体面衣裳去镇住经理。想想吧，只要你拿出一点点寻常的决心把事情办成，凭你那一份赃款能置办多少东西。想想那些西装、靴子、帽子、鞋罩。你老念叨你的锦绣前程，说只消一身好衣裳就能把你送上西区的舞台。喏，机会来了。"

他的雄辩没有白费。特迪·威克斯眼里浮起一缕热望，就像摩西在毗斯迦山顶上必曾浮起的那种。他呼吸粗重。看得出，此人在精神上正沿着科克街踱步，掂量着这一家名店与那一家名店的高下。

"这么办吧，"他忽然说。"要我冷血冰冰地办这事，办不了。我压根没这个胆。可要是你们几位今晚请我一顿，香槟管够，我想那能把我提起神来。"

沉重的沉默降临全屋。香槟！这词像丧钟。

"香槟咱们怎么请得起？"维克托·比米什说。

"反正话放这儿了，"特迪·威克斯说。"爱要不要。"

"诸位，"厄克里奇说，"看来本公司需要追加资本。怎么样，老伙计们？咱们本着坦率的、公事公办的摊牌精神凑一凑，看能想什么办法。我能筹出十先令。"

"什么！"全体在场者惊呼。"怎么筹？"

"我当一把班卓琴。"

"你没有班卓琴。"

"没有，可乔治·塔珀（George Tupper）有，而且我知道他搁哪儿。"

===Original===

Started in this spirited way, the subscriptions came pouring in. I contributed a cigarette-case, Bertram Fox thought his landlady would let him owe for another week, Robert Dunhill had an uncle in Kensington who, he fancied, if tactfully approached, would be good for a quid, and Victor Beamish said that if the advertisement-manager of the O-So-Eesi Piano-Player was churlish enough to refuse an advance of five shillings against future work he misjudged him sadly. Within a few minutes, in short, the Lightning Drive had produced the impressive total of two pounds six shillings, and we asked Teddy Weeks if he thought that he could get adequately keyed up within the limits of that sum.

“I’ll try,” said Teddy Weeks.

So, not unmindful of the fact that that excellent hostelry supplied champagne at eight shillings the quart bottle, we fixed the meeting for seven o’clock at Barolini’s.

Considered as a social affair, Teddy Weeks’s keying-up dinner was not a success. Almost from the start I think we all found it trying. It was not so much the fact that he was drinking deeply of Barolini’s eight-shilling champagne while we, from lack of funds, were compelled to confine ourselves to meaner beverages; what really marred the pleasantness of the function was the extraordinary effect the stuff had on Teddy. What was actually in the champagne supplied to Barolini and purveyed by him to the public, such as were reckless enough to drink it, at eight shillings the bottle remains a secret between its maker and his Maker; but three glasses of it were enough to convert Teddy Weeks from a mild and rather oily young man into a truculent swashbuckler.

===Chinese===

在这一豪迈先例的带动下，认捐滚滚而来。我捐了一只烟盒，伯特伦·福克斯觉得他房东太太兴许肯让他再赊一星期，罗伯特·邓希尔有位住在肯辛顿的叔叔，他料想只要劝得得法，一镑总出得起，维克托·比米什则表示，"欧索易"自动钢琴的广告经理若是吝啬到连五先令的预支稿酬都不肯给，那他就大大看错此人了。不多几分钟，这场闪电募集已凑出两镑六先令的可观总额，我们便问特迪·威克斯，这点钱够不够把他提到位。

"我试试，"特迪·威克斯说。

于是，惦记着那家上等酒馆的香槟卖八先令一夸脱瓶，我们把会面定在七点钟，地点巴罗利尼。

作为一场社交活动，特迪·威克斯的提神宴不算成功。几乎从头一起，我想我们人人都觉得煎熬。倒不全是因为他在痛饮巴罗利尼八先令的香槟，而我们因囊中羞涩只能屈就低档饮料；真正败坏这场雅集兴致的，是那玩意儿在特迪身上引发的奇效。巴罗利尼供应并零售给那些胆敢喝它的公众的、八先令一瓶的香槟里究竟是什么，那是其酿造者与造物主之间的秘密；但三杯下肚，已足以把特迪·威克斯从一个温和乃至滑腻的青年，改造成一个好斗的莽汉。

===Original===

He quarrelled with us all. With the soup he was tilting at Victor Beamish’s theories of Art; the fish found him ridiculing Bertram Fox’s views on the future of the motion picture; and by the time the leg of chicken with dandelion salad arrived﻿—or, as some held, string salad﻿—opinions varied on this point﻿—the hell-brew had so wrought on him that he had begun to lecture Ukridge on his misspent life and was urging him in accents audible across the street to go out and get a job and thus acquire sufficient self-respect to enable him to look himself in the face in a mirror without wincing. Not, added Teddy Weeks with what we all thought uncalled-for offensiveness, that any amount of self-respect was likely to do that. Having said which, he called imperiously for another eight bobs’-worth.

We gazed at one another wanly. However excellent the end towards which all this was tending, there was no denying that it was hard to bear. But policy kept us silent. We recognised that this was Teddy Weeks’s evening and that he must be humoured. Victor Beamish said meekly that Teddy had cleared up a lot of points which had been troubling him for a long time. Bertram Fox agreed that there was much in what Teddy had said about the future of the closeup. And even Ukridge, though his haughty soul was seared to its foundations by the latter’s personal remarks, promised to take his homily to heart and act upon it at the earliest possible moment.

“You’d better!” said Teddy Weeks, belligerently, biting off the end of one of Barolini’s best cigars. “And there’s another thing﻿—don’t let me hear of your coming and sneaking people’s socks again.”

“Very well, laddie,” said Ukridge, humbly.

“If there is one person in the world that I despise,” said Teddy, bending a red-eyed gaze on the offender, “it’s a snock-seeker﻿—a seek-snocker﻿—a﻿—well, you know what I mean.”

We hastened to assure him that we knew what he meant and he relapsed into a lengthy stupor, from which he emerged three-quarters of an hour later to announce that he didn’t know what we intended to do, but that he was going. We said that we were going too, and we paid the bill and did so.

===Chinese===

他跟我们全体都吵了。上汤时他在攻击维克托·比米什的艺术理论；上鱼时他在讥笑伯特伦·福克斯关于电影前途的见解；等到端上配蒲公英沙拉的鸡腿——也有人咬定是纸浆草沙拉——这一点众说不一——那地狱酿已在他身上充分发作，他开始训诫厄克里奇荒废的一生，还用隔着一条街都听得见的嗓音敦促他出门找个工作，好挣得足够的自尊，使他照镜子时不必别过脸去。特迪·威克斯又补上一句——我们都认为这话说得刻薄无谓——再多的自尊恐怕也办不到这一点。说完，他威风凛凛地又叫了一客八先令的份。

我们面面相觑，面色发青。不论这一切趋向的目标多么美妙，无可否认，过程难熬。但策略使我们缄默。我们承认，今晚是特迪·威克斯的夜晚，得顺着他。维克托·比米什温顺地说，特迪澄清了许多长久困扰他的疑点。伯特伦·福克斯同意，特迪关于特写镜头前途的见解大有道理。连厄克里奇——尽管后者涉及个人的议论把他那骄傲的灵魂从根上灼了个透——也允诺把这篇训诫铭记在心，并尽早付诸行动。

"最好如此！"特迪·威克斯好斗地说，一口咬掉巴罗利尼上好雪茄的头。"还有一条——别让我再听说你到处顺人家的袜子。"

"好的，小家伙，"厄克里奇谦卑地说。

"这世上我要是还看得起一个人，"特迪红着眼瞪着罪犯说，"也绝看不惯偷袜的——袜偷的——呃——反正，你懂我的意思。"

我们赶紧向他保证我们懂他的意思。他重新坠入漫长的昏沉，三刻钟后才浮出来宣布：他不知道我们打算怎么办，反正他走了。我们说我们也走，我们付了账，也真走了。

===Original===

Teddy Weeks’s indignation on discovering us gathered about him upon the pavement outside the restaurant was intense, and he expressed it freely. Among other things, he said﻿—which was not true﻿—that he had a reputation to keep up in Soho.

“It’s all right, Teddy, old horse,” said Ukridge, soothingly. “We just thought you would like to have all your old pals round you when you did it.”

“Did it? Did what?”

“Why, had the accident.”

Teddy Weeks glared at him truculently. Then his mood seemed to change abruptly, and he burst into a loud and hearty laugh.

“Well, of all the silly ideas!” he cried, amusedly. “I’m not going to have an accident. You don’t suppose I ever seriously intended to have an accident, do you? It was just my fun.” Then, with another sudden change of mood, he seemed to become a victim to an acute unhappiness. He stroked Ukridge’s arm affectionately, and a tear rolled down his cheek. “Just my fun,” he repeated. “You don’t mind my fun, do you?” he asked, pleadingly. “You like my fun, don’t you? All my fun. Never meant to have an accident at all. Just wanted dinner.” The gay humour of it all overcame his sorrow once more. “Funniest thing ever heard,” he said cordially. “Didn’t want accident, wanted dinner. Dinner daxident, danner dixident,” he added, driving home his point. “Well, good night all,” he said, cheerily. And, stepping off the kerb on to a banana-skin, was instantly knocked ten feet by a passing lorry.

“Two ribs and an arm,” said the doctor five minutes later, superintending the removal proceedings. “Gently with that stretcher.”

It was two weeks before we were informed by the authorities of Charing Cross Hospital that the patient was in a condition to receive visitors. A whip-round secured the price of a basket of fruit, and Ukridge and I were deputed by the shareholders to deliver it with their compliments and kind enquiries.

“Hallo!” we said in a hushed, bedside manner when finally admitted to his presence.

“Sit down, gentlemen,” replied the invalid.

I must confess even in that first moment to having experienced a slight feeling of surprise. It was not like Teddy Weeks to call us gentlemen. Ukridge, however, seemed to notice nothing amiss.

“Well, well, well,” he said, buoyantly. “And how are you, laddie? We’ve brought you a few fragments of fruit.”

===Chinese===

特迪·威克斯发现我们聚在餐馆外的人行道上围着他，其愤慨是强烈的，且表达得酣畅淋漓。他说的种种之中有一句——不实之词——说他在索霍区还有名声要爱惜。

"没事的，特迪老伙计，"厄克里奇安抚道。"我们只是想，你办事的时候，身边该围着全体老友。"

"办事？办什么事？"

"嗐，出意外呀。"

特迪·威克斯凶悍地瞪着他。随即他的情绪似乎陡然一变，爆出一阵响亮爽朗的大笑。

"嗐，多蠢的念头！"他乐呵呵地喊。"我才不去出什么意外。你们不会真以为我当过真吧？就是逗你们玩。"随即情绪又是陡然一变，他似乎陷入了剧烈的哀伤。他深情地抚摸厄克里奇的胳膊，一滴泪滚下面颊。"就是逗你们玩，"他重复。"你们不介意我逗着玩，对吧？"他央求道。"你们喜欢我逗着玩，对吧？我全是逗着玩。压根没想出什么意外。就是想要顿饭。"这满堂的妙趣又一次压倒了他的悲伤。"天底下最好笑的事，"他热诚地说。"不要意外，要晚饭。晚饭意外，意外晚饭，"他补充着，把这层道理钉死。"好，各位晚安。"他说完，兴冲冲地一脚踏下路缘，踩上一块香蕉皮，当即被一辆过路的货车撞出十英尺远。

"两根肋骨加一条胳膊，"五分钟后医生说，监督着搬运事宜。"担架轻一点。"

两星期后，查令十字医院当局才通知我们，病人已可会客。大家凑份子买了一篮水果，股东们委派厄克里奇与我，代表全体登门奉送，附致问候与慰问。

"喂！"终于被放进他跟前时，我们以压低的病榻口吻说。

"二位请坐，"病人答道。

我得承认，初见那一刻我就微微吃了一惊。称我们"二位先生"可不像特迪·威克斯的做派。厄克里奇却似乎毫无察觉。

"哎呀呀，"他喜洋洋地说。"你怎么样了，小家伙？我们给你带来了几片水果的碎屑。"

===Original===

“I am getting along capitally,” replied Teddy Weeks, still in that odd precise way which had made his opening words strike me as curious. “And I should like to say that in my opinion England has reason to be proud of the alertness and enterprise of her great journals. The excellence of their reading-matter, the ingenuity of their various competitions, and, above all, the go-ahead spirit which has resulted in this accident insurance scheme are beyond praise. Have you got that down?” he enquired.

Ukridge and I looked at each other. We had been told that Teddy was practically normal again, but this sounded like delirium.

“Have we got that down, old horse?” asked Ukridge, gently.

Teddy Weeks seemed surprised.

“Aren’t you reporters?”

“How do you mean, reporters?”

“I thought you had come from one of these weekly papers that have been paying me insurance money, to interview me,” said Teddy Weeks.

Ukridge and I exchanged another glance. An uneasy glance this time. I think that already a grim foreboding had begun to cast its shadow over us.

“Surely you remember me, Teddy, old horse?” said Ukridge, anxiously.

Teddy Weeks knit his brow, concentrating painfully.

“Why, of course,” he said at last. “You’re Ukridge, aren’t you?”

“That’s right. Ukridge.”

“Of course. Ukridge.”

“Yes. Ukridge. Funny your forgetting me!”

“Yes,” said Teddy Weeks. “It’s the effect of the shock I got when that thing bowled me over. I must have been struck on the head, I suppose. It has had the effect of rendering my memory rather uncertain. The doctors here are very interested. They say it is a most unusual case. I can remember some things perfectly, but in some ways my memory is a complete blank.”

“Oh, but I say, old horse,” quavered Ukridge. “I suppose you haven’t forgotten about that insurance, have you?”

“Oh, no, I remember that.”

Ukridge breathed a relieved sigh.

“I was a subscriber to a number of weekly papers,” went on Teddy Weeks. “They are paying me insurance money now.”

“Yes, yes, old horse,” cried Ukridge. “But what I mean is you remember the Syndicate, don’t you?”

Teddy Weeks raised his eyebrows.

“Syndicate? What Syndicate?”

“Why, when we all got together and put up the money to pay for the subscriptions to these papers and drew lots, to choose which of us should go out and have an accident and collect the money. And you drew it, don’t you remember?”

===Chinese===

"我康复得很好，"特迪·威克斯答道，仍是那种古怪的一板一眼，正是它使他开场的称呼叫我纳闷。"而且我愿在此指出，依我之见，英格兰有理由为她各大报刊的警觉与进取自豪。它们正文的精彩，它们种种竞赛的巧思，尤其是促成本项意外保险方案的那种先进精神，都值得称颂。记下了吗？"他问。

厄克里奇和我面面相觑。人家告诉我们特迪基本复原了，可这听着像谵妄。

"记下了吗，老伙计？"厄克里奇轻轻地问。

特迪·威克斯似乎吃了一惊。

"你们不是记者？"

"什么意思，记者？"

"我还以为你们是哪家付我保险金的周刊派来采访我的，"特迪·威克斯说。

厄克里奇和我又交换了一瞥。这回是不安的一瞥。我想，一个不祥的预感已经开始向我们投下阴影。

"你总还记得我吧，特迪老伙计？"厄克里奇焦急地说。

特迪·威克斯皱起眉，苦苦凝想。

"哦，当然，"他终于说。"你是厄克里奇，对吧？"

"正是。厄克里奇。"

"当然。厄克里奇。"

"对。厄克里奇。亏你忘得了我！"

"是，"特迪·威克斯说。"这是那玩意儿把我撞翻时受的震荡闹的。我大概叫撞着了脑袋。它把我的记忆弄得相当靠不住。这里的医生们都很感兴趣。他们说这是极罕见的病例。有些事我记得清清楚楚，可某些方面，我的记忆一片空白。"

"哎呀，我说老伙计，"厄克里奇声音发颤。"你不会把那保险的事也忘了吧？"

"哦，没忘，那个我记得。"

厄克里奇如释重负地舒了口气。

"我订了好几家周刊，"特迪·威克斯继续说。"他们现在正在付我保险金。"

"对，对，老伙计，"厄克里奇喊。"可我的意思是，你总还记得辛迪加吧？"

特迪·威克斯扬起了眉毛。

"辛迪加？什么辛迪加？"

"嗐，就是咱们大伙凑钱订那些报刊、抽签定谁出门去出意外、好领钱的那回事啊。签是你抽中的，你不记得了？"

===Original===

Utter astonishment, and a shocked astonishment at that, spread itself over Teddy Weeks’s countenance. The man seemed outraged.

“I certainly remember nothing of the kind,” he said, severely. “I cannot imagine myself for a moment consenting to become a party to what from your own account would appear to have been a criminal conspiracy to obtain money under false pretences from a number of weekly papers.”

“But, laddie﻿—”

“However,” said Teddy Weeks, “if there is any truth in this story, no doubt you have documentary evidence to support it.”

Ukridge looked at me. I looked at Ukridge. There was a long silence.

“Shift-ho, old horse?” said Ukridge, sadly. “No use staying on here.”

“No,” I replied, with equal gloom. “May as well go.”

“Glad to have seen you,” said Teddy Weeks, “and thanks for the fruit.”

The next time I saw the man he was coming out of a manager’s office in the Haymarket. He had on a new Homburg hat of a delicate pearl grey, spats to match, and a new blue flannel suit, beautifully cut, with an invisible red twill. He was looking jubilant, and; as I passed him, he drew from his pocket a gold cigarette-case.

It was shortly after that, if you remember, that he made a big hit as the juvenile lead in that piece at the Apollo and started on his sensational career as a matinée idol.

Inside the church the organ had swelled into the familiar music of the Wedding March. A verger came out and opened the doors. The five cooks ceased their reminiscences of other and smarter weddings at which they had participated. The cameramen unshipped their cameras. The costermonger moved his barrow of vegetables a pace forward. A dishevelled and unshaven man at my side uttered a disapproving growl.

“Idle rich!” said the dishevelled man.

Out of the church came a beauteous being, leading attached to his arm another being, somewhat less beauteous.

There was no denying the spectacular effect of Teddy Weeks. He was handsomer than ever. His sleek hair, gorgeously waved, shone in the sun, his eyes were large and bright; his lissome frame, garbed in faultless morning-coat and trousers, was that of an Apollo. But his bride gave the impression that Teddy had married money. They paused in the doorway, and the cameramen became active and fussy.

“Have you got a shilling, laddie?” said Ukridge in a low, level voice.

“Why do you want a shilling?”

===Chinese===

全然的惊愕——而且是震骇的惊愕——布满了特迪·威克斯的脸。此君像是被冒犯了。

"那样的事我毫无印象，"他严正地说。"我无法想象自己会一时糊涂，加入一场——照你自己说法——以虚假名目骗取多家周刊钱财的犯罪阴谋。"

"可是，小家伙——"

"不过，"特迪·威克斯说，"这事若属实，想必你们握有书面凭据。"

厄克里奇看看我。我看看厄克里奇。长久的沉默。

"撤吧，老伙计？"厄克里奇悲哀地说。"再待下去没用了。"

"没用了，"我同样阴郁地应道。"走吧。"

"很高兴见到你们，"特迪·威克斯说，"多谢你们的水果。"

我下一次见到此人，是他从干草市场一家经理办公室里出来。他戴着一顶珠灰色的新洪堡帽，配一双同色鞋罩，一身剪裁极美的新蓝法兰绒西装，暗红斜纹夹里。他满面春风；同他擦肩而过时，他从口袋里摸出一只金烟盒。

就是那以后不久——你总记得——他在阿波罗剧院那出戏里以小生身份一炮而红，开始了日后轰动一时的少女偶像生涯。

教堂里，管风琴已涨成《婚礼进行曲》熟悉的旋律。一名司事出来打开了大门。五个厨娘停止了她们对另几场更体面婚礼——她们都曾躬逢其盛——的追忆。摄影师们支起机器。菜贩把菜车向前挪了一步。我身旁一个蓬头垢面、胡子拉碴的男人发出一声不满的咕噜。

"游手好闲的阔佬！"蓬头垢面的人说。

教堂里走出一位光彩照人的存在，臂上挽着另一位光彩稍逊的存在。

特迪·威克斯的舞台效果无可否认。他比以往更俊了。他那油光光的头发烫得华美，在太阳底下发亮；眼睛又大又亮；那副穿无可挑剔的晨礼服与裤子的灵巧身段，是阿波罗的身材。可他的新娘给人一个印象：特迪娶的是钱。二人在门洞里驻足，摄影师们忙碌而忙乱起来。

"有一先令吗，小家伙？"厄克里奇用低而平的声音说。

"要一先令干什么？"

===Original===

“Old horse,” said Ukridge, tensely, “it is of the utmost vital importance that I have a shilling here and now.”

I passed it over. Ukridge turned to the dishevelled man, and I perceived that he held in his hand a large rich tomato of juicy and overripe appearance.

“Would you like to earn a bob?” Ukridge said.

“Would I!” replied the dishevelled man.

Ukridge sank his voice to a hoarse whisper.

The cameramen had finished their preparations. Teddy Weeks, his head thrown back in that gallant way which has endeared him to so many female hearts, was exhibiting his celebrated teeth. The cooks, in undertones, were making adverse comments on the appearance of the bride.

“Now, please,” said one of the cameramen.

Over the heads of the crowd, well and truly aimed, whizzed a large juicy tomato. It burst like a shell full between Teddy Weeks’s expressive eyes, obliterating them in scarlet ruin. It spattered Teddy Weeks’s collar, it dripped on Teddy Weeks’s morning-coat. And the dishevelled man turned abruptly and raced off down the street.

Ukridge grasped my arm. There was a look of deep content in his eyes.

“Shift-ho?” said Ukridge.

Arm-in-arm, we strolled off in the pleasant June sunshine.

===Chinese===

"老伙计，"厄克里奇紧张地说，"此时此地，我急需一先令，兹事体大。"

我递了过去。厄克里奇转向那蓬头垢面的男人，我看见他手里托着一只肥大、多汁、熟透模样的大番茄。

"想挣一先令吗？"厄克里奇说。

"怎么不想！"蓬头垢面的男人答。

厄克里奇把嗓音压成沙哑的耳语。

摄影师们准备停当。特迪·威克斯头颅后仰，摆出那个赢得过无数芳心的英姿势态，正展览他那口名牙。厨娘们在低声对新娘的仪表发表负面评论。

"好了，请让一让。"一位摄影师说。

一只肥大多汁的番茄越过众人的头顶，瞄得又稳又准，嗖地飞去。它在特迪·威克斯那双多情眼正中炸开，像一发炮弹，把他俩淹没在猩红的废墟里。它溅污了特迪·威克斯的领子，滴落在特迪·威克斯的晨礼服上。那蓬头垢面的男人猛一转身，撒腿飞奔下街去了。

厄克里奇攥住我的胳膊。他眼里有一种深深的满足。

"撤吧？"厄克里奇说。

我们挽着臂，在六月宜人的阳光里信步走远了。

## Ukridge’s Dog College / 厄克里奇的狗狗学院
===Original===

“Laddie,” said Stanley Featherstonehaugh Ukridge, that much-enduring man, helping himself to my tobacco and slipping the pouch absently into his pocket, “listen to me, you son of Belial.”

“What?” I said, retrieving the pouch.

“Do you want to make an enormous fortune?”

“I do.”

“Then write my biography. Bung it down on paper, and we’ll split the proceeds. I’ve been making a pretty close study of your stuff lately, old horse, and it’s all wrong. The trouble with you is that you don’t plumb the wellsprings of human nature and all that. You just think up some rotten yarn about some-dam-thing-or-other and shove it down. Now, if you tackled my life, you’d have something worth writing about. Pots of money in it, my boy﻿—English serial rights and American serial rights and book rights, and dramatic rights and movie rights﻿—well, you can take it from me that, at a conservative estimate, we should clean up at least fifty thousand pounds apiece.”

“As much as that?”

“Fully that. And listen, laddie, I’ll tell you what. You’re a good chap and we’ve been pals for years, so I’ll let you have my share of the English serial rights for a hundred pounds down.”

“What makes you think I’ve got a hundred pounds?”

“Well, then, I’ll make it my share of the English and American serial rights for fifty.”

“Your collar’s come off its stud.”

“How about my complete share of the whole dashed outfit for twenty-five?”

“Not for me, thanks.”

“Then I’ll tell you what, old horse,” said Ukridge, inspired. “Just lend me half a crown to be going on with.”

---

If the leading incidents of S. F. Ukridge’s disreputable career are to be given to the public﻿—and not, as some might suggest, decently hushed up﻿—I suppose I am the man to write them. Ukridge and I have been intimate since the days of school. Together we sported on the green, and when he was expelled no one missed him more than I. An unfortunate business, this expulsion. Ukridge’s generous spirit, ever ill-attuned to school rules, caused him eventually to break the solemnest of them all by sneaking out at night to try his skill at the coconut-shies of the local village fair; and his foresight in putting on scarlet whiskers and a false nose for the expedition was completely neutralised by the fact that he absentmindedly wore his school cap throughout the entire proceedings. He left the next morning, regretted by all.

===Chinese===

"小家伙，"斯坦利·费瑟斯通豪·厄克里奇，这位百折不挠的人说，一边自取我的烟草，又心不在焉把烟袋顺手揣进自己口袋，"听我说，你这彼列之子。"

"什么？"我说，把烟袋拿了回来。

"你想不想发一笔大财？"

"想。"

"那就给我写传记。把它往纸上一倒，咱们分账。最近我把你那些货色研究得相当细，老伙计，全不对路。你的毛病在于不去深挖人性的源泉那一套。你只想出个什么劳什子的烂故事，硬塞出去。要是你写我的一生，那才算有东西可写。里面有大把的钱，我的伙计——英国连载权、美国连载权、出书权、戏剧权、电影权——嗐，你只管信我的，保守估计，咱们每人至少净捞五万镑。"

"有那么多？"

"只多不少。还有，听着，小家伙，我跟你讲。你是条好汉，咱们又是多年的哥们儿，所以我把英国连载权我那一股让给你，一百镑现付。"

"你凭什么认为我有一百镑？"

"那好，改成英美两项连载权我那一股，五十镑。"

"你的领子从领扣上崩开了。"

"整套要命的权利我那全份，二十五镑怎么样？"

"我不要，谢谢。"

"那么这样，老伙计，"厄克里奇灵机一动。"先借我半克朗应应急。"

---

S. F. 厄克里奇那段声名狼藉的生涯，其要紧事迹若要公之于众——而不是像有人会主张的那样体面地捂住——我想，执笔的合该是我。厄克里奇同我打中学时代起就过从甚密。我们一同在草地上嬉戏；他被开除那天，没人比我更想念他。那次开除是件不幸的事。厄克里奇那副慷慨心肠，从来同校规不合拍，终于叫他犯了其中最庄严的一条：夜里溜出去，到本村集市上小试套圈赢椰子的身手；他为此次远征煞费苦心戴上红假须和假鼻子，却因整个过程中一直心不在焉地戴着学校制帽，前功尽弃。次日一早他离校而去，举校同憾。

===Original===

After this there was a hiatus of some years in our friendship. I was at Cambridge, absorbing culture, and Ukridge, as far as I could gather from his rare letters and the reports of mutual acquaintances, flitting about the world like a snipe. Somebody met him in New York, just off a cattle-ship. Somebody else saw him in Buenos Aires. Somebody, again, spoke sadly of having been pounced on by him at Monte Carlo and touched for a fiver. It was not until I settled down in London that he came back into my life. We met in Piccadilly one day, and resumed our relations where they had been broken off. Old associations are strong, and the fact that he was about my build and so could wear my socks and shirts drew us very close together.

Then he disappeared again, and it was a month or more before I got news of him.

It was George Tupper who brought the news. George was head of the school in my last year, and he has fulfilled exactly the impeccable promise of those early days. He is in the Foreign Office, doing well and much respected. He has an earnest, pulpy heart and takes other people’s troubles very seriously. Often he had mourned to me like a father over Ukridge’s erratic progress through life, and now, as he spoke, he seemed to be filled with a solemn joy, as over a reformed prodigal.

“Have you heard about Ukridge?” said George Tupper. “He has settled down at last. Gone to live with an aunt of his who owns one of those big houses on Wimbledon Common. A very rich woman. I am delighted. It will be the making of the old chap.”

I suppose he was right in a way, but to me this tame subsidence into companionship with a rich aunt in Wimbledon seemed somehow an indecent, almost a tragic, end to a colourful career like that of S. F. Ukridge. And when I met the man a week later my heart grew heavier still.

===Chinese===

此后我们的交谊中断了几年。我在剑桥吞咽文化，而厄克里奇，从他不常来的信和共同熟人的转述里拼凑，正像一只鹬鸟似的满世界飞掠。有人在纽约见到他，刚下运牛船。又有人在布宜诺斯艾利斯见到他。还有人痛心地谈起，在蒙特卡洛被他逮个正着，通融了五镑。直到我在伦敦定居下来，他才重新进入我的生活。一天我们在皮卡迪利相遇，从当年中断处再续前缘。旧日的情分是顽强的，加上他身量同我相仿、穿得上我的袜子和衬衫这一事实，把我们拉得更紧了。

随后他又失踪了，一个多月后我才得到他的消息。

带来消息的是乔治·塔珀。乔治是我最后一年里的全校第一，而他也分毫不差地兑现了当年的完美前程。他在外交部任职，干得漂亮，颇受敬重。他有一副热忱软和的心肠，把别人的难处看得极重。他常像父亲一样为厄克里奇在人生路上的一路乱窜向我哀叹；此刻说话时，他满怀庄严的喜悦，像在说一个回头浪子。

"你听说厄克里奇了吗？"乔治·塔珀说。"他总算安顿下来了。搬去同他一位姑妈住，姑妈在温布尔登公地有一所大宅。很有钱的女人。我太高兴了。这将成就这老家伙。"

我想，某种意义上他是对的；可在我看，这样驯顺地熄火，屈身去温布尔登伴一位阔姑妈，对 S. F. 厄克里奇这般多彩的生涯而言，多少是个不体面的、近乎悲剧的收场。而一星期后我见到此人，我的心愈发沉重了。

===Original===

It was in Oxford Street at the hour when women come up from the suburbs to shop; and he was standing among the dogs and commissionaires outside Selfridge’s. His arms were full of parcels, his face was set in a mask of wan discomfort, and he was so beautifully dressed that for an instant I did not recognise him. Everything which the Correct Man wears was assembled on his person, from the silk hat to the patent-leather boots; and, as he confided to me in the first minute, he was suffering the tortures of the damned. The boots pinched him, the hat hurt his forehead, and the collar was worse than the hat and boots combined.

“She makes me wear them,” he said, moodily, jerking his head towards the interior of the store and uttering a sharp howl as the movement caused the collar to gouge his neck.

“Still,” I said, trying to turn his mind to happier things, “you must be having a great time. George Tupper tells me that your aunt is rich. I suppose you’re living off the fat of the land.”

“The browsing and sluicing are good,” admitted Ukridge. “But it’s a wearing life, laddie. A wearing life, old horse.”

“Why don’t you come and see me sometimes?”

“I’m not allowed out at night.”

“Well, shall I come and see you?”

A look of poignant alarm shot out from under the silk hat.

“Don’t dream of it, laddie,” said Ukridge, earnestly. “Don’t dream of it. You’re a good chap﻿—my best pal and all that sort of thing﻿—but the fact is, my standing in the home’s none too solid even now, and one sight of you would knock my prestige into hash. Aunt Julia would think you worldly.”

“I’m not worldly.”

“Well, you look worldly. You wear a squash hat and a soft collar. If you don’t mind my suggesting it, old horse, I think, if I were you, I’d pop off now before she comes out. Goodbye, laddie.”

“Ichabod!” I murmured sadly to myself as I passed on down Oxford Street. “Ichabod!”

I should have had more faith. I should have known my Ukridge better. I should have realised that a London suburb could no more imprison that great man permanently than Elba did Napoleon.

One afternoon, as I let myself into the house in Ebury Street of which I rented at that time the bedroom and sitting room on the first floor, I came upon Bowles, my landlord, standing in listening attitude at the foot of the stairs.

===Chinese===

那是在牛津街，正值女人们从郊区进城购物的钟点；他站在塞尔福里奇门外的一片狗和门卫中间。他双臂抱满包裹，脸罩着一层惨兮兮的不适，而那一身穿戴漂亮得我一时没认出他来。"体面男士"该有的一切都已在他身上列队，从丝质礼帽到漆皮靴；而且，照他第一分钟里的自白，他正遭受着炼狱般的酷刑。靴子夹脚，帽子硌额头，而领子比帽子加靴子加起来还要命。

"她逼我穿的，"他闷闷地说，朝店堂里扬了扬头，这一动叫领子剜了一下脖子，他发出一声锐利的哀号。

"不过，"我说，努力把他的心思引向愉快的事，"你日子一定过得美。乔治·塔珀告诉我你姑妈有钱。想必你吃着肥甘、喝着琼浆了。"

"吃吃喝喝确实不赖，"厄克里奇承认。"可这种日子磨人哪，小家伙。磨人哪，老伙计。"

"你怎么不时常来看我？"

"晚上不许我出门。"

"那我看你去？"

丝礼帽底下射出一道锥心的惊惶。

"想都别想，小家伙，"厄克里奇恳切地说。"想都别想。你是条好汉——我最好的哥们儿等等——可实情是，我在那个家里的地位眼下就不算牢靠，你一露面，我的威望就得碎成渣。朱莉娅（Julia）姑妈会认为你俗气。"

"我不俗气。"

"呃，你看着俗气。你戴软塌帽，穿软领子。不嫌弃我多嘴的话，老伙计，我要是你，趁她还没出来，这就溜之大吉。再见，小家伙。"

"以迦博！"我沿牛津街走开时忧伤地喃喃。"以迦博！"（Ichabod）

我本该多些信心。我本该更了解我的厄克里奇。我本该明白，伦敦一个郊区，是关不住那位伟人的，正如厄尔巴岛关不住拿破仑。

一天下午，我用自己的钥匙开进当时整租着二楼卧室与客厅的伊伯里街那所宅子，撞见我的房东鲍尔斯，正以谛听的姿态立在楼梯脚。

===Original===

“Good afternoon, sir,” said Bowles. “A gentleman is waiting to see you. I fancy I heard him calling me a moment ago.”

“Who is he?”

“A Mr. Ukridge, sir. He﻿—”

A vast voice boomed out from above.

“Bowles, old horse!”

Bowles, like all other proprietors of furnished apartments in the southwestern district of London, was an ex-butler, and about him, as about all ex-butlers, there clung like a garment an aura of dignified superiority which had never failed to crush my spirit. He was a man of portly aspect, with a bald head and prominent eyes of a lightish green﻿—eyes that seemed to weigh me dispassionately and find me wanting. “H’m!” they seemed to say. “Young﻿—very young. And not at all what I have been accustomed to in the best places.” To hear this dignitary addressed﻿—and in a shout at that﻿—as “old horse” affected me with much the same sense of imminent chaos as would afflict a devout young curate if he saw his bishop slapped on the back. The shock, therefore, when he responded not merely mildly but with what almost amounted to camaraderie was numbing.

“Sir?” cooed Bowles.

“Bring me six bones and a corkscrew.”

“Very good, sir.”

Bowles retired, and I bounded upstairs and flung open the door of my sitting room.

“Great Scott!” I said, blankly.

The place was a sea of Pekingese dogs. Later investigation reduced their numbers to six, but in that first moment there seemed to be hundreds. Goggling eyes met mine wherever I looked. The room was a forest of waving tails. With his back against the mantelpiece, smoking placidly, stood Ukridge.

“Hallo, laddie!” he said, with a genial wave of the hand, as if to make me free of the place. “You’re just in time. I’ve got to dash off and catch a train in a quarter of an hour. Stop it, you mutts!” he bellowed, and the six Pekingese, who had been barking steadily since my arrival, stopped in mid-yap, and were still. Ukridge’s personality seemed to exercise a magnetism over the animal kingdom, from ex-butlers to Pekes, which bordered on the uncanny. “I’m off to Sheep’s Cray, in Kent. Taken a cottage there.”

“Are you going to live there?”

“Yes.”

“But what about your aunt?”

“Oh, I’ve left her. Life is stern and life is earnest, and if I mean to make a fortune I’ve got to bustle about and not stay cooped up in a place like Wimbledon.”

“Something in that.”

===Chinese===

"下午好，先生，"鲍尔斯说。"有位先生正等您。方才我好像听见他叫我。"

"他是谁？"

"一位厄克里奇先生，先生。他——"

楼上一个洪亮的声音轰然响起。

"鲍尔斯，老伙计！"

鲍尔斯同伦敦西南区一切备家具出租房的房东一样，是位前管家；他周身同一切前管家一样，裹着一件威严优越的气袍，从未失效地压碎过我的精神。他是个富态的人，秃顶，一双浅绿色暴眼——那双眼睛仿佛冷静地称量我，判我不合格。"哼！"它们似乎在说。"年轻——太年轻。跟我在上等府邸里见惯的完全不搭。"听见这位显贵被唤作——还是吼出来的——"老伙计"，我心中那种大难将至之感，同一个虔诚的年轻副牧师目睹主教被人拍背所感的相差无几。因此，当他回应时不但温和、简直带着几分袍泽之谊时，这打击是麻木级的。

"先生？"鲍尔斯柔声问。

"给我拿六根骨头和一个开瓶器来。"

"好的，先生。"

鲍尔斯退下，我蹦上楼梯，推开客厅的门。

"我的天！"我发懵地说。

满屋是北京哈巴狗的汪洋。事后清点，数目是六只；可在那头一眼里，仿佛有好几百只。无论往哪儿看，都有鼓凸的眼睛与我相迎。满屋是一片摇尾之林。厄克里奇背倚壁炉架，安详地抽着烟。

"喂，小家伙！"他亲切地一挥手，仿佛请我随意。"你来得正好。一刻钟后我就得撒腿赶火车。消停，你们这群浑球！"他吼道，六只哈巴——它们打我进门就没停过口——当即叫到一半收声，寂然不动。厄克里奇的人格对动物王国（从前管家到哈巴）行使着一种近乎妖异的磁力。"我去肯特郡的希普斯克雷。在那儿租了座村屋。"

"你要住那儿？"

"是。"

"那你姑妈呢？"

"哦，我把她甩了。人生严峻，人生认真，我要想发财，就得四处奔忙，不能老闷在温布尔登那种地方。"

"这话有几分道理。"

===Original===

“Besides which, she told me the very sight of me made her sick and she never wanted to see me again.”

I might have guessed, directly I saw him, that some upheaval had taken place. The sumptuous raiment which had made him such a treat to the eye at our last meeting was gone, and he was back in his pre-Wimbledon costume, which was, as the advertisements say, distinctively individual. Over grey flannel trousers, a golf coat, and a brown sweater he wore like a royal robe a bright yellow mackintosh. His collar had broken free from its stud and showed a couple of inches of bare neck. His hair was disordered, and his masterful nose was topped by a pair of steel-rimmed pince-nez cunningly attached to his flapping ears with ginger-beer wire. His whole appearance spelled revolt.

Bowles manifested himself with a plateful of bones.

“That’s right. Chuck ’em down on the floor.”

“Very good, sir.”

“I like that fellow,” said Ukridge, as the door closed. “We had a dashed interesting talk before you came in. Did you know he had a cousin on the music-halls?”

“He hasn’t confided in me much.”

“He’s promised me an introduction to him later on. May be useful to be in touch with a man who knows the ropes. You see, laddie, I’ve hit on the most amazing scheme.” He swept his arm round dramatically, overturning a plaster cast of the Infant Samuel at Prayer. “All right, all right, you can mend it with glue or something, and, anyway, you’re probably better without it. Yessir, I’ve hit on a great scheme. The idea of a thousand years.”

“What’s that?”

“I’m going to train dogs.”

“Train dogs?”

“For the music-hall stage. Dog acts, you know. Performing dogs. Pots of money in it. I start in a modest way with these six. When I’ve taught ’em a few tricks, I sell them to a fellow in the profession for a large sum and buy twelve more. I train those, sell ’em for a large sum, and with the money buy twenty-four more. I train those﻿—”

“Here, wait a minute.” My head was beginning to swim. I had a vision of England paved with Pekingese dogs, all doing tricks. “How do you know you’ll be able to sell them?”

“Of course I shall. The demand’s enormous. Supply can’t cope with it. At a conservative estimate I should think I ought to scoop in four or five thousand pounds the first year. That, of course, is before the business really starts to expand.”

“I see.”

===Chinese===

"再说，她明明白白告诉我，一看见我就恶心，再也不想见我。"

其实我一见他，就该猜到出了大变故。上次见面把他打扮成人间奇观的华服没有了，他重新穿回温布尔登之前的行头——用广告上的话说，独具个性。灰法兰绒裤、高尔夫外套、棕色毛衣之外，他如披王袍般罩着一件明黄的胶布雨衣。领子挣脱了领扣，露出两寸光脖子。头发散乱，那管气宇轩昂的鼻子上架着钢框夹鼻眼镜，用姜汁汽水铁丝巧妙地拴在两只招风耳上。他整副仪表写的是两个字：造反。

鲍尔斯端着一盘骨头现身。

"这就对了。往地板上一倒。"

"好的，先生。"

"我喜欢这家伙，"门关上后厄克里奇说。"你进来之前，咱们进行了一场顶有意思的谈话。你知道他在杂耍场有个表兄弟吗？"

"他没跟我多交过心。"

"他答应日后给我引见。认识个懂行的，说不定有用。你瞧，小家伙，我撞上一个惊天地的大方案了。"他手臂一挥，气势磅礴，扫倒了石膏像"祈祷的小撒母耳"。"没事没事，拿胶水什么的粘粘就好，再说没准你还更好看些。是了，先生，我撞上一个大方案。千年一遇的主意。"

"什么主意？"

"我要训练狗。"

"训练狗？"

"往杂耍场的台上训。狗戏，懂吧。表演狗。大把的钱。我先拿这六只小打小闹。教会它们几手，高价卖给圈里人，再买十二只。训好这批，高价卖掉，拿钱再买二十四只。再训这批——"

"慢着，慢着。"我脑袋开始发晕。我眼前浮现出英格兰全境以北京哈巴铺路、个个都在把戏的图景。"你怎么知道卖得掉？"

"当然卖得掉。需求大得吓人。供给根本跟不上。保守估计，头一年我少说搂它四五千镑。这当然还是买卖真正铺开之前。"

"哦。"

===Original===

“When I get going properly, with a dozen assistants under me and an organised establishment, I shall begin to touch the big money. What I’m aiming at is a sort of Dogs’ College out in the country somewhere. Big place with a lot of ground. Regular classes and a set curriculum. Large staff, each member of it with so many dogs under his care, me looking on and superintending. Why, once the thing starts moving it’ll run itself, and all I shall have to do will be to sit back and endorse the cheques. It isn’t as if I would have to confine my operations to England. The demand for performing dogs is universal throughout the civilised world. America wants performing dogs. Australia wants performing dogs. Africa could do with a few, I’ve no doubt. My aim, laddie, is gradually to get a monopoly of the trade. I want everybody who needs a performing dog of any description to come automatically to me. And I’ll tell you what, laddie. If you like to put up a bit of capital, I’ll let you in on the ground floor.”

“No, thanks.”

“All right. Have it your own way. Only don’t forget that there was a fellow who put nine hundred dollars into the Ford Car business when it was starting and he collected a cool forty million. I say, is that clock right? Great Scott! I’ll be missing my train. Help me mobilise these dashed animals.”

Five minutes later, accompanied by the six Pekingese and bearing about him a pound of my tobacco, three pairs of my socks, and the remains of a bottle of whisky, Ukridge departed in a taxicab for Charing Cross Station to begin his lifework.

Perhaps six weeks passed, six quiet Ukridgeless weeks, and then one morning I received an agitated telegram. Indeed, it was not so much a telegram as a cry of anguish. In every word of it there breathed the tortured spirit of a great man who has battled in vain against overwhelming odds. It was the sort of telegram which Job might have sent off after a lengthy session with Bildad the Shuhite:﻿—

“Come here immediately, laddie. Life and death matter, old horse. Desperate situation. Don’t fail me.”

It stirred me like a bugle, I caught the next train.

===Chinese===

"等我全面开动，手下十几个助手、机构齐备，就该摸大钱了。我瞄准的，是在乡下什么地方办一座狗狗学院。大场子，大片地。正经的班级，成套的课程。庞大的教职员，每人名下分管若干只狗，我在一旁督察。嗐，这东西一转起来就自己跑了，我只消坐着背书支票。再说，买卖也不必困在英伦三岛。文明世界对表演狗的需求是普世的。美国要表演狗。澳洲要表演狗。非洲没准也想要几只。我的目标，小家伙，是逐步垄断这一行。我要让天下需要表演狗的人，无论要哪种，都自动找上我。对了还有，小家伙，你要是肯出点本钱，我让你占先入股。"

"不，谢谢。"

"随你。只是别忘了，当年有个家伙往福特汽车投了九百美元，后来落袋整整四千万。——咦，那钟准吗？我的天！我要误车了。帮我动员动员这群要命的畜生。"

五分钟后，厄克里奇带着六只北京哈巴，随身携带我的一磅烟草、三双袜子和一瓶威士忌的残余，乘出租车奔赴查令十字车站，去开始他的毕生事业了。

约莫过了六个星期——六个没有厄克里奇的太平星期——一天早晨我收到一封惶急的电报。说电报，不如说是一声惨呼。每个字里都喘息着一位伟人苦斗强敌而不得的受难魂灵。那是约伯同书亚人比勒达长谈一场之后也许会拍出的电报：——

"速来，小家伙。人命关天，老伙计。绝境。勿弃我。"

它像军号一样把我吹起，我赶上了下一班车。

===Original===
